| Varying quadratic companions | The same data and a bound $A$ for the number of prime factors of the companion discriminant; its prime sizes do not enter.                                                                                             |
| Fixed-companion transfer     | The companion field is fixed before the cube dimension. Constants may depend on its fixed base Selmer data, as specified in Section <a href="#rd:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:section">5</a>. |
| Finite realization           | Choose the cube dimension and finite networks, then the required coefficient precision and a sufficiently late arithmetic stage. Prime sizes and stage thresholds may depend on these choices.                         |

</div>

<a id="cy:section"></a>
### Cyclotomic interpolation for extensions of trivial residual modules

Throughout this section, $E/\mathbb Q$ is fixed, its conductor is $N$, and $E[2]$ is an extension of two trivial $\mathbb F _2$-modules. Write $T=T_2E$ and $V=T\otimes_{\mathbb Z _2}\mathbb Q _2$. The word “ordinary” applied to global cohomology below means that the real-place modification has not been made. It imposes no ordinary-reduction hypothesis at $2$.

The discriminants considered here are products of distinct signed primes $q^*=(-1/q)q$, with $q\nmid 2N$. Fix their sign and local squareclasses at $2N$. For such a discriminant $h$, put $$t_q=\dim_{\mathbb F _2}E[2]^{\operatorname{Fr}_q},\qquad
 2w(h)=\sum_{q\mid h}t_q,\qquad
 \mathcal L(h)=\frac{\sqrt{|h|}\,L(E^{(h)},1)}{\Omega_E^{\operatorname{sgn}(h)}}.$$ Here fixed nonzero periods in the two period lines have been chosen once. Changing these choices changes the bounds below by a constant depending only on $E$. Valuations of algebraic central values mean their $2$-adic valuations, with $v_2(2)=1$. When $s_2(E^{(h)})=0$, the group $\operatorname{Sha}(E^{(h)}/\mathbb Q)[2^\infty]$ is finite; set $$s(h)=\operatorname{length}_{\mathbb Z _2}
       \operatorname{Sha}(E^{(h)}/\mathbb Q)[2^\infty],\qquad
 D(h)=v_2(\mathcal L(h))-2w(h)-s(h)$$ when the central value is nonzero. By Lemma <a href="#pr:ranks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pr:ranks">2</a>, $s(h)$ is defined at a corank-zero base even before its central value is known to be nonzero. Only the definition of $D(h)$ requires that nonvanishing.

We prove the two assertions needed later.

<a id="stmt-cy-lower-bound"></a>
<div id="cy:lower-bound" class="theorem">

**Theorem 4** (Cyclotomic lower bound). *There is a constant $C_E$ such that, for every allowed $h$ with $L(E^{(h)},1)\ne0$, $$v_2(\mathcal L(h))\ \geq\ 2w(h)+s(h)-C_E.$$ The constant is independent of the number and sizes of the primes dividing $h$.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-cy-missing-vertex"></a>
<div id="cy:missing-vertex" class="theorem">

**Theorem 5** (Transfer to the missing vertex). *Let $h_0$ be an allowed discriminant with $s_2(E^{(h_0)})=0$. Suppose that for arbitrarily large $b$ there are a finite set $Q$ of distinct primes $q\nmid 2Nh_0$ and linear forms $\lambda_q:\mathbb F _2^b\longrightarrow\mathbb F _2$ giving $$h_x=h_0\prod_{q\in Q}(q^*)^{\lambda_q(x)},\qquad x\in\mathbb F _2^b,$$ all in the fixed local and sign classes. Suppose that a number $B$, independent of the family, satisfies $$L(E^{(h_x)},1)\ne0,\qquad D(h_x)\leq B\qquad(x\ne0).$$ Then $L(E^{(h_0)},1)\ne0$ and $D(h_0)\leq B+C_E$. Here $C_E$ is independent of $h_0$, $Q$, and $b$; the dimension needed in the proof is allowed to depend on the fixed precision being tested.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### The integral scalar family and two algebraic steps

Put $$\Lambda=\mathbb Z _2[[t]],\qquad
 \mathcal O=\widehat{\Lambda_{(2)}},\qquad
 \kappa=\mathcal O/2\mathcal O=\mathbb F _2((t)).$$ The completion defining $\mathcal O$ is $2$-adic. An element of $\mathcal O$ has, modulo any $2^M$, a Laurent expansion with a finite negative part. There is no evaluation homomorphism $\mathcal O\to\mathbb Q _2$ given by $t=0$. All central evaluations below are made on elements of $\Lambda[1/2]$ before extending coefficients to $\mathcal O$.

For $G=(\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z)^b$, index its characters by $x\in\mathbb F _2^b$, and denote character evaluation by a subscript $x$.

The arithmetic construction below will produce a scalar family $u\in\Lambda[1/2][G]$ with one uniform denominator: $$2^{C_{\mathrm{int}}}u\in\Lambda[G].$$ At a constituent with $s_2(E^{(h_x)})=0$, its central value $u_x(0)$ will vanish exactly when $L(E^{(h_x)},1)$ does; in the nonvanishing case, $$\bigl|v_2(u_x(0))-D(h_x)\bigr|\leq C_{\mathrm{loc}}.$$ Both constants will be independent of the number and sizes of all varying primes, including those of the base $h_0$. Integrality gives the lower bound, while a congruence between the constant coefficients at $0$ and at some nonzero address gives the missing-vertex theorem.

Two different arguments establish the uniform denominator. Rational divisibility will show $u_x\in\Lambda[1/2]$ character by character. A fixed integral Kato construction will instead give $2^{C_{\mathrm{int}}}u\in\mathcal O[G]$ before taking characters. The first lemma combines these assertions without a loss depending on $|G|$; the second supplies the congruence used at the end.

<a id="stmt-cy-fourier"></a>
<div id="cy:fourier" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Integral Fourier intersection). *Suppose $u\in\mathcal O[1/2][G]$, all $u_x$ belong to $\Lambda[1/2]$, and $2^C u\in\mathcal O[G]$. Then $2^C u\in\Lambda[G]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cy-fourier"><summary>Proof of stmt-cy-fourier</summary>

*Proof.* Fourier inversion in characteristic zero expresses every group-basis coefficient of $u$ as a sum of the $u_x$ divided by $|G|$. Thus it belongs to $\Lambda[1/2]$. The corresponding coefficient of $2^C u$ also belongs to $\mathcal O$. For a power series with bounded $2$-denominator, nonnegative $\mathcal O$-valuation means that every coefficient is integral. Consequently $\mathcal O\cap\Lambda[1/2]=\Lambda$, which proves the assertion with the original exponent $C$. ◻

</details>

The common-zero parity step below is the characteristic-two case of the Chevalley–Warning principle; see (Heath-Brown 2011, sec. 1, equation (1)) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[HeathBrownChevalleyWarning](#ref-HeathBrownChevalleyWarning)]. We retain the elementary proof of this step.

<a id="stmt-cy-binary"></a>
<div id="cy:binary" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Binary congruences). *Let $z_1,\ldots,z_r\in\mathbb Z _2[G]$. Given $M\geq1$, if $b>r(2^M-1)$, there is an $x\ne0$ such that $$(z_j)_x\equiv(z_j)_0\pmod {2^M}\qquad(1\leq j\leq r).$$ The bound depends on the number of tests and their precision, and not on the group-ring coefficients.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cy-binary"><summary>Proof of stmt-cy-binary</summary>

*Proof.* Choose generators of $G$. Every character-value function $z(x)$ has an expansion $$z(x)=a_\varnothing+
       \sum_{\varnothing\ne I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,b\}}
           2^{|I|}a_I\prod_{i\in I}x_i,
       \qquad a_I\in\mathbb Z _2.$$ This follows by expanding each sign character as a product of factors $1-2x_i$. The $j$th binary digit of a $2$-adic integer $a$ is $\binom a{2^j}\bmod2$, equivalently the coefficient of $Y^{2^j}$ in $(1+Y)^a$ over $\mathbb F _2$. On the Boolean cube, the contribution of the monomial $x_I=\prod_{i\in I}x_i$ to this generating function is $$1+x_I\bigl((1+Y)^{2^{|I|}a_I}-1\bigr).$$ Its nonconstant terms have $Y$-degree at least $2^{|I|}$. In a contribution to $Y^{2^j}$, the sum of the quantities $2^{|I|}$ used is at most $2^j$. Since $|I|\leq2^{|I|}$, the resulting Boolean polynomial has degree at most $2^j$. These formulas for $2$-adic exponents follow either by continuity from nonnegative integers or in each finite coefficient quotient.

Express all $rM$ digit equalities as Boolean equations $P_{ij}(x)=0$. Their total degree is at most $r(2^M-1)$. The indicator of their common zero set is $\prod_{i,j}(1+P_{ij})$. The remaining count is the Chevalley–Warning degree argument (Chevalley 1935; Warning 1935) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Chevalley1935](#ref-Chevalley1935), [Warning1935](#ref-Warning1935)]. A Boolean polynomial of degree less than $b$ has even sum over $\mathbb F _2^b$: every monomial omits a variable, and summing over that variable gives zero in $\mathbb F _2$. Hence the number of common zeros is even. Since $0$ is a common zero, another one exists. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### The positive global complex

The scalar $u$ will be the coordinate of two cohomology classes in an integral determinant line. One class comes from the real place and the other from Kato’s construction. We first construct the complex and show that its localization at $(2)$ is free of rank two in degree one.

Let $\Gamma=\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb Q_\infty/\mathbb Q)$ be the real cyclotomic $\mathbb Z _2$-group and identify its generator with $1+t$. For a binary family, let $\psi:G_{\mathbb Q}\to G$ be its universal quadratic character: evaluation at $x$ is $\chi_{h_x/h_0}$. Put $$R=\Lambda[G],\qquad
 \mathbb T=T\otimes_{\mathbb Z _2}R,$$ with the fixed twist $\chi_{h_0}$, the universal twist $\psi$, and the inverse cyclotomic action. One may replace $G$ by the actual image of $\psi$ and subsequently extend scalars. In a family in Theorem <a href="#cy:missing-vertex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:missing-vertex">5</a>, a nonzero character trivial on that image already gives the conclusion directly.

<a id="cy:positive"></a>
Let $S$ contain $2N$, the prime factors of $h_0$, and all the new primes. Use continuous cochains with their completed coefficient topology, and set $$\label{cy:positive}
 C_S^+(\mathbb T)=
 \operatorname{Cone}\bigl(R\Gamma(G_{\mathbb Q,S},\mathbb T)
              \longrightarrow R\Gamma(\mathbb R,\mathbb T)\bigr)[-1].$$ The real complex here uses ordinary cochains in nonnegative degrees. There are no imposed local conditions at the finite places.

<a id="stmt-cy-perfect"></a>
<div id="cy:perfect" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Perfection and the real-place class). *The complex <a href="#cy:positive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:positive">[cy:positive]</a> is perfect over $R$ and computes derived coefficient specializations. There is a class $a\in H^1(C_S^+(\mathbb T))$ whose rational span is the real-place summand. Its integral index in that summand depends only on $E$ and the fixed real sign.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cy-perfect"><summary>Proof of stmt-cy-perfect</summary>

*Proof.* Here are the finiteness and coefficient issues in the use of global cochains. For a finite coefficient quotient of $R$, continuous cochains are filtered unions of finite products of free coefficient modules. Refinement of finite partitions makes these modules flat; over an Artin local coefficient ring they are free. For finite residual coefficients, global cohomology is finite in each degree, and restriction to the real places is an isomorphism in degrees greater than two. The latter is the real-place clause of global duality. Thus the residual fiber has bounded amplitude and finite-dimensional cohomology. The same amplitude bound holds for every finite coefficient module, by a composition series and the cohomology long exact sequence.

This gives a bounded finite free model as follows. Over the residue field split off the acyclic disks, leaving its finitely many cohomology spaces. Lift each invertible disk differential over an Artin coefficient quotient and cancel it. Invertibility of a lifted inverse follows from nilpotence of the maximal ideal. Equivalently one can perform this on a free resolution; a minimal free model cannot have an additional term whose reduction has zero cohomology, because its residual differential is zero. Hence the remaining ranks are the finite residual ranks and are bounded independently of the coefficient quotient. Minimal models identified by a homotopy equivalence over an Artin local ring are isomorphic. Lifting their changes of basis therefore makes these models compatible along $R/\mathfrak m^n$. Taking the inverse limit gives a bounded finite free model over $R$. The cochain construction with finite free coefficients and the same cancellation also proves derived base change. This is a fixed-$S$ argument; it does not assert uniform bounds on the number of generators as $S$ varies.

All the characters of the family have the same real sign, so the action at the real place is the fixed action on $T\chi_{h_0}$ extended to $R$. Choose a nonzero vector in its integral invariant line. Its connecting image in the fiber is $a$. There are no global invariant vectors in the rational elliptic representation, and consequently this connecting map is injective rationally. The choice and its index are fixed. Notice also that $H^1(\mathbb R,\mathbb T)$ is killed by $2$; thus twice any ordinary cohomology class lifts to the positive complex. These observations use compact global duality, including the real-place extension (Nekovář 2006, vol. 310, sec. 5.7 and 6.9.1) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Nekovar2006](#ref-Nekovar2006)] of the complex formulation in (Nekovář 2006, Theorem 6.3.4) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Nekovar2006](#ref-Nekovar2006)]. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cy-residual"></a>
<div id="cy:residual" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Residual concentration). *There is an isomorphism in the derived category $$C_S^+(\mathbb T)\otimes_R\mathcal O[G]
       \simeq \mathcal O[G]^2[-1].$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cy-residual"><summary>Proof of stmt-cy-residual</summary>

*Proof.* The ring $\mathcal O[G]$ is local, with residue field $\kappa$ obtained by setting $2=0$ and every $g=1$. Thus the quadratic characters disappear. Since $E[2]$ has a filtration with trivial factors, it is enough first to compute the positive residual complex for the trivial representation.

Let $K_n$ be the real cyclotomic layer of degree $d_n=2^n$. The number of finite primes of $K_n$ above a fixed $S$ is bounded with $n$: at $2$ there is total ramification, and the Frobenius of each fixed odd prime has nonzero image in $\Gamma$. The $2$-ranks of the class groups of $K_n$ are also bounded. Indeed the cyclotomic $\mu$-invariant is zero for this abelian tower (Ferrero and Washington 1979) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[FerreroWashington1979](#ref-FerreroWashington1979)]; the structure theorem and class-group control identify their $2$-ranks, up to bounded kernels and cokernels, with those of the coinvariants of a finitely generated $\mathbb Z _2$-module. The same bound holds for $S$-class groups.

The signature map of units in $K_n$ is surjective. For $n\geq1$, let $\zeta$ be a primitive $2^{n+2}$th root and put $\epsilon=1+\zeta+\zeta^{-1}$. If $f_1(X)=X^2-2$ and $f_{n+1}(X)=f_n(X^2-2)$, then $f_n$ is the minimal polynomial of $\zeta+\zeta^{-1}$ and $f_n(-1)=-1$. Hence $N_{K_n/\mathbb Q}(\epsilon)=-1$. Its signature vector has odd augmentation. The embeddings form a regular set for the cyclic $2$-group $\operatorname{Gal}(K_n/\mathbb Q)$, whose group algebra over $\mathbb F _2$ is local. A vector with odd augmentation is a unit in this group algebra, so its translates span every signature. For $n=0$ the assertion follows from the unit $-1$.

We now use Kummer theory to isolate the one dimension that survives after inverting $t$. Writing $s_n$ for the number of finite support places and $c_n$ for the $2$-rank of the $S$-class group, one has $$\dim H^1(G_{K_n,S},\mu_2)=d_n+s_n+c_n.$$ The real degree-one restriction is surjective by the signature result. In degree two, the Kummer sequence has class-group term of dimension $c_n$ and Brauer term. The Brauer invariants at the real and finite support places have their single sum relation. Since a finite support place above $2$ is present, restriction of the Brauer term to the real places is surjective and has kernel of dimension $s_n-1$. Thus the degree-two kernel of restriction to infinity is bounded. Restriction is an isomorphism in higher degrees by global duality. The degree-zero restriction is injective. The cohomology of the positive fiber is therefore bounded in every degree except one; its degree-one dimension is $d_n+O_S(1)$.

Apply Lemma <a href="#cy:perfect" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:perfect">8</a> modulo $2$. Derived specialization to the $n$th layer is specialization modulo $t^{2^n}$. For a perfect complex over $\mathbb F _2[[t]]$, the universal-coefficient exact sequence shows that a rank-$r$ cohomology module contributes $r2^n+O(1)$ to these dimensions; the torsion terms contribute only $O(1)$. It follows that after inverting $t$ the trivial-constituent fiber has dimension one in degree one and vanishes in every other degree. Extensions preserve this concentration, so the fiber for $E[2]$ has dimension two in degree one. A minimal finite free complex over the local ring $\mathcal O[G]$ has zero residual differentials. The concentration just proved forces all its terms except the rank-two term in degree one to vanish. No denominator is introduced by this exact freeness statement. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Fixed-form zeta classes and the integral coefficient map

We need a global class whose denominator stays bounded as the tame support grows. For this purpose every auxiliary trace will land in the lattice of one fixed modular form. Let $f$ be the fixed weight-two form of $E$, and choose its fixed integral cohomological lattice, with the Tate twist making its rational representation $V$. Kato’s classes (8.1.2)–(8.1.3), for a fixed symbol $\xi\in\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb Z)$, exist for arbitrary $m$ and any $S$ containing the prime factors of $mN$. The trace in Section 8.9 is a homomorphism into the lattice $$\mathbb Z _2[\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb Q(\mu_m)/\mathbb Q)]
       \otimes V_{2,\mathbb Z _2}(Y_1(N))(1).$$ Section 8.11 then projects to the lattice of the fixed form. Proposition 8.12 supplies its norm relations. These assertions hold at the coefficient prime $2$ (Kato 2004, secs. 5.1, 8.1, 8.9–8.12) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Kato2004](#ref-Kato2004)]. In particular, the auxiliary modular-curve level used to define a trace does not become the level of the projected newform.

Choose an isomorphism of the resulting fixed rational representation with $V$. A single power $2^{c_E}$ sends its integral lattice into $T$. Choose fixed symbols with nonzero projections on the required period lines; their existence and the construction with both signs are made explicit in (Kato 2004, sec. 13.6 and 13.9) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Kato2004](#ref-Kato2004)]. Taking a fixed linear combination, if needed, costs another fixed denominator.

<a id="cy:coefficient-map"></a>
Here is the coefficient map in this application. Let $m$ be odd and divisible by the conductors of all the tame characters in the family, including the odd part of $\chi_{h_0}$. At a sufficiently large cyclotomic level write $A_{m,n}=\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb Q(\mu_{m2^n})/
\mathbb Q)$. Shapiro identifies the cohomology of the cyclotomic field with global cohomology with induced group-ring coefficients. Push the class along the ring homomorphism $$\label{cy:coefficient-map}
 \mathbb Z _2[A_{m,n}]\longrightarrow
       \mathbb Z _2[\Gamma_n\times G],\qquad
 [\sigma]\longmapsto
       \chi_{h_0}(\sigma)[\sigma|_{\Gamma_n}][\psi(\sigma)].$$ Use inverse actions on both sides if this is required by the chosen Shapiro convention. This is a homomorphism of integral coefficient modules and acts on actual cohomology classes. It requires neither division by $|G|$ nor surjectivity of a cohomological specialization map. The prime $2$ belongs to the fixed omitted support, so Proposition 8.12 gives exact norm compatibility as $n$ increases. The pushed classes therefore give an integral smoothed Iwasawa class with coefficients in $\mathbb T$, up to the single fixed power $2^{c_E}$.

We next remove smoothing. In weight two and with $r=r'=1$, its factors are <a id="cy:smoothing"></a>$$\label{cy:smoothing}
 (c^2-c\sigma_c)(d^2-d\sigma_d).$$ This is the specialization of (Kato 2004, Theorem 6.6 and (4.2.4)) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Kato2004](#ref-Kato2004)]. Choose a fixed $e\geq\max(2,v_2(N))$, increasing it to accommodate the finitely many fixed symbol levels. The Chinese remainder theorem permits $c,d>1$, prime to $6Nm$, such that $$c,d\equiv1\pmod{3N_{\mathrm{odd}}m},\qquad
 c,d\equiv1+2^e\pmod{2^{e+1}}.$$ Their tame actions in <a href="#cy:coefficient-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:coefficient-map">[cy:coefficient-map]</a> are trivial. If $\sigma_c$ acts in $\Gamma$ as $(1+t)^{\kappa(c)}$, then $v_2(\kappa(c))=e-2$. Modulo the maximal ideal of $\mathcal O[G]$ its smoothing factor is $$1-(1+t)^{\kappa(c)}\ne0\quad\hbox{in }\mathbb F _2((t)).$$ It is therefore a unit in $\mathcal O[G]$. Its central value is $c(c-1)$, of valuation exactly $e$. The same statements hold for $d$ and for inverse-translate conventions. Thus unsmoothing introduces neither a $2$-adic denominator at $(2)$ nor a growing central denominator. The integers $c,d$ vary with $m$; the congruences fix the needed valuations, not their exact cyclotomic group elements.

<a id="cy:reciprocity"></a>
Denote the resulting rational ordinary class by $z_S^{(0)}$. Theorem 9.7 of (Kato 2004) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Kato2004](#ref-Kato2004)] applies to $k=2,r=r'=1$. In the character constituent $x$, its central reciprocity formula reads $$\label{cy:reciprocity}
 v_2\bigl(\exp_2^*z_{S,x}^{(0)}(0)\bigr)
 =v_2(\mathcal L(h_x))+
  \sum_{\substack{q\in Q\,,\ q\nmid h_x}}
       v_2\bigl(\#E^{(h_x)}(\mathbb F _q)\bigr)+O_E(1)$$ when the value is nonzero, and a zero central value gives a zero dual exponential. Fixed omitted primes are included in the error. To check the normalization, the character sum in <a href="#cy:coefficient-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:coefficient-map">[cy:coefficient-map]</a> is the unnormalized sum, as in Kato’s trace. Its tame quadratic Gauss sum supplies $\sqrt{|h_x|}$ up to a root of unity. Tame conductors and their Gauss sums are $2$-adic units. The period vector is the fixed symbol vector of $f$. More explicitly, in the required sign line write the period represented by $\delta(f,1,\xi)^{\pm}$ as $c_\xi^{\pm}\Omega_E^{\pm}$. The nonzero scalar $c_\xi^{\pm}$ is fixed: the character-sum formula of (Kato 2004, Theorem 6.6) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Kato2004](#ref-Kato2004)] uses this same $\delta(f,1,\xi)^{\pm}$ for every tame character. The tame character enters through its $L$-value and Gauss sum, not through a newly chosen period vector. The comparison with the minimal differential of $E^{(h_x)}$ at $2$ has bounded valuation, because there are only finitely many local twisting classes there. At a good unused prime the omitted factor is $$1-\frac{a_q(E)\chi_{h_x}(\operatorname{Fr}_q)}q+\frac1q
       =\frac{\#E^{(h_x)}(\mathbb F _q)}q;$$ at a good ramified prime the local $L$-factor is $1$. These account for every varying factor in <a href="#cy:reciprocity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:reciprocity">[cy:reciprocity]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Rational divisibility and the local Euler corrections

The fixed-form construction controls the denominator at $(2)$. We now prove the complementary assertion: the determinant coordinate has no pole at any other height-one prime. This step is characterwise and uses only rational divisibility. Write $A=\Lambda[1/2]$. Theorem 12.5(3) of (Kato 2004) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Kato2004](#ref-Kato2004)] is a height-one divisibility over $A$; its possible local $H^2$ correction comes from Galois cohomology over $\mathbb Q_2$. The global groups there use $j_*$ outside that prime. The stronger integral assertion 12.5(4) is not used. In the non-CM case, the large-image input in Kato’s proof follows from the open image theorem (Serre 1972) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Serre1972](#ref-Serre1972)]; see (Kato 2004, Remark 12.8, (12.8.2), Theorem 13.4(v), and Section 13.13) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Kato2004](#ref-Kato2004)]. This is not an additional residual-image hypothesis in Theorem 12.5(3). For CM the required rational statement at every prime, including $2$, is supplied by (A. A. Burungale and Tian 2026, Theorem 2.6 and Remark 2.7) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[BurungaleTian2026](#ref-BurungaleTian2026)]. Neither statement asserts uniform integrality in a quadratic group ring.

<a id="cy:local-block"></a>
<a id="cy:local-sequence"></a>
Fix a character constituent $h$ and write $V_h=V\chi_h$. Let $C_{j_*}$ be the rational Iwasawa complex in Kato’s formulation and $C_S$ the rational full global complex. At an odd prime put $$W_q=H^1(I_q,V_h).$$ The localization triangle has local cone $$\label{cy:local-block}
 Q_q=R\Gamma\bigl(G_{\mathbb F _q},
                   W_q\otimes A(\Gamma^\iota)\bigr)[-1]
   =\left[W_q\otimes A
       \xrightarrow{\ 1-\gamma_q\operatorname{Fr}_{W_q}\ }
             W_q\otimes A\right]$$ in degrees one and two. Here $\gamma_q$ denotes the scalar cyclotomic Frobenius in the chosen convention. Its exponent is nonzero. Consequently $D_q=\det(1-\gamma_q\operatorname{Fr}_{W_q})$ is a nonzero element of $A$, and the map in <a href="#cy:local-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:local-block">[cy:local-block]</a> is injective. This remains true after localization at a height-one prime dividing $D_q$: localization does not mean reduction modulo that prime. Thus $$H^1(Q_q)=0,\qquad
 \operatorname{length}_{A_{\mathfrak p}}H^2(Q_q)_{\mathfrak p}
       =v_{\mathfrak p}(D_q).$$ The long exact sequence gives $$\label{cy:local-sequence}
 \begin{gathered}
 H^1(C_{j_*})\simeq H^1(C_S),\\
 0\longrightarrow H^2(C_{j_*})\longrightarrow H^2(C_S)
       \longrightarrow\bigoplus_{q\in S\setminus\{2\}}H^2(Q_q)
       \longrightarrow H^3(C_{j_*}).
 \end{gathered}$$ In particular, the length of $H^2(C_S)$ is at most the length of $H^2(C_{j_*})$ plus the sum of the local lengths. No assertion that $H^3(C_{j_*})$ vanishes is required here.

<a id="cy:good-euler"></a>
At a good unused prime, $W_q=V_h(-1)$, so, on including the quadratic Frobenius sign in $Z$, $$\label{cy:good-euler}
 D_q=P_q(Z),\qquad P_q(Z)=1-\frac{a_q(E)}q Z+\frac1q Z^2.$$ At a good active prime, inertia acts by $-1$ on $V_h$. Its rational inertia cohomology is zero, so $W_q=0$ and $D_q=1$.

At fixed bad primes the general expression is $H^1(I_q,V_h)$ in <a href="#cy:local-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:local-block">[cy:local-block]</a>. Replacing it indiscriminately by $V_h^{I_q}(-1)$ would be incorrect. For example, at split multiplicative reduction, $$V_h^{I_q}=\mathbb Q _2(1),\qquad
 H^1(I_q,V_h)=\mathbb Q _2(-1),$$ and the correct determinant is $1-\gamma_q/q$, with nonzero central value. The incorrect replacement would give $1-\gamma_q$. In general $D_q(0)\ne0$ follows from local duality: $H^2(\mathbb Q _q,V_h)=H^0(\mathbb Q _q,V_h)^*=0$, since elliptic torsion over a local field is finite. The fixed bad-prime corrections therefore have nonzero central values for every reduction type.

<a id="cy:rational-comparison"></a>
Let $z_{\mathrm{BK},h}$ denote a generator of the rational Kato zeta line in the real cyclotomic component with the fixed sign. Compare it with the fixed-form class only after taking the character constituent. In the chosen Euler convention the comparison is $$\label{cy:rational-comparison}
 z_{S,h}^{(0)}
  =c_h\epsilon_h(t)E_h^{\mathrm{fix}}(t)
    \prod_{\substack{q\in Q\,,\ q\nmid h}}
       P_q\bigl(\chi_h(\operatorname{Fr}_q)\gamma_q\bigr)
       z_{\mathrm{BK},h},$$ where $c_h\in\mathbb Q_2^\times$ and $\epsilon_h$ is a group-like unit of $A$. The factor $E_h^{\mathrm{fix}}$ consists of the omitted local $L$-polynomials away from $2$ at primes of $N$ and the fixed auxiliary support. It ranges over a finite collection, because the local twisting classes there are fixed. The primes of $h_0$ are good ramified primes and contribute $1$, regardless of their number. These factors have nonzero central values: they are good-prime point counts divided by the prime, multiplicative factors $1\mp1/q$, or $1$. They are not being identified with the local cohomology determinants $D_q$ at bad primes; any discrepancy there will be cleared by a fixed multiplier below.

Here is the derivation of <a href="#cy:rational-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:rational-comparison">[cy:rational-comparison]</a>. Both global Iwasawa $H^1$ modules have rank one. The reciprocity formulas (Kato 2004, Theorems 6.6, 9.7, 12.5(1), and Lemma 13.10(2)) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Kato2004](#ref-Kato2004)] compare the same fixed period vector and the relevant imprimitive $L$-values. Their quotient supplies the displayed omitted Euler factors; a good ramified prime contributes $1$. Coprime Gauss-sum factorization and the fixed period comparison supply $c_h$ and $\epsilon_h$; restricting conductor exponents to either fixed parity removes any factor $\chi_h(2)^n$. Infinitely many of these tests have nonzero central value by cyclotomic nonvanishing, as used in (Kato 2004, Theorem 13.5(2)) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Kato2004](#ref-Kato2004)]. After clearing the finitely many denominators of the scalar ratio, the difference is a power series vanishing at infinitely many finite-order characters. Weierstrass preparation forces that difference to be zero. This proves the generic comparison without identifying the integral lattices of varying twisted newforms. The scalar $c_h$ need not have a uniformly bounded $2$-valuation: every such scalar is a unit of $A$ and does not affect height-one divisibility over $A$. The uniform integral and central bounds instead concern the actual fixed-form class and the fixed period vector in <a href="#cy:reciprocity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:reciprocity">[cy:reciprocity]</a>.

Equations <a href="#cy:local-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:local-sequence">[cy:local-sequence]</a>–<a href="#cy:good-euler" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:good-euler">[cy:good-euler]</a> now show exactly how the imprimitive factors enter divisibility. At any height-one $\mathfrak p$ of $A$, enlarging the global complex increases its degree-two length by at most $\sum_qv_{\mathfrak p}(D_q)$, whereas multiplying the rank-one zeta generator by $\prod_qD_q$ adds exactly that quantity to its index. To cover Kato’s local correction at $2$, multiply the class also by a fixed element $F_2\in\Lambda$ divisible over $A$ by the characteristic ideal of the relevant local $H^2_{\mathrm{Iw}}$. It may be chosen with $F_2(0)\ne0$. Indeed derived local control gives $$H^2_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb Q _2,T\chi_h)_\Gamma\otimes\mathbb Q _2
       =H^2(\mathbb Q _2,V_h)=0,$$ so the characteristic ideal has no factor $t$. Local twisting classes at $2$ are fixed, and one element covers their finite set.

<a id="cy:divisibility"></a>
Likewise any additional fixed Euler factors in the rational comparison can be cleared by a fixed product with nonzero central value, using the actual blocks <a href="#cy:local-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:local-block">[cy:local-block]</a>. Denote the total fixed multiplier by $F_E$. Multiplying by it only changes the fixed constants in <a href="#cy:reciprocity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:reciprocity">[cy:reciprocity]</a> and the integrality bound. Set $z_S=F_Ez_S^{(0)}$. For this corrected class we have at every height-one $\mathfrak p$ of $A$ $$\label{cy:divisibility}
 \operatorname{length}_{A_{\mathfrak p}}H^2(C_S)_{\mathfrak p}
 \leq
 \operatorname{length}_{A_{\mathfrak p}}
       \bigl(H^1(C_S)/Az_{S,h}\bigr)_{\mathfrak p}.$$

We finish by checking that the chosen Euler convention can be imposed integrally before character evaluation. If the original coefficient map uses inverse Frobenius, the varying unused factors change as follows. In $\mathcal O[G]$, both $P_q(Z)$ and $Z^2P_q(Z^{-1})$ are units: their reductions are nonzero polynomials evaluated at the nonconstant cyclotomic Frobenius. The identity $$P_q(Z)-Z^2P_q(Z^{-1})=\frac{q-1}{q}(1-Z^2)$$ shows that their ratio $r_q$ is $1$ modulo $2$. If $g_q$ is the universal inertia element, then <a id="cy:euler-convention"></a>$$\label{cy:euler-convention}
 U_q=1+\frac{r_q-1}{2}(1+g_q)\in\mathcal O[G]^\times$$ is integral. At an active character it is $1$; at an unused character it is $r_q$. Its unused central value is $1$, since $Z=\pm1$ there. Thus inverse Frobenius conventions can be reconciled before character evaluation, without a denominator for each prime. Products of these units have the same property. The resulting rational character coordinates have the Euler convention in <a href="#cy:divisibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:divisibility">[cy:divisibility]</a>. We use this convention for $z_S$ from now on; these units alter neither its uniform integrality bound nor its central reciprocity values.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The determinant and its central specialization

<a id="cy:uniform-integrality"></a>
Choose a basis $\mathbf e$ of the invertible $R$-module $\det_R^{-1}C_S^+(\mathbb T)$; $R$ is local, so such a basis exists. Lift $2z_S$ to positive cohomology and take its wedge with $a$. The factor $2$ is fixed, and two lifts differ by a real-place class, which does not change this wedge. In generic cohomology, the inverse determinant identifies with the exterior square of the rank-two degree-one space. Define its scalar coordinate $u$ by $$a\wedge\widetilde{2z_S}=u\mathbf e.$$ Here $z_S$ already includes the fixed multiplier $F_E$ above. The real-place summand introduces no height-one torsion over $A$. Thus <a href="#cy:divisibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:divisibility">[cy:divisibility]</a> says that $u_x$ has no pole at any height-one prime of $\Lambda$ except possibly $(2)$. Since $\Lambda$ is normal, <a id="cy:rational-membership"></a>$$\label{cy:rational-membership}
 u_x\in\Lambda[1/2]\qquad\hbox{for every }x.$$ On the other hand, the fixed integral trace, <a href="#cy:coefficient-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:coefficient-map">[cy:coefficient-map]</a>, unsmoothing over $\mathcal O[G]$, and Lemma <a href="#cy:residual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:residual">9</a> give $$2^{C_{\mathrm{int}}}u\in\mathcal O[G]$$ with $C_{\mathrm{int}}$ independent of $S$, $h_0$, $G$, and $b$. The determinant identification in a free complex introduces no further index. Applying Lemma <a href="#cy:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:fourier">6</a> yields the crucial uniform statement $$\label{cy:uniform-integrality}
                  2^{C_{\mathrm{int}}}u\in\Lambda[G].$$

<a id="stmt-cy-central"></a>
<div id="cy:central" class="proposition">

<a id="cy:central-formula"></a>
**Proposition 10** (Central determinant formula). *There is a constant $C_{\mathrm{loc}}$, depending only on the fixed curve and local classes, with the following property. If $s_2(E^{(h_x)})=0$, then $u_x(0)=0$ when $L(E^{(h_x)},1)=0$. Otherwise $$\label{cy:central-formula}
 \bigl|v_2(u_x(0))-D(h_x)\bigr|\leq C_{\mathrm{loc}}.$$ The rational positive complex at $t=0$ has cohomology only in degree one, of dimension two, so these are specializations of the same determinant.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cy-central"><summary>Proof of stmt-cy-central</summary>

*Proof.* Abbreviate $T_h=T_2E^{(h_x)}$ and $A_h=E^{(h_x)}[2^\infty]$. Use the integral Kummer subgroup $$K_v=\operatorname{im}\bigl(E^{(h_x)}(\mathbb Q _v)^{\wedge}_2
                              \longrightarrow H^1(\mathbb Q _v,T_h)\bigr)$$ at every finite place. For $v\ne2$ it is all of $H^1(\mathbb Q _v,T_h)$. One way to see this integrally is the inverse-limit Kummer exact sequence: its quotient is $T_2H^1(\mathbb Q _v,E^{(h_x)})$, which is zero because local Tate duality identifies the $2$-primary local Weil–Châtelet group with the dual of the finite $2$-completion of the local point group. At $2$ the quotient $$L_2=H^1(\mathbb Q _2,T_h)/K_2$$ is a free rank-one $\mathbb Z _2$-module. The same Kummer sequence and local Tate duality identify it with the $\mathbb Z _2$-dual of the free part of $E^{(h_x)}(\mathbb Q _2)^{\wedge}_2$.

The dual exponential identifies $L_2\otimes\mathbb Q _2$ with the one-dimensional de Rham target. Integrally its index depends only on the local curve and the chosen differential. This follows from the compatibility of the Kummer map and local Tate duality with the formal elliptic logarithm: on a sufficiently small open formal subgroup that logarithm is an isomorphism, and its adjoint is the dual exponential. Every elliptic curve over $\mathbb Q _2$ has such a formal subgroup, irrespective of reduction type. Since only finitely many local twists occur, the indices are uniformly bounded.

The assumption on the Selmer corank implies that the compact finite Selmer group is torsion and that the discrete finite Selmer group has length $s(h_x)$. Rational Poitou–Tate consequently identifies ordinary global $H^1$ with the singular line at $2$, and gives ordinary global $H^2=0$ rationally. Adding the real invariant line gives the claimed dimension two in degree one. This equals the generic rank, so derived base change of the perfect complex specializes its determinant without a change of rational cohomological rank.

<a id="cy:pt-length"></a>
The integral calculation explains the two subtractions in $D(h_x)$. The index of global localization will cancel; the remaining local terms will cancel the unused Euler factors and contribute $2w(h_x)$ at the active primes. Let $J$ be the image of the free part of global $H^1(G_{\mathbb Q,S},T_h)$ in $L_2$, and put $i=\operatorname{length}(L_2/J)$. The compact/discrete Poitou–Tate sequence for the mutually orthogonal Kummer conditions contains $$\begin{aligned}
 0\longrightarrow L_2/J
 &\longrightarrow \operatorname{Sel}_{2^\infty}(E^{(h_x)}/\mathbb Q)^\vee
 \longrightarrow H^2(G_{\mathbb Q,S},T_h)\\
 &\longrightarrow \bigoplus_{q\in S}H^2(\mathbb Q _q,T_h)
 \longrightarrow H^0(\mathbb Q,A_h)^\vee\longrightarrow0,
\end{aligned}$$ with the usual real Tate-cohomology terms inserted. Those terms are killed by $2$ and have dimensions bounded by the fixed dimension of $T$. Taking lengths and using local duality gives $$\label{cy:pt-length}
 \operatorname{length}H^2(G_{\mathbb Q,S},T_h)
   =s(h_x)-i+\sum_{q\in S}\operatorname{length}H^0(\mathbb Q _q,A_h)
      +O_E(1).$$ This sequence is the cohomology consequence of integral Poitou–Tate duality with the finite local conditions; see (Nekovář 2006, Theorem 6.3.4 and Section 6.9.1) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[Nekovar2006](#ref-Nekovar2006)], with the real Tate-cochain convention of (Nekovář 2006, vol. 310, sec. 5.7.1.6) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[Nekovar2006](#ref-Nekovar2006)]. No unramified condition replaces the Kummer condition at a bad prime. Global torsion is bounded by its injection into $E^{(h_x)}(\mathbb Q _2)[2^\infty]$, whose order is bounded by the fixed local twisting classes. The finite terms at $2$ and at fixed bad primes are bounded for the same reason. Passing between ordinary and positive cohomology changes only these bounded real and global torsion terms.

Suppose first that the central value is nonzero. The coordinate of $z_{S,x}(0)$ in the free global line has valuation $$v_2(\exp_2^*z_{S,x}(0))-i+O_E(1).$$ For the inverse determinant, torsion in degree two subtracts its length and torsion in degree one adds its length. This sign can be checked on the complex $[\mathbb Z _2\xrightarrow{2^a}\mathbb Z _2]$ in degrees one and two: its inverse determinant lattice maps to $2^a\mathbb Z _2$ in the rational determinant. A vector’s coordinate thus loses $a$. Using <a href="#cy:pt-length" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:pt-length">[cy:pt-length]</a>, the two appearances of $i$ cancel, leaving $$v_2(u_x(0))=
 v_2(\exp_2^*z_{S,x}(0))-s(h_x)
       -\sum_{q\in S}\operatorname{length}H^0(\mathbb Q _q,A_h)+O_E(1).$$

At an active good prime, quadratic inertia acts by $-1$ on the Tate module. Its invariants on $A_h$ are therefore exactly $E[2]$, and taking Frobenius invariants gives length $t_q$. At an unused good prime, reduction identifies the local $2$-primary torsion with $E^{(h_x)}(\mathbb F _q)[2^\infty]$, of length $v_2(\#E^{(h_x)}(\mathbb F _q))$. These unused terms cancel exactly those in <a href="#cy:reciprocity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:reciprocity">[cy:reciprocity]</a>. The active terms, including the primes of $h_0$, sum to $2w(h_x)$. All other terms have already been bounded independently of the growing support. This proves <a href="#cy:central-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:central-formula">[cy:central-formula]</a>.

If the central value is zero, reciprocity makes the dual exponential zero. The rational ordinary specialization then satisfies the finite condition at $2$, and it satisfies the finite conditions elsewhere automatically. It lies in the rational finite Selmer group, which is zero. Its wedge with the real-place class is therefore zero. The constant-rank determinant specialization proves $u_x(0)=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Proofs of the interpolation theorems

<details id="proof-stmt-cy-central-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-cy-central</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#cy:lower-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:lower-bound">4</a>.* Use the preceding construction for the single discriminant $h$. The forward analytic rank-zero theorem gives $s_2(E^{(h)})=0$ and finiteness of its Shafarevich–Tate group. By <a href="#cy:uniform-integrality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:uniform-integrality">[cy:uniform-integrality]</a>, $v_2(u_h(0))\geq-C_{\mathrm{int}}$. Proposition <a href="#cy:central" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:central">10</a> then gives $D(h)\geq-C_{\mathrm{int}}-C_{\mathrm{loc}}$. Every constant in the construction came from the fixed form, a fixed real period line, or the finite set of local twists at $2N$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-cy-central-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-cy-central</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#cy:missing-vertex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:missing-vertex">5</a>.* At every nonzero address the forward rank-zero theorem gives Selmer corank zero. Proposition <a href="#cy:central" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:central">10</a> gives $$v_2(u_x(0))\leq B+C_{\mathrm{loc}}\qquad(x\ne0).$$ Choose an integer $M$ such that $M-C_{\mathrm{int}}>B+C_{\mathrm{loc}}$. By <a href="#cy:uniform-integrality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:uniform-integrality">[cy:uniform-integrality]</a>, the constant coefficient of $2^{C_{\mathrm{int}}}u$ belongs to $\mathbb Z _2[G]$. For sufficiently large $b$, Lemma <a href="#cy:binary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:binary">7</a> gives an $x\ne0$ such that $$v_2(u_x(0)-u_0(0))\geq M-C_{\mathrm{int}}.$$ It follows that $u_0(0)$ is nonzero and $v_2(u_0(0))\leq B+C_{\mathrm{loc}}$: either failure would contradict the displayed upper bound at this $x$. The corank-zero hypothesis at the base and Proposition <a href="#cy:central" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:central">10</a> imply $L(E^{(h_0)},1)\ne0$ and $$D(h_0)\leq B+2C_{\mathrm{loc}}.$$ Taking $C_E$ large enough proves both theorems with a common constant. In particular no loss proportional to $b$, $|Q|$, or the prime count of $h_0$ has entered this argument. ◻

</details>

<a id="rl:section"></a>
### Ring-class interpolation and integral deformations

The ring-class argument has two outputs: a lower bound for the index of a Heegner point at a known simple zero, and a transfer of nonvanishing to an unknown base. The first is uniform as the companion field varies with bounded ramification; the second fixes that field before a binary family grows. We state both outputs before constructing their cochain and evaluation maps. Their determinant proofs occupy Section <a href="#rd:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:section">5</a>.

Fix $E/\mathbf Q$ of conductor $N$, and write $T=T_2E$ and $\Lambda=\mathbf Z_2[[t]]$. The imaginary quadratic field $K=\mathbf Q(\sqrt k)$ satisfies the split Heegner hypothesis at $2N$. We omit the two fields with extra units and, when $E$ has complex multiplication, its CM field. Let $h$ be a product of distinct signed good odd primes, all split in $K$, with $(h,k)=1$; $h=1$ is allowed. The primes dividing $h$ are called the *active conductor primes*. The character $\chi=\chi_h$ is the corresponding quadratic character over $\mathbf Q$.

Fix a modular parametrization $\pi:X_0(N)\to E$ with $\pi(\infty)=0$. Write $H_c$ for the ring-class field of $K$ of conductor $c$, and $y_c$ for the image under $\pi$ of a conductor-$c$ CM point, with fixed CM orientations. The restriction of $\chi_h$ to $G_K$ factors through $\operatorname{Gal}(H_{|h|}/K)$. The primitive weighted Heegner sum is $$P_h=\sum_{\sigma\in\operatorname{Gal}(H_{|h|}/K)}
             \chi_h(\sigma)\,\sigma(y_{|h|}).$$ Through the quadratic-twist identification, it is a point of $E^{(h)}(K)$. When the product of $L(E^{(h)},s)$ and $L(E^{(hk)},s)$ has a simple zero, the Heegner-point theorem makes $P_h$ nontorsion and $E^{(h)}(K)$ has rank one. Let $j_h$ be the $2$-adic index valuation of $P_h$ in the free rank-one lattice $E^{(h)}(K)^\wedge_2/\mathrm{torsion}$. The quantities $w(h)$, $s(h)$, and $s(hk)$ have the meanings fixed in Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>; the two Shafarevich–Tate lengths are finite in this simple-zero case.

<a id="stmt-rd-ring-lower"></a>
<div id="rd:ring-lower" class="corollary">

**Corollary 11** (Ring-class lower bound). *Every simple-zero ring instance in the stated data satisfies <a id="rd:ring-lower-equation"></a>$$2j_h\ge 4w(h)+s(h)+s(hk)-C(E,A),
       \qquad \omega(k)\le A.
 \label{rd:ring-lower-equation}$$*

</div>

<a id="rd:binary-data"></a>
For the second output, fix $K$ and the base $h_0=1$. A binary family has the form $$h_x=\prod_{q\in Q}(q^*)^{\lambda_q(x)},\qquad
 \lambda_q:\mathbb F_2^b\longrightarrow\mathbb F_2\text{ linear},
 \quad x\in\mathbb F_2^b.
 \label{rd:binary-data}$$ All primes in $Q$ are good, odd, disjoint from the fixed support, and split in $K$. All vertices have the same local squareclasses at $2N$ and the same required real sign.

<a id="stmt-rd-missing-vertex"></a>
<div id="rd:missing-vertex" class="theorem">

<a id="rd:base-corank"></a>
**Theorem 12** (The fixed-$K$ missing-vertex implication). *Fix the ring data $E,K$, with $$\operatorname{corank}_{\mathbb Z_2}
       \operatorname{Sel}_{2^\infty}(E/K)=1.
 \label{rd:base-corank}$$ Suppose binary families <a href="#rd:binary-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:binary-data">[rd:binary-data]</a> exist for arbitrarily large $b$, and for every nonzero $x$ the product $L(E^{(h_x)},s)L(E^{(h_xk)},s)$ has a simple zero and <a id="rd:nonzero-bound"></a>$$2j_{h_x}-4w(h_x)-s(h_x)-s(h_xk)\le B
 \label{rd:nonzero-bound}$$ with $B$ independent of the family. Then <a id="rd:missing-conclusion"></a>$$\operatorname{ord}_{s=1}
      \bigl(L(E,s)L(E^{(k)},s)\bigr)=1.
 \label{rd:missing-conclusion}$$ This implication does not assume Mordell–Weil rank one or finite Shafarevich–Tate group at the base.*

</div>

To prove these assertions, we compare the Heegner-point indices with integral Selmer determinants. The present section constructs the limiting complexes and the maps needed to test their classes at new primes. Taking limits of differential matrices alone would not suffice: a cycle in the limit may lift only to approximate cycles at finite precision, and its localization must still be evaluation of a crossed cocycle.

There are two passages to a limit. In the inner construction $K$ and a finite active support are fixed while an auxiliary scalar-conductor prime $r_i$ and the coefficient precision vary. Additional inert *derivative primes* will realize evaluations and switch local conditions. In the outer construction $K$ and the active support may vary, but $\omega(k)$ and the number of retained derivative primes are bounded. This second passage gives the uniform lower bound; the final fixed-$K$ transfer instead uses the bounded clearing factor of Proposition <a href="#rd:family-clearing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:family-clearing">38</a>.

We use $$A_i=(\mathbf Z/2^i\mathbf Z)[t]/(t^i).$$ The same constructions work over $A_i[G]$ and $\Lambda[G]$, where $G$ is a fixed finite elementary abelian $2$-group and the additional character has values in $G$. Statements uniform in the number of generators of $G$ will be identified separately; finite-diagram compactness by itself is a statement for fixed $G$.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Free models with maps to the original cochains

<a id="stmt-rl-finite-model"></a>
<div id="rl:finite-model" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Finite Artin models). *Let $(A,\mathfrak m,k)$ be an Artin local ring, and let $D$ be a complex of flat $A$-modules. Suppose $H^*(D\otimes_A k)$ is finite dimensional and supported in $[a,b]$. There is a bounded finite free complex $C$, with $$\operatorname{rank}_A C^j=\dim_k H^j(D\otimes_A k),$$ and a strong deformation retraction $$i:C\longrightarrow D,\qquad p:D\longrightarrow C,\qquad
 pi=1,\qquad 1-ip=dh+hd.$$ Thus maps to the original complex, as well as maps between finite models, are available. Any fixed finite collection of chain maps, homotopies, triangles, and perfect pairings can be transported to these models.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rl-finite-model"><summary>Proof of stmt-rl-finite-model</summary>

*Proof.* Flat modules over an Artin local ring are free, including modules of infinite rank. Here is a useful version of the argument. Lift a basis of $M/\mathfrak
mM$ to a flat module $M$, and map the corresponding free module to $M$. Its cokernel is zero because $\mathfrak m$ is nilpotent. Flatness shows that the kernel has zero reduction, and nilpotence again makes the kernel zero.

Split the residual complex as its cohomology, with zero differential, plus contractible two-term disks. Lift all the graded bases. These lifts are graded isomorphisms: an inverse modulo $\mathfrak m$ lifts to an inverse by a finite geometric series. Let $d_0$ be the lifted disk differential and write $d=d_0+\epsilon$. All coefficients of $\epsilon$ belong to $\mathfrak m$. For the disk contraction $(i_0,p_0,h_0)$, arrange $h_0^2=h_0i_0=p_0h_0=0$ and $1-i_0p_0=d_0h_0+h_0d_0$. The operators $1+h_0\epsilon$ and $1+\epsilon h_0$ have finite geometric inverses. The elementary perturbation identities give the differential $$d_C=p_0\epsilon(1+h_0\epsilon)^{-1}i_0$$ on the lifted residual cohomology, and give $$i=(1+h_0\epsilon)^{-1}i_0,\quad
 p=p_0(1+\epsilon h_0)^{-1},\quad
 h=h_0(1+\epsilon h_0)^{-1}.$$ Substitution, using $(d_0+\epsilon)^2=0$, proves the asserted contraction identities. These calculations are valid for infinite disk sums: each operator is a module homomorphism and every geometric series terminates at the nilpotence exponent of $\mathfrak m$.

For a map between original complexes use $p'fi$; insert the contraction homotopies when composing maps. A quasi-isomorphism between bounded free complexes has a contractible cone and hence a homotopy inverse. This gives the stated finite-diagram assertion, including the homotopies expressing triangles and perfect duality. ◻

</details>

For a profinite group and a finite free coefficient module over $A$, continuous inhomogeneous cochains are flat: locally constant functions are the filtered union of the modules of functions on finite clopen partitions, and these modules are finite free. Their reduction is the cochain complex with reduced coefficients. The same observations apply to restrictions, mapping cones, and local conditions given by finite free complexes. In the applications below the groups are Galois groups of totally imaginary number fields or nonarchimedean local fields. Their $2$-cohomological dimension and the usual finiteness theorems give the bounded residual cohomology required in Lemma <a href="#rl:finite-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-model">13</a>.

<a id="stmt-rl-finite-limits"></a>
<div id="rl:finite-limits" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (Limits of specified diagrams). *Suppose diagrams over $A_i$, or over $A_i[G]$, have uniformly bounded cohomological amplitudes and graded free ranks, and include the chain homotopies and inverses for every asserted identity or equivalence. After taking a cofinal subsequence, or a nonprincipal ultrafilter limit, their matrices define the same diagram of perfect complexes over $\Lambda$, or $\Lambda[G]$.*

*Moreover, the contractions into the original cochains may be retained for the evaluation construction in Lemma <a href="#rl:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:evaluation">19</a>. Adding finitely many new objects or maps does not require changing already chosen models for the old objects.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rl-finite-limits"><summary>Proof of stmt-rl-finite-limits</summary>

*Proof.* There are finitely many possible graded ranks. Fix them on an ultrafilter-large set. At a fixed precision $j$, all matrix entries belong to the finite ring $A_j$ (or $A_j[G]$), so have unique ultrafilter limits. These limits are compatible as $j$ varies. Matrix identities, including the homotopy-inverse identities, are finite polynomial identities and pass to the limit. New models and maps may be mapped to old models by their already fixed contractions. Passing to any slower cofinal precision has the same old limits. For the all-sequence evaluation statements below, fix this ultrafilter once and for all, and keep the original contraction at every stage. Later arithmetic diagrams must extend these same stages. Restriction to an ultrafilter-large set and passage to slower cofinal coefficient precision preserve every old evaluation on every old group sequence. An arbitrary later subsequence or a different ultrafilter is not allowed in this assertion. Notice that this argument preserves specified arithmetic maps; it does not create an arithmetic map from a formal description of its expected properties. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### Target-side data.

The ring-class fields used to construct a class need not have bounded degree. Their cochain complexes are not objects of the limiting diagram. At each finite stage, first perform descent, Shapiro transfer, the coefficient weighting, and every coset sum. These operations give cochains on the fixed target field $K$, with their local lifts. Only then apply Lemma <a href="#rl:finite-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-model">13</a> to the target global and local complexes and their Selmer cones.

Here is the required inner rank bound. Fix $K$, the active support, a finite set of derivative slots, and, when used, $G$. The permitted ramification set $S_i$ consists of this fixed support and a bounded number of moving primes. Modulo the maximal ideal of $A_i$ or $A_i[G]$, all scalar characters are trivial and the representation is $E[2]$. Put $F=K(E[2])$, a fixed field. Kummer theory bounds $H^1(G_{F,S_i},\mathbf F_2)$ by the rank of the $S_i$-unit group and the dimension of the fixed group $\operatorname{Cl}(F)[2]$. Both bounds are uniform: the number of primes above $S_i$ is bounded and passing to the $S_i$-class group only decreases the required class-group bound. The fixed finite group $\operatorname{Gal}(F/K)$ and inflation–restriction then bound $H^1(G_{K,S_i},E[2])$. Degree zero is bounded by two, and the global Euler characteristic bounds degree two; higher degrees vanish because $K$ is totally imaginary. Local cohomology has uniform bounds as well: the local degrees over $\mathbf Q$ are at most two, there are only finitely many fixed local conditions at $2N$, and the moving good-prime and derivative complexes have fixed ranks. The Selmer-cone exact sequence gives the same conclusion for every target local condition used below. Thus Lemma <a href="#rl:finite-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-model">13</a> gives uniformly bounded target ranks. This inner bound may depend on the fixed active support and is not a bound for a sequence of growing binary families. The separate uniform bound after completion at $(2)$ is Lemma <a href="#rl:residual-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:residual-bound">18</a>; binary-family uniformity is obtained only after the elimination in Proposition <a href="#rd:family-clearing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:family-clearing">38</a>.

For clarity, let $D_i$ be a target cochain complex and write its contraction as $(\iota_i,p_i,h_i)$. A transferred cycle $z_i$ is retained as the vector $p_i z_i$. Its representative in the original target cochains differs from $z_i$ by the boundary $d h_i z_i$. If an arithmetic identity is $z_i-z'_i=d b_i$, retain the target vectors $p_i z_i,p_i z'_i,p_i b_i$; their identity is exact. For a target comparison map $f_i:D_i\to D'_i$, retain $p'_i f_i\iota_i$, together with the contraction homotopy. For example, $$p'_i f_i z_i-p'_i f_i\iota_i p_i z_i
       =d\bigl(p'_i f_i h_i z_i\bigr).$$ Apply this also to local lifts in the Selmer cone, specialization comparisons, and the homotopies expressing duality and local switches. Each specified cycle or boundary witness is a vector in a bounded-rank target term, so adds only finitely many coordinates. Boundary witnesses are compressed after transfer and have target rank; no list indexed by the ring-class cosets is retained. The lemma therefore retains all these identities without retaining a source cochain complex or a growing transfer matrix. The original target contractions remain available in degrees zero, one, and two for all-sequence evaluation. All operations use the same original stages and ultrafilter; a loss of precision is accommodated by one common slower cofinal precision for the entire finite diagram. Adding derivative slots means extending each old stage by finitely many target objects, not taking a limit with an unbounded number of objects.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Kummer conditions, including their derived degree zero

<a id="stmt-rl-kummer"></a>
<div id="rl:kummer" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15** (Integral Kummer local condition). *Let $F$ be a finite extension of a nonarchimedean local field of characteristic zero, and let $B/F$ be any elliptic curve. Put $M_F=B(F)^\wedge_2=\varprojlim_n B(F)/2^nB(F)$. There is a perfect local condition $$U_F=M_F[-1]\longrightarrow R\Gamma(F,T_2B).$$ For every $n\geq1$, derived reduction has $$H^0(U_F\otimes^{\mathbf L}\mathbf Z/2^n)=B(F)[2^n],\qquad
 H^1(U_F\otimes^{\mathbf L}\mathbf Z/2^n)=B(F)/2^nB(F).$$ Its map in degree zero is the isomorphism onto $H^0(F,B[2^n])$, and its map in degree one is the finite Kummer injection. The condition is its own exact orthogonal complement under local Tate duality. These assertions hold at $F\mid2$, for all reduction types, and without a condition on $B[2]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rl-kummer"><summary>Proof of stmt-rl-kummer</summary>

*Proof.* The local analytic description of $B(F)$ shows that $M_F$ is a finitely generated $\mathbf Z_2$-module. Its torsion is the finite group $B(F)[2^\infty]$. Consequently $M_F$ has a free resolution of length at most one. Also $H^0(F,T_2B)=0$, since $B(F)[2^\infty]$ has bounded exponent. The integral Kummer injection into $H^1(F,T_2B)$ therefore defines the displayed morphism using the truncation in degrees at most one of $R\Gamma(F,T_2B)$.

Tensor the free resolution of $M_F$ with $\mathbf Z/2^n$. Its two cohomology groups are $M_F[2^n]$ and $M_F/2^nM_F$. The inverse limits of the ordinary Kummer exact sequences identify their maps with the two maps in the statement. In particular finite torsion invariants have not been discarded.

For orthogonality, compose cup product and the Weil pairing with the local invariant map. A morphism $$M_F[-1]\otimes^{\mathbf L}M_F[-1]
       \longrightarrow\mathbf Z_2[-2]$$ is determined by its homomorphism $M_F\otimes M_F\to\mathbf Z_2$: the possible $\operatorname{Tor}_1$ is in degree one and gives no extra degree-zero morphism to this target. The indicated homomorphism vanishes, because the finite Kummer image annihilates itself at every level by local Tate duality (Milne 2006, I, Theorem 3.2 and Corollary 3.4) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[MilneADT2006](#ref-MilneADT2006)]. Choose a nullhomotopy in free models. The induced morphism $$R\Gamma(F,T_2B)/U_F
       \longrightarrow R\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf Z_2}
                         (U_F,\mathbf Z_2)[-2]$$ is a quasi-isomorphism. This can be checked after reduction modulo $2$: degree one is the perfect pairing of the finite Kummer quotient with the Kummer image, and degree two is dual to $B(F)[2]$. Its perfect cone is therefore zero by derived Nakayama. This proves exact orthogonality and also its compatibility with derived reduction. The derived duality of the ambient local cochains is (Nekovář 2006, Proposition 5.2.4) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[Nekovar2006](#ref-Nekovar2006)]; the Kummer condition and its orthogonality have been constructed above. No part of the proof uses an ordinary filtration or a classification of reduction types. ◻

</details>

Where the scalar deformation is trivial, extend this local condition by derived scalar extension to $A_i$ or $A_i[G]$. For a finite free coefficient representation $M$ unramified at a good prime $v$, use the inflated residue-field complex $$U_v=[\,M\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Fr}_v-1}M\,]
 \quad\text{in degrees }0,1.$$ Its quotient in full local cochains is the singular local complex. The full local condition is the identity on local cochains, and its orthogonal strict condition is zero. The conditions at a derivative prime are different: once its local cochains have the split model constructed in Lemma <a href="#rd:local-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:local-model">25</a>, they are the truncations specified below. Write that model as $$L_v=D^0\oplus(F_v\oplus S_v)[-1]\oplus D^2[-2],$$ where the differential is zero, $D^0$ is dual to $D^2$, and the finite and transverse planes $F_v,S_v$ are each isotropic. Put $$U_{\rm str}=D^0,\qquad
 U_{\rm rel}=D^0\oplus(F_v\oplus S_v)[-1],\qquad
 U_F=D^0\oplus F_v[-1],\qquad U_S=D^0\oplus S_v[-1].$$ Then $U_{\rm str}^{\perp}=U_{\rm rel}$ and each plane condition is its own orthogonal complement. Every displayed condition maps isomorphically onto local $H^0$ and injectively into local $H^1$. Thus they satisfy precisely the detection hypotheses used below. Lemma <a href="#rd:local-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:local-model">25</a> proves their finite arithmetic realization and supplies the isotropic-plane nullhomotopies. These truncated conditions are not identified with the full local condition.

<a id="rl:selmer-definition"></a>
<a id="rl:global-duality"></a>
For a finite permitted set $S$ the Selmer complex is, by definition, $$\label{rl:selmer-definition}
 C_S=\operatorname{Cone}\left(
 R\Gamma(G_{K,S},M)\oplus\bigoplus_{v\in S}U_v
 \longrightarrow\bigoplus_{v\in S}R\Gamma(K_v,M)
 \right)[-1].$$ At $2N$ take the Kummer conditions just constructed. Restriction and localization triangles are the triangles of this cone. The finite rings $A_i$ and $A_i[G]$ are Frobenius coefficient rings; alternatively the duality maps may be checked after reduction to their residue field. Exact local orthogonality and global duality therefore give $$\label{rl:global-duality}
 C_S\simeq R\operatorname{Hom}(C_S^{\perp},A_i)[-3],$$ with the appropriate dual representation. This application uses the orthogonality hypotheses of (Nekovář 2006, Theorem 6.3.4) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[Nekovar2006](#ref-Nekovar2006)]; the theorem is not being used to assert the existence of a varying-prime limit. Complex places contribute zero Tate complexes. If the character is inverted by the nontrivial automorphism of $K/\mathbf Q$, conjugate transport followed by the Weil pairing identifies the two representations over the same scalar ring. All maps and nullhomotopies in this paragraph are finite diagrams to which Lemma <a href="#rl:finite-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-limits">14</a> applies.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### The moving conductor character

<a id="stmt-rl-characters"></a>
<div id="rl:characters" class="proposition">

<a id="rl:trace-bound"></a>
**Proposition 16** (Finite-precision ring-class characters). *Fix finitely many split primes of $K$ above odd rational primes, outside $2N$, and orient one prime in each conjugate pair. There are distinct split rational primes $r_i$ avoiding all the fixed data and characters $$\xi_i:\operatorname{Gal}(H_{r_i}/K)\longrightarrow
                 \mathbf Z/2^{n_i}\mathbf Z,
 \qquad n_i\longrightarrow\infty,$$ such that $\xi_i$ is surjective on the inertia quotient at $r_i$, is zero at the primes above $2N$, and has odd value at each prescribed oriented prime. We can require $r_i\to1$ in $\mathbf Z_2$ and $$\label{rl:trace-bound}
 v_2(2+a_{r_i}(E)),\quad v_2(2-a_{r_i}(E)),\quad
 v_2\det(1\pm\operatorname{Fr}_{r_i}\mid T)\ \leq c_E.$$ The constant $c_E$ is independent of the list and of $K$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rl-characters"><summary>Proof of stmt-rl-characters</summary>

*Proof.* We give the compatibility argument before imposing the Tate condition. Choose prime ideals $\mathfrak p_1,\ldots,\mathfrak p_s$ with pairwise disjoint conjugate pairs, including the oriented prescribed primes and the primes above $2N$, and enough additional split prime ideals to generate $\operatorname{Cl}(K)$. Such additional ideals may avoid any fixed finite set. Put weight $w_j=1$ at a prescribed odd prime and $w_j=0$ at every additional prime and every prime above $2N$. The conjugate weight is $-w_j$. Let $$R=\ker\bigl(\mathbf Z^s\longrightarrow\operatorname{Cl}(K)\bigr),$$ choose a basis $m^{(a)}$ of $R$, and choose $\beta_a\in K^\times$ with $(\beta_a)=\prod_j\mathfrak p_j^{m_j^{(a)}}$.

<a id="rl:kummer-prescription"></a>
Work first over $F_\infty=K(\mu_{2^\infty})$ and adjoin $2^n$-th roots of the finitely many $\beta_a/\overline\beta_a$. Prescribe on these roots the multipliers $$\label{rl:kummer-prescription}
 \zeta_{2^n}^{\,-\sum_j m_j^{(a)}w_j}.$$ The minus sign fixes the choice of connecting map in the ring-class sequence and could be reversed throughout. These prescriptions respect every Kummer relation. Indeed, if $$\prod_a(\beta_a/\overline\beta_a)^{b_a}
                         \in(F_\infty^\times)^{2^n},$$ take its valuation at a prime above each $\mathfrak p_j$ of nonzero weight. These odd primes are unramified in $F_\infty/K$. Thus $2^n$ divides $\sum_a b_am_j^{(a)}$ for each such $j$, proving the required compatibility of <a href="#rl:kummer-prescription" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rl:kummer-prescription">[rl:kummer-prescription]</a>. Primes over $2$ have weight zero and impose no division by a ramification index. Units impose no additional weighted relation. Kummer duality supplies the prescribed automorphism of the radical extension over $F_\infty$.

At a rational prime $r$ split in a sufficiently large finite cyclotomic subfield, the power-residue character of $\mathbf F_r^\times$ is surjective onto $\mu_{2^n}$. The split ring-class exact sequence is $$1\longrightarrow
  (\mathcal O_K/r\mathcal O_K)^\times/(\mathbf Z/r\mathbf Z)^\times
 \longrightarrow\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbf Z+r\mathcal O_K)
 \longrightarrow\operatorname{Cl}(K)\longrightarrow1.$$ There is no unit correction under our exclusion of the extra-unit fields. The first group is $\mathbf F_r^\times$, with a principal relation measured by $\beta/\overline\beta$ at the oriented prime over $r$. Consequently <a href="#rl:kummer-prescription" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rl:kummer-prescription">[rl:kummer-prescription]</a> says exactly that its primitive power-residue character extends to the class group with the specified values $w_j$. Since the ring-class extension is unramified at $2N$, value zero on Frobenius means that the resulting local character is trivial there. Conjugation acts by inversion.

We now show that imposing these finitely many radical conditions cannot force either forbidden Tate trace. In the non-CM case the image of $G_{K(\mu_{2^\infty})}$ is an open subgroup of $\operatorname{SL}_2(\mathbf Z_2)$, by the open-image theorem (Serre 1972) <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[Serre1972](#ref-Serre1972)]. Its index in the corresponding fixed-$E$ image changes by at most two when $K$ changes. Its commutator subgroup contains a fixed open subgroup $U_E$ of $\operatorname{SL}_2(\mathbf Z_2)$. One may see uniformity by starting with a principal congruence subgroup in the fixed image: every index-two subgroup contains its squares, and the commutators of the resulting fixed congruence subgroup are open. Because the radical extension over $F_\infty$ is abelian, these commutators act trivially on it. Hence every permitted Tate coset contains a translate of $U_E$.

For completeness, in the CM case let $M$ be the CM field. The image over $KM(\mu_{2^\infty})$ contains a fixed open subgroup of the norm-one Cartan; the index changes by a bounded factor, as $[KM:\mathbf Q]\leq4$. Choose $\gamma\in G_{KM}$ with $\chi_{\rm cyc}(\gamma)=5^4=625$. This is possible uniformly: the cyclotomic image of $G_{KM}$ has index at most four in $\mathbf Z_2^\times$, so contains fourth powers. On the radical Galois group, conjugation by $\gamma$ is raising to $625$, because all the radicands lie in $K$. On the Tate image over $KM$, conjugation is trivial. Therefore $$\gamma u\gamma^{-1}u^{-625},\qquad
 u\in G_{KM(\mu_{2^\infty})},$$ fixes every prescribed radical and acts on $T$ as $\rho_T(u)^{-624}$. Raising to the fixed nonzero power $624$ carries an open subgroup of the one-dimensional norm-one Cartan onto an open subgroup. This proves the required uniform kernel statement also in the CM case. The open Cartan image used here is the CM part of the usual $\ell$-adic image theorem (Serre 1968, chap. IV, §2.2, Remark) <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[Serre1968](#ref-Serre1968)].

On a coset of an open subgroup of $\operatorname{SL}_2$, trace is not identically $2$ or $-2$. The same holds on a coset of an open norm-one Cartan subgroup; on the other Cartan-normalizer component trace is zero. The relevant determinant-one sets are compact and have finitely many cosets modulo the fixed open subgroup. Choose in each coset an element whose trace is neither forbidden value. The largest of the finitely many resulting valuations is a constant depending only on $E$.

Finally restrict the prescribed radical automorphism and such a Tate lift to a finite Galois extension, including as many roots of unity as required. Chebotarev gives infinitely many rational primes with this Frobenius class; choose the place over the prime to realize the chosen representative. They split in $K$ and have arbitrarily high prescribed congruence to $1$. Since $\det(1\pm\operatorname{Fr}_r)=1\pm a_r+r$, a congruence precision larger than the fixed trace bound gives all of <a href="#rl:trace-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rl:trace-bound">[rl:trace-bound]</a>. This is a finite Chebotarev condition at each stage, not a demand that one prime split in an infinite extension. ◻

</details>

Choose $n_i$ large enough that $1+t$ has order dividing $2^{n_i}$ in $A_i^\times$, and put $\Psi_i=(1+t)^{\xi_i}$. For example $n_i\geq2i$ is sufficient. After the matrix limits, the inertia exponent at the moving prime is a unit of $\mathbf Z_2$, and a prescribed oriented Frobenius exponent is odd. We can orient the generator of inertia so its action is exactly $1+t$.

<a id="stmt-rl-local-acyclic"></a>
<div id="rl:local-acyclic" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (Local height-one tests). *For the individual coefficient system $T\chi_h\Psi$, use full local conditions at the moving prime $r$ and the active primes dividing $h$, without adding primes from a larger binary family. The limiting full local complexes are acyclic over every height-one residue field of $\Lambda$ of characteristic zero. At $(2)$, with residue field $\Omega=\mathbf F_2((t))$, the $r$-complex is acyclic, and so are the complexes at active split primes whose prescribed exponent is odd. These full conditions therefore agree with their orthogonal conditions on the stated tests.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rl-local-acyclic"><summary>Proof of stmt-rl-local-acyclic</summary>

*Proof.* Use tame inertia and unramified Frobenius, or the Hochschild–Serre complex for these two procyclic directions. At $r$, inertia minus one is multiplication by $t$ times a unit. It is invertible on all the indicated fields except the characteristic-zero fiber $t=0$. There, $r\to1$, and the two remaining Frobenius differentials have nonzero determinant by <a href="#rl:trace-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rl:trace-bound">[rl:trace-bound]</a>, including either sign of the quadratic twist. The bound also ensures that the integral local torsion at this central fiber has bounded length.

At an active odd conductor prime, inertia of the quadratic character acts by $-1$. Its differential is thus $-2$ and is invertible in characteristic zero. In characteristic two the quadratic character disappears. At a split prime the Frobenius action is $zB$, where $B\in\operatorname{GL}_2(\mathbf F_2)$ and $z=(1+t)^a$ has odd $a\in\mathbf Z_2$. Its coefficient of $t$ is one, so $z$ is nonconstant. No nonzero polynomial over the finite constant field vanishes at $z$. Thus both Frobenius complexes, with operators $zB-1$ and $q^{-1}zB-1$, are invertible over $\Omega$; here $q=1$ in the residue field. This argument applies also to a reducible residual representation.

The orthogonal full and strict conditions agree when full local cochains are acyclic. Equation <a href="#rl:global-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rl:global-duality">[rl:global-duality]</a> then gives self-duality. The global degree-zero assertion will follow from the evaluation maps in Lemma <a href="#rl:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:evaluation">19</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rl-residual-bound"></a>
<div id="rl:residual-bound" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (Uniform residual sizes). *Suppose $\omega(\operatorname{disc}K)\leq A$, the local twisting classes at $2N$ belong to a fixed finite list, and all active conductor primes are split with odd exponent. After localization and completion at $(2)$, the minimal free ranks of the Selmer complexes are bounded in terms of $E,A$, and the number of retained derivative primes. They do not depend on the number of active conductor primes.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rl-residual-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-rl-residual-bound</summary>

*Proof.* Reduce first over $\mathbf F_2[[t]]$. The quadratic character is now trivial. Removing the active conductor primes from the permitted ramification set changes the global complex by their singular local complexes. These, and their unramified local complexes, are acyclic after inverting $t$, by Lemma <a href="#rl:local-acyclic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:local-acyclic">17</a>. The resulting smaller-support complex, including its comparison maps, can be constructed at the original Artin stages before taking the limit. Its support consists of $2N,r$, and the retained derivative primes. Its $\mathbf F_2((t))$ cohomology dimensions are bounded by those at $t=0$: choose a minimal finite free model over $\mathbf F_2[[t]]$.

Here is a uniform bound for the latter residual cohomology. Put $F=\mathbf Q(E[2])$, a fixed field. Then $FK/F$ is at most quadratic. Enlarge the allowed set of primes of $F$ by those ramified in $K$. Its cardinality is bounded by a constant depending on $E,A$ and the number of derivative primes. Kummer theory bounds the number of generators of the maximal pro-$2$ Galois group over $F$ unramified outside this set, using the fixed unit group, the fixed $2$-class group, and the allowed valuations. The subgroup defining $FK$ has index at most two; the elementary Schreier bound bounds its generator number. Every finite Galois $2$-extension of $FK$ with this ramification has Galois closure of $2$-power degree over $F$, so this subgroup indeed controls the required pro-$2$ extensions of $FK$.

Over $FK$ the residual Tate module is trivial. Restriction and inflation–restriction therefore bound the residual $H^1$ over $K$. The $H^0$ bound is immediate, and the global Euler characteristic or global duality bounds $H^2$. The local terms at the remaining bounded set have bounded dimensions, with degrees of the local fields at most two over the fixed rational local fields. At $2N$ there are only finitely many local quadratic twisting classes. The same bounds apply to the Selmer cone. Lemma <a href="#rl:finite-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-model">13</a> now bounds the minimal ranks. No bound on a torsion exponent is inferred from these rank bounds. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Evaluation on every sequence, rather than only a chosen list

<a id="stmt-rl-evaluation"></a>
<div id="rl:evaluation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (All-sequence evaluation). *Fix $K$ and a sequence of the finite cochain constructions above. Keep the contraction maps into the original global cochains. Let $\Gamma_K=\prod_i G_K$ and $\Gamma=\Gamma_K\rtimes\langle\tau\rangle\subset\prod_iG_{\mathbf Q}$, with simultaneous complex conjugation $\tau$. Thus $\Gamma_K$ has index two in $\Gamma$; the larger unrestricted product of rational Galois groups is not used for induction. The matrix limit defines a coefficient action of $\Gamma_K$ on $\Lambda^2$. For every field $L$ receiving a map from $\Lambda$, there is a natural injection $$H^1(C\otimes_\Lambda L)\longrightarrow
 H^1_{\rm abs}(\Gamma_K,L^2),$$ where the right side denotes abstract crossed-homomorphism classes. Degree-zero evaluation likewise injects $H^0(C\otimes_\Lambda L)$ into $(L^2)^{\Gamma_K}$. Here $C$ may be the global complex or a Selmer complex whose local condition maps induce isomorphisms on local $H^0$ and injections on local $H^1$. The same statement holds for the induced representation of $\Gamma$.*

*There are evaluation maps on arbitrary sequences, not just on the finite list used to detect cohomology. Their degree-two identities remain valid after arbitrary coefficient extension.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rl-evaluation"><summary>Proof of stmt-rl-evaluation</summary>

<a id="rl:eval-boundary"></a>
<a id="rl:eval-cocycle"></a>
*Proof.* Let $C_i$ be a finite model and let $\iota_i$ be its chosen map to the original global cochains, using the projection from the Selmer cone when appropriate. For every $g=(g_i)$ and $h=(h_i)$ define matrices $$e^0_i:C_i^0\to A_i^2,\quad
 e^1_{g,i}:C_i^1\to A_i^2,\quad
 e^2_{g,h,i}:C_i^2\to A_i^2$$ by evaluating $\iota_i$ in degrees zero, one, and two. Their source ranks are fixed and their entries belong to $A_i$. Thus each has a coefficientwise ultrafilter limit, for every choice of $g,h$. No diagonal selection over the set of group elements is necessary: one fixed ultrafilter defines all these limits. Likewise $\rho(g)=\lim_i\rho_i(g_i)$ is a representation. The original cochain identities imply $$\begin{aligned}
 e^1_g d&=(\rho(g)-1)e^0,\label{rl:eval-boundary}\\
 e^2_{g,h}d&=\rho(g)e^1_h-e^1_{gh}+e^1_g.
                         \label{rl:eval-cocycle}
