\end{aligned}$$ All identities pass to the limit because their matrix expressions have finitely many terms. A cycle $x\in C^1\otimes L$ consequently gives the crossed homomorphism $g\mapsto e^1_g(x)$, and a boundary gives a coboundary.

We prove injectivity in a way that also controls base change. At each finite stage let $k=\mathbf F_2$ be the residual field. Choose a space of representatives for residual $H^1$. Evaluate pairs $(c,m)$, where $c$ is in this space and $m\in k^2$, by $b_c(g)-(\rho(g)-1)m$. The intersection of the kernels of all these evaluations consists precisely of pairs $(0,m)$ with $m$ an invariant. Since the vector space is finite dimensional, a list of at most $\dim H^1+2$ elements already has this intersection. For these elements evaluation is a chain map $$C_i\longrightarrow D_i=
 [\,A_i^2\xrightarrow{((\rho_i(g_j)-1))_j}(A_i^2)^d\,]$$ in degrees zero and one, inducing an isomorphism on residual $H^0$ and an injection on residual $H^1$. For Selmer complexes this uses the exact sequence of <a href="#rl:selmer-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rl:selmer-definition">[rl:selmer-definition]</a>: the indicated local degree-zero isomorphisms give $H^0(C_i)=H^0_{\rm glob}$ and $H^1(C_i)\hookrightarrow H^1_{\rm glob}$ residually.

Retain these matrices and their chosen group-element sequences. The cone of the limiting map $C\to D$ has zero residual cohomology in degrees at most zero. Successively cancel its unit differential blocks. The resulting bounded free cone starts in degree one. After every coefficient extension it still starts in degree one; the long exact sequence therefore makes $H^1(C\otimes L)\to
H^1(D\otimes L)$ injective. A crossed cocycle that is a coboundary on all of $\Gamma_K$ is in particular a coboundary on the detecting list, proving the desired injection. The same cone gives an isomorphism on $H^0$ after coefficient extension. Since $H^0(D\otimes L)$ is a subspace of $L^2$, degree-zero evaluation is injective; by <a href="#rl:eval-boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rl:eval-boundary">[rl:eval-boundary]</a>, its image is fixed by every element of $\Gamma_K$, not merely by the detecting list. For induction the same proof has coefficient rank four and the detecting bound is $\dim H^1+4$. ◻

</details>

We can now exclude global invariants on the tests of Lemma <a href="#rl:local-acyclic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:local-acyclic">17</a>. In characteristic two the nontrivial $r$-inertia scalar excludes coefficient invariants. In characteristic zero the same argument works unless that scalar is trivial. In the latter case $t=0$, and $T\otimes\mathbf Q_2$ over $G_K$ is absolutely irreducible: for non-CM curves this follows from the open image, and for CM curves from the open Cartan and its nontrivial normalizer component, since $K$ is not the CM field. Twisting by $\chi_h$ does not alter its invariant subspaces. The degree-zero injection just proved therefore gives $H^0=0$ for the limiting Selmer complex. Together with <a href="#rl:global-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rl:global-duality">[rl:global-duality]</a>, this leaves degrees one and two paired and kills degree three as well.

<a id="stmt-rl-kernel"></a>
<div id="rl:kernel" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20** (The exact common kernel). *In the notation of Lemma <a href="#rl:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:evaluation">19</a>, let $H$ be the group of sequences each of whose entries acts trivially on the full Tate module, on all ring-class fields of $K$, and on the quadratic characters in use. Restriction from the crossed classes in that Lemma to $H$ is injective at every characteristic-zero test. It is also injective at the residual $(2)$ test. The characteristic-zero annihilator needed for this assertion can be chosen with a fixed finite $2$-valuation depending only on $E$, also as $K$ varies.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rl-kernel"><summary>Proof of stmt-rl-kernel</summary>

*Proof.* The Tate image contains an open subgroup of scalar homotheties, both in the CM and non-CM cases (Bogomolov 1981, sec. 2, Theorem 3 and paragraph 7) <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[Bogomolov1981Points](#ref-Bogomolov1981Points)]. Choose once and for all $g\in G_{\mathbf Q}$ with Tate action $u\,1$, where $u\in1+4\mathbf Z_2$ and $u\ne1$. Its square belongs to every quadratic subgroup. Put $$z=g^2\tau g^2\tau^{-1}.$$ The generalized dihedral property of every ring-class extension implies that $z$ is ring-class trivial. It is also trivial on every quadratic character, and its Tate action is the fixed scalar $u^4$. For every sequence in $\Gamma_K$, its commutator with the constant sequence $z$ is in the exact group $H$, since its Tate commutator and all its abelian character images are trivial. Thus $z$ is central in $\Gamma_K/H$ and acts on the coefficients by $u^4$.

Here the elementary central-scalar argument suffices. If a crossed cocycle $b$ is defined on a group with such a central element, comparison of $b(zg)$ and $b(gz)$ gives $$(u^4-1)b(g)=(g-1)b(z).$$ In characteristic zero this makes $b$ a coboundary. Inflation–restriction then gives injectivity on $H$. The valuation of $u^4-1$ is fixed.

In characteristic two use instead, at the $i$-th stage, an inertia element at $r_i$ mapping to the chosen generator of $\xi_i$. Its Tate action is trivial and its ring-class image commutes with all ring-class images. The associated sequence is therefore central modulo $H$, and its coefficient action is $1+t$. Since $t$ is invertible in $\mathbf F_2((t))$, the same calculation applies. It is essential here that $H$ is defined by the exact actions at each stage, not merely by an action converging to the identity. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The outer characteristic-zero test

<a id="stmt-rl-outer-evaluation"></a>
<div id="rl:outer-evaluation" class="proposition">

**Proposition 21** (Integral evaluation through the outer limit). *Let $C_j$ be any family of the preceding limiting complexes, completed over $\mathcal O_j=\widehat{\Lambda_{(2)}}$, possibly for different quadratic fields $K_j$. Suppose their minimal ranks are bounded. Keep their actual evaluation matrices, and transport them through the integral contractions to these minimal models. For a nonprincipal ultrafilter $\mathcal U$, put $$\mathcal O_\infty=
 \left(\prod_{\mathcal U}\mathcal O_j\right)/\mathfrak a,
 \qquad
 \mathfrak a=\{(x_j):
    \{j:v_2(x_j)\geq n\}\in\mathcal U\text{ for every }n\}.$$ Then $\mathcal O_\infty$ is a DVR with uniformizer $2$ and a characteristic-zero fraction field $L_\infty$. The outer complex has an injection of its $H^1$ over $L_\infty$ into crossed classes on the product of the inner sequence groups. Its restriction to the product of their exact common kernels is injective. All arbitrary-sequence degree-two evaluation identities survive. These conclusions also hold after retaining any fixed finite collection of additional integral diagrams.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rl-outer-evaluation"><summary>Proof of stmt-rl-outer-evaluation</summary>

*Proof.* A nonzero element of the quotient has a representative whose valuations are bounded on an ultrafilter-large set. A finite partition then makes its valuation equal to a single integer $n$ on such a set. It is $2^n$ times a unit. Products add these finite valuations, so the quotient is a domain, and every nonzero ideal has an element of smallest valuation. It is consequently a DVR. The integer $2^n$ is nonzero for every $n$, so its fraction field has characteristic zero.

For each $j$, all-sequence evaluation maps originally have entries in $\Lambda$. Extension to $\mathcal O_j$ and composition with an integral contraction still give integral matrices. Thus evaluation on any sequence of inner group elements has an entrywise image in $\mathcal O_\infty$. Equations <a href="#rl:eval-boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rl:eval-boundary">[rl:eval-boundary]</a> and <a href="#rl:eval-cocycle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rl:eval-cocycle">[rl:eval-cocycle]</a> survive for all such sequences. In particular, if an outer cycle is represented by vectors $x_j$ for which $d_jx_j$ has valuation tending to infinity, the evaluation of $d_jx_j$ does too: the degree-two evaluation matrix is integral. This proves the crossed identity for an outer cycle without replacing the $x_j$ by genuine inner cycles.

Over the inner residue field $\Omega_j$, choose a detecting list as in Lemma <a href="#rl:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:evaluation">19</a>; its length is at most $\dim_{\Omega_j}H^1(C_j\otimes\Omega_j)+\operatorname{rank}M$. These lengths are bounded. Include in the integral diagrams the evaluation map to its two-term complex and a contraction of its cone to a complex starting in degree one. Such a contraction uses only unit pivots over $\mathcal O_j$ and has bounded graded sizes. All its matrices, including inverses and homotopies, are integral and pass to the quotient. Its cone still starts in degree one after extension to $L_\infty$. This proves injectivity just as in Lemma <a href="#rl:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:evaluation">19</a>.

Let $H_j$ be the inner exact kernel. The product of the central elements of Lemma <a href="#rl:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:kernel">20</a> is central modulo $\prod_jH_j$. Its scalar is the same fixed $u^4$ in every component, and $u^4-1$ remains nonzero in $\mathcal O_\infty$. The explicit central-scalar calculation and inflation–restriction give the claimed restriction injection. Additional fixed finite diagrams are treated by the same entrywise operations. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rl-irreducibility"></a>
<div id="rl:irreducibility" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22** (Irreducibility on the characteristic-zero tests). *On an inner or outer characteristic-zero test where $\Psi^2\ne1$, the four-dimensional representation induced from $T\chi\Psi$ is absolutely irreducible. If $\Psi^2=1$ on an inner test, it splits as two inequivalent absolutely irreducible quadratic twists. In the outer test $\Psi$ has infinite order.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rl-irreducibility"><summary>Proof of stmt-rl-irreducibility</summary>

*Proof.* The two induced summands are distinguished by $r$-inertia, whose scalars are $\Psi$ and $\Psi^{-1}$. On either summand, multiplying the Tate matrices by scalars does not alter invariant subspaces. The Tate representation restricted to $G_K$ is absolutely irreducible under the exclusions already specified. These facts give absolute irreducibility of the induction when the two inertia characters differ.

For the outer argument this assertion requires a uniform finite witness. The images of the quadratic subgroups have index at most two in the fixed compact $2$-adic Tate image. A compact $2$-adic analytic group is topologically finitely generated and hence has only finitely many closed subgroups of index at most two. Choose one on an ultrafilter-large set. Its absolutely irreducible representation has finitely many matrices spanning $M_2(\mathbf Q_2)$; the determinant expressing this spanning property is a fixed nonzero $2$-adic number. Every one of these Tate matrices is realized by choices of Galois elements in each component. Their accompanying character scalars are units and do not change the spanning assertion. Its determinant remains nonzero in $L_\infty$. This proves absolute irreducibility in that field. Also $1+t$ has infinite order already in every $\Omega_j$: for a nonzero integer $n$, the first nonzero term of $(1+t)^n-1$ occurs in finite degree. It remains a unit modulo $2$ in every component, so no such power becomes one in the outer field.

If $\Psi^2=1$, extension across conjugation gives the two quadratic twists. They are inequivalent, since a quadratic self-twist of the Tate representation here would make its restriction to $G_K$ reducible over an algebraic closure. In the CM case this would force $K$ to be the CM field, which was excluded. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rl-chebotarev-evaluation"></a>
<div id="rl:chebotarev-evaluation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (Simultaneous finite Chebotarev realization). *For any element $u$ of an exact kernel in Lemma <a href="#rl:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:kernel">20</a>, derivative primes can be chosen, at increasing finite precision, whose rational Frobenius is $\tau u$ on all retained data. Their finite localization is evaluation on $(\tau u)^2$. The assertion is compatible with the outer construction of Proposition <a href="#rl:outer-evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:outer-evaluation">21</a> and with any fixed finite list of previous derivative operations.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rl-chebotarev-evaluation"><summary>Proof of stmt-rl-chebotarev-evaluation</summary>

*Proof.* At one Artin stage, a coordinate of a cochain in the image of a finite model is a locally constant function. The finitely many coordinates being used factor through one finite quotient of the absolute Galois group. Enlarge it to contain the Tate action at the required precision, the finitely many ring-class fields and quadratic characters currently in use, and all old finite data. Chebotarev realizes the conjugacy class of $\tau u$ on this finite quotient, with a chosen place realizing the desired representative. It gives a rational prime inert in $K$, outside the old finite set. Because $u$ is exactly Tate-trivial, its Frobenius tends to $\rho_T(\tau)$; in particular $a_\ell\to0$ and $\ell\to-1$. Because $u$ is exactly ring-class trivial, the square Frobenius on the $K$-prime is the identity on every retained ring-class field. The unramified local coordinate is therefore the stated Frobenius-square evaluation.

Repeat at each finite stage with increasing precision. All conditions remain finite, and no density bound or prime-size bound is required. For an outer sequence do this separately for each inner sequence, retaining the relevant finite evaluation matrices and degree-two identities before taking the outer quotient. Approximate inner cycles cause no problem, by the integrality calculation in Proposition <a href="#rl:outer-evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:outer-evaluation">21</a>. Previously imposed finite diagrams are unchanged. ◻

</details>

In induced-module notation, restriction to the exact kernel is additive. For a crossed cocycle $b$ and an exact-kernel element $u$, $$b((\tau u)^2)=(1+\tau)b(u).$$ Indeed $\tau^2=1$, $u$ acts trivially on the coefficients, and the cocycle identity cancels the two occurrences of $b(\tau)$. Projection to either induced summand identifies $\operatorname{im}(1+\tau)$ with a two-dimensional coefficient module, also in characteristic two.

<a id="stmt-rl-limit-evaluation-package"></a>
<div id="rl:limit-evaluation-package" class="theorem">

**Theorem 24** (The limit and evaluation interface). *For the ring data fixed at the start of this section, retain the finite target-side arithmetic diagrams with their maps to original cochains, homotopies, and dualities as in Lemma <a href="#rl:finite-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-limits">14</a>. The moving characters, local conditions, and evaluation constructions then have the following simultaneous properties:*

1.  *Every specified finite target-side arithmetic diagram admits a bounded free model together with maps to its original cochains; the specified maps, homotopies and dualities survive the inner limit.*

2.  *At the stated height-one tests the relaxed active local complexes are acyclic, and the Selmer duality pairs degrees one and two. Integral Kummer conditions at $2N$ include their full derived degree-zero invariants.*

3.  *The residual sizes needed for the outer construction are bounded independently of the active conductor support, with dependence only on $E$, the bound on $\omega(\operatorname{disc}K)$, and the number of retained derivative primes.*

4.  *Inner and outer $H^1$ are detected by actual crossed cocycles, including on the exact common kernel. The evaluations and their degree-two identities can be realized by finite Chebotarev conditions without disturbing earlier finite data.*

*These assertions do not assert an Euler-system reciprocity law or an integral derivative class that has not first been constructed at finite level. Once those finite arithmetic diagrams are constructed, this Theorem supplies their limit compatibility.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rl-limit-evaluation-package"><summary>Proof of stmt-rl-limit-evaluation-package</summary>

*Proof.* Combine Lemmas <a href="#rl:finite-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-model">13</a> and <a href="#rl:finite-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-limits">14</a>, Lemma <a href="#rl:kummer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:kummer">15</a>, Proposition <a href="#rl:characters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:characters">16</a>, Lemmas <a href="#rl:local-acyclic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:local-acyclic">17</a> and <a href="#rl:residual-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:residual-bound">18</a>, Lemmas <a href="#rl:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:evaluation">19</a> and <a href="#rl:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:kernel">20</a>, Proposition <a href="#rl:outer-evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:outer-evaluation">21</a>, and Lemma <a href="#rl:chebotarev-evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:chebotarev-evaluation">23</a>. Every passage uses actual integral matrices of bounded size; arbitrary evaluations are retained by their original contraction maps, not by an inference from a finite list of identities. ◻

</details>

<a id="rd:section"></a>
### Ring-class switches, determinant lattices, and transfer

This section constructs the derivative classes and proves the determinant and transfer statements. The arithmetic models, the character $\Psi$, and their evaluation maps are those of Lemmas <a href="#rl:finite-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-model">13</a>, <a href="#rl:finite-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-limits">14</a>, <a href="#rl:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:evaluation">19</a>, and <a href="#rl:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:kernel">20</a>, and Propositions <a href="#rl:characters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:characters">16</a> and <a href="#rl:outer-evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:outer-evaluation">21</a>. There are two outputs. Corollary <a href="#rd:ring-lower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:ring-lower">11</a> bounds a Heegner-point index at a known simple zero, uniformly in the conductor and with dependence only on a bound for the number of companion primes. Theorem <a href="#rd:missing-vertex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:missing-vertex">12</a> instead fixes the companion field and recovers an unknown base derivative from a family of known simple zeros. The first step is an integral switch: adding a derivative prime replaces a finite local condition by its singular counterpart without changing the determinant valuation. Repeated switches then give the uniform lower bound. For the missing vertex we need more: the determinants must occur in one group-ring family with a uniformly bounded clearing factor. We construct the arithmetic diagrams before applying these two arguments, and verify their specialization and family properties where they are used.

Fix $E/\mathbb Q$, put $T=T_2(E)$, and normalize $v(2)=1$. Let $$\Lambda=\mathbb Z_2[[t]],\qquad
 \mathcal O=\widehat{\Lambda_{(2)}},\qquad
 \Omega=\mathbb F_2((t)).$$ For a perfect cohomological complex $C$, use the inverse determinant line <a id="rd:det-convention"></a>$$\mathcal D(C)=\bigotimes_i(\det C^i)^{(-1)^{i+1}}.
 \label{rd:det-convention}$$ In particular, for a complex in degrees one and two this is $\det C^1\otimes(\det C^2)^{-1}$. A basis of this integral line is fixed whenever a determinant coordinate is mentioned. Changing it multiplies the coordinate by an integral unit.

The ring data consist of an imaginary quadratic field $K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt k)$ split at $2N$, a product $h$ of signed good odd primes split in $K$, and $(h,k)=1$. The fields with extra units and the CM field of $E$ are excluded. Write <a id="rd:weights"></a>$$t_p=\dim_{\mathbb F_2} E[2]^{\operatorname{Fr}_p},\qquad
 w(h)=\frac12\sum_{p\mid h}t_p.
\label{rd:weights}$$ Choose the modular parametrization $\pi:X_0(N)\to E$ normalized by $\pi(\infty)=0$, translating it by the rational point $-\pi(\infty)$ first if necessary. By the Manin–Drinfeld theorem (Manin 1972; Drinfeld 1973) <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[Manin1972](#ref-Manin1972), [Drinfeld1973](#ref-Drinfeld1973)], images of cusp differences are torsion, so one fixed integer kills every cuspidal ambiguity. No assertion that an arbitrary translate has torsion value at $\infty$ is used. The scalar deformation is trivial at $2N$, has unit exponent on the auxiliary conductor inertia, and odd exponent at the indicated split conductor Frobenii. Its auxiliary prime $r$ is allowed to change with the precision. The trace bound in Proposition <a href="#rl:characters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:characters">16</a> is part of the data.

<a id="rd:local-subsection"></a>
#### The local switch and its integral determinant

<a id="stmt-rd-local-model"></a>
<div id="rd:local-model" class="lemma">

<a id="rd:local-cohomology"></a>
<a id="rd:coordinates"></a>
**Lemma 25** (The split local model). *Let a sequence of inert good primes $\ell$ have rational Frobenius lifts tending on $T$ to a fixed complex conjugation $J$. At the unique prime of $K$ above $\ell$, the limiting local complex has $$H^0=M,\qquad H^1=F\oplus S=M\oplus M,\qquad H^2=M,
 \label{rd:local-cohomology}$$ with zero differentials in a free split model. Here $M=T\otimes\Lambda$ with the relevant scalar coefficient structure. The two degree-one coordinates of a cocycle are $$f(c)=c(\gamma^2),\qquad s(c)=c(\sigma),
 \label{rd:coordinates}$$ where $\gamma$ is the chosen rational Frobenius lift and $\sigma$ is an oriented pro-$2$ tame generator. The plane conditions $F=\{s=0\}$ and $S=\{f=0\}$ include $H^0$ and exclude $H^2$. Each is its own orthogonal complement for conjugate-Weil duality. The strict condition $H^0$ and the relaxed condition $H^0\oplus H^1[-1]$ are mutually dual. All four inclusions can be equipped with compatible integral duality triangles.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-local-model"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-local-model</summary>

*Proof.* The prime-to-$\ell$ local cochains are computed by the tame inertia and residue-field directions. In these two directions the differentials are formed from inertia minus one and Frobenius minus one, with the usual Frobenius action on tame inertia. On $M$, inertia is trivial at the derivative prime. The square of a rational Frobenius is trivial on every ring-class character. In the limit it is also the identity on $T$, while $\ell^2$ tends to one. Thus both differentials become zero, giving <a href="#rd:local-cohomology" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:local-cohomology">[rd:local-cohomology]</a>.

Here is a finite-precision check of the pairing, needed at $2$. At a retained quotient killed by $2^a$, impose also $q=\ell^2\equiv1\pmod{2^{a+1}}$, as well as trivial Frobenius-square action on the coefficient module at this quotient. The tame two-generator complex then has zero differentials, with the coordinates in <a href="#rd:coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:coordinates">[rd:coordinates]</a>. Its degree-one scalar generators are the unramified and tame characters. More explicitly, use the presentation $\phi\sigma\phi^{-1}\sigma^{-q}=1$. After augmentation with trivial coefficient action its Fox resolution has terms of ranks $1,2,1$, with $d^0=0$ and $d^1=(0,1-q)$, in the ordered finite and tame coordinates. The diagonal from the same resolution has zero pure-finite term, unit mixed terms, and pure-tame coefficient $\pm\binom q2$. The latter coefficient is the augmented second derivative of $\sigma^{-q}$ together with its adjacent $\sigma$ in the relation. Both $1-q$ and $\binom q2$ vanish modulo $2^a$ under the imposed congruence. Thus this is an actual split complex and an actual pure-plane zero pairing at the retained precision. The field contains the $2^{a+1}$-st roots of unity, so $-1$ is a $2^a$-th power. The norm-residue identity $(x,x)=(x,-1)$ for the $2^a$-Hilbert symbol therefore makes the square of each scalar generator zero. The two generators form a basis; local Tate duality makes their cross pairing a unit. The same calculation applies coefficientwise to the free Artin coefficient module, including its nilpotent $t$ and quadratic variables. Thus the pure plane restrictions are already zero at every retained quotient. All these congruences can be imposed simultaneously with the derivative Frobenius conditions by increasing their precision. Taking the compatible limit proves integral vanishing of the pure squares. It is not deduced from skew symmetry in characteristic two.

<a id="rd:cross-pairing"></a>
Equivalently, in the limiting tame presentation $\phi\sigma\phi^{-1}=\sigma^q$ with $q\to1$, the rank-$(1,2,1)$ resolution specializes to the exterior resolution for two procyclic directions. The finite Hilbert-symbol calculation specifies the cup product on its split cohomology and rules out a hidden square term. Conjugation transports the finite Tate coordinate by $J$ and the singular coordinate by $-J$, with possible scalar units coming from the orientations. Consequently the cross pairing is $$\beta(x,y)=\epsilon\,\langle x,Jy\rangle,\qquad
 \epsilon\in\Lambda^\times,\quad x\in F,\ y\in S,
 \label{rd:cross-pairing}$$ where $\langle\, ,\,\rangle$ is the perfect alternating Weil form. This proves the assertions about orthogonal complements. Since the restrictions of this explicit pairing to either pure plane are zero as maps of the split complexes, their isotropy homotopies may be chosen to be zero. Only a product of two degree-one terms could map to the degree-two local invariant target; the degree-zero terms introduce no additional restriction. These zero homotopies agree on the strict subcomplex. These are the actual orthogonality data required in the Selmer duality construction (Nekovář 2006, Theorem 6.3.4) <a id="pm-cite-50"></a>[[Nekovar2006](#ref-Nekovar2006)]; the compatibility assertion is therefore an assertion about these displayed complexes and maps. Lemma  <a href="#rl:finite-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-limits">14</a> is used to retain this finite diagram together with the unchanged local data. ◻

</details>

We now construct global classes with these local coordinates, using the CM points $y_m\in E(H_m)$ fixed in Section <a href="#rl:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:section">4</a>. For the fixed twist put $c=|h|$. If $I$ is a finite set of derivative primes, put $n=\prod_{\ell\in I}\ell$; the points used for this set have conductor $crn$.

Let $C_I$ denote the limiting Selmer complex with coefficients $T\chi_h\Psi$. Its local conditions are Kummer at $2N$, full at the active primes dividing $h$ and at $r$, singular at the primes in $I$, and unramified elsewhere. At a new derivative prime $\ell\notin I$, the unramified condition is the finite plane $F_\ell$ of Lemma <a href="#rd:local-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:local-model">25</a>. Both plane conditions retain local $H^0$ and omit $H^2$, as in that lemma. The next result constructs the class $P_I$ in this complex; its two displayed localizations are taken at the newly added prime $\ell$.

<a id="stmt-rd-derivative"></a>
<div id="rd:derivative" class="lemma">

<a id="rd:switch"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Descent and the switch at $2$). *Perform each finite ring-class construction and its weighted transfer before retaining its target-side data by the construction following Lemma <a href="#rl:finite-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-limits">14</a>. There is one integer $L\ne0$, depending on the fixed curve, parametrization and fixed local types, such that the classes obtained from the Heegner derivatives of $L$ times the point satisfy, for every finite switched set $I$, $$P_I\in H^1(C_I),\qquad
 f(P_{I\cup\{\ell\}})=0,\qquad
 s(P_{I\cup\{\ell\}})=J f(P_I).
 \label{rd:switch}$$ The same $L$ works for every number of active and derivative primes. The previously imposed singular conditions are retained.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-derivative"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-derivative</summary>

<a id="rd:derivative-identity"></a>
<a id="rd:heegner-relations"></a>
*Proof.* We use Kolyvagin’s derivative operators (Kolyvagin 1990) <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[Kolyvagin1990](#ref-Kolyvagin1990)], keeping their integral descent explicit at $2$. Choose the orientations coherently. The relative ring-class group at an inert prime $\ell$ is cyclic of order $\ell+1$. For distinct new primes these groups form a product: the ring-class exact sequence has kernel the product of the local residue-unit quotients, and the only global units here are $1,-1$, whose images in these quotients are trivial. Choose a generator $\sigma_\ell$ compatibly with the tame orientation in Lemma <a href="#rd:local-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:local-model">25</a>; its pro-$2$ component gives the coordinate $s$ at the retained precision. Set $$D_\ell=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell} i\sigma_\ell^i,\qquad
 D_I=\prod_{\ell\in I}D_\ell.$$ Direct multiplication gives $$(\sigma_\ell-1)D_\ell=(\ell+1)-
             \sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\sigma_\ell^i.
 \label{rd:derivative-identity}$$ The Hecke correspondence and reduction of its CM isogenies give, for every class-group translate, $$\operatorname{Tr}(y_{c r n})=a_\ell y_{c r n/\ell},
 \qquad
 \widetilde y_{c r n}=\operatorname{Fr}_\ell
                       \widetilde y_{c r n/\ell}.
 \label{rd:heegner-relations}$$ These are the point relations, not an application of a residual irreducibility theorem. A fixed multiple of the parametrization removes cuspidal terms.

There is a uniform bound on the $2$-primary torsion over the union of the ring-class fields in use. To see this, choose a fixed nonidentity scalar homothety in the $2$-adic Tate image. Square a lift so that it belongs to every quadratic subgroup under consideration. Multiply it by its conjugate under complex conjugation. Its action on each ring-class field is trivial, because conjugation inverts the abelian ring-class action, while its action on $T$ is still a fixed nonidentity scalar. Write that scalar minus one as $2^e$ times a unit. Then $2^e$ kills the torsion in question. The existence of this fixed scalar, including the CM case, is the Tate-image input used in Lemma <a href="#rl:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:kernel">20</a>.

Work first with coefficients modulo $2^a$. Require $a_\ell$ and $\ell+1$ to vanish to a precision substantially larger than $a$. Equations <a href="#rd:derivative-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:derivative-identity">[rd:derivative-identity]</a> and <a href="#rd:heegner-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:heegner-relations">[rd:heegner-relations]</a> then make the derivative Kummer class invariant under the relative ring-class group. In the inflation–restriction sequence, both the obstruction to descent and the ambiguity of descent are killed by $2^e$, since their coefficient module is killed by $2^e$. Multiplication by $2^{2e}$ therefore gives a descent independent of this ambiguity: descend $2^e$ times the class and multiply its lift by $2^e$. There is no repetition of this factor for individual primes; descent is performed once from the field with the complete conductor.

The target coefficient module is obtained by an integral transfer. For a finite Artin coefficient ring $A$, let $\Delta=\operatorname{Gal}(H_{cr}/K)$, through which the finite scalar and quadratic characters factor. Shapiro’s cochain map and the coefficient homomorphism $$A[\Delta]\longrightarrow A,\qquad
 [\sigma]\longmapsto\chi_h(\sigma)\Psi(\sigma)$$ send the descended class to coefficients $T\chi_h\Psi$. For a universal quadratic family, replace the quadratic value by its group element in $A[G]$, also including the fixed base character. Choose inverse actions on both sides when using that Shapiro convention. Explicitly, these maps are finite coset sums with the indicated scalar weights; there is no division by $\#\Delta$. These chain maps, in all degrees, are used at this finite stage. Naturality of restriction and Kummer corestriction supplies their local lifts and comparison homotopies. Apply the maps to the classes and these witnesses before passing to bounded target models. Neither the complex over $H_{cr}$ nor its induced coefficient module $A[\Delta]$ is retained in the limiting diagram. We check the required target local conditions next.

At a place over $2N$ these descent extensions are unramified. For the identity component of a Neron model, the obstruction to unramified descent vanishes. Indeed, Lang’s theorem gives the assertion on the connected special fiber, and the successive formal-group quotients are additive residue-field modules, whose unramified cyclic cohomology vanishes by the normal-basis and trace calculation. Passing to the inverse limit of these quotients preserves the assertion. The remaining obstruction is killed by the geometric component-group order. Those orders have a uniform bound over unramified extensions of the finitely many fixed local twisting types. Include their product, the descent factor, and the parametrization factor in $L$. Thus the descended class has the integral Kummer condition at $2N$. No good-ordinary or semistability condition was used. Transfer with the scalar character preserves this condition because $\Psi=1$ there.

Here is the local calculation, including the division by two. Apply the derivatives at all primes other than $\ell$, and write $X,Y$ for the resulting lower and upper points. Put $F_\ell=\operatorname{Fr}_\ell$. The lower field splits at the $K$-prime $(\ell)$, since that ideal is principal. Reduction therefore gives $F_\ell^2\widetilde X=\widetilde X$. Good reduction identifies the prime-to-$\ell$ torsion with the torsion of the reduced curve. In the following coordinate formulas, $F_\ell$ acts on reduced points, where it satisfies its Frobenius polynomial. If $Q$ is a $2^a$-division point of $LX$, the finite coordinate of its Kummer class is <a id="rd:finite-formula"></a>$$f=(F_\ell^2-1)\widetilde Q=
       \bigl(a_\ell F_\ell-(\ell+1)\bigr)\widetilde Q.
 \label{rd:finite-formula}$$ We give the descent-cocycle calculation of the other coordinate. Choose $Z$ with $2^aZ=LD_\ell Y$, and choose the descended cocycle $b$ so that on the upper-field Galois group it is $b(g)=(g-1)Z$. A change by the already bounded descent ambiguity has no effect on the eventual limit. The crossed identity gives $$U=(\sigma_\ell-1)Z-b(\sigma_\ell)
                  \in E(H_{crn}).$$ For $g$ in the upper-field Galois group, compare the two expansions of $b(g\sigma_\ell)$; they give $gU=U$. Moreover, $$2^aU=L\bigl((\ell+1)Y-a_\ell X\bigr).$$ Both integers $\ell+1,a_\ell$ are divisible by $2^a$. Consequently $$U_0=L\frac{\ell+1}{2^a}Y-
                      L\frac{a_\ell}{2^a}X$$ is an actual upper-field point with the same $2^a$-multiple as $U$. Thus $U-U_0$ is upper-field torsion of bounded exponent. This comparison with an actual integer quotient is what bounds the ambiguity; arbitrary division points would not give this bound. Reduction makes $\sigma_\ell$ act trivially and $\widetilde Y=F_\ell\widetilde X$. Therefore reduction of $b(\sigma_\ell)=-U$ gives its singular coordinate <a id="rd:singular-formula"></a>$$s=\bigl(a_\ell-(\ell+1)F_\ell\bigr)\widetilde Q
 \label{rd:singular-formula}$$ up to the uniformly bounded descent torsion already described. The Frobenius polynomial yields the exact operator identity <a id="rd:exact-frobenius"></a>$$(F_\ell-a_\ell)
     \bigl(a_\ell F_\ell-(\ell+1)\bigr)
       =a_\ell-(\ell+1)F_\ell.
 \label{rd:exact-frobenius}$$ Thus $s=(F_\ell-a_\ell)f$; when $a_\ell$ vanishes at the retained torsion precision, this is $s=F_\ell f$. Taking the Frobenius limit gives $s=Jf$.

For the new finite coordinate, reduction of the derivative point is <a id="rd:half-factor"></a>$$\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}iF_\ell\widetilde X
       =\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}2F_\ell\widetilde X.
 \label{rd:half-factor}$$ The conductor extension at $\ell$ is totally ramified, so a Frobenius lift may be chosen in the upper-field local Galois group. There the descended cocycle restricts to the Kummer cocycle of the derivative point. If $v_2(\ell+1)\ge a+1$, its displayed reduction is $2^a$ times an $F_\ell^2$-fixed point. Its finite Kummer coordinate is therefore zero modulo $2^a$. The additional $1$ is essential at $2$. It is imposed in the choice of the next precision, and does not multiply the class by a new denominator. Bounded torsion ambiguities disappear on passing from $E[2^a]$ to $E[2^m]$ with $a-m\ge e$. Choose $a$ and the Frobenius precision successively faster than $m$.

The calculation is compatible with every translate in the transfer: transport the place and its Frobenius before applying that translate. The scalar weight on the lower and upper class is identical. Applying the same calculation at each $\ell\in I$ proves the earlier singular conditions as well.

Finally complete the weighted transfer at each stage and retain the resulting target Selmer-cone cycles and their local lifts. An equality of the transferred local classes gives a boundary witness in the target local complex; retain that witness after contraction as well. For instance, retain the degree-zero vectors whose boundaries are the differences in the two switch identities. Include the split local Fox complexes, their zero isotropy homotopies, and the target comparison matrices. The target-side construction in Section <a href="#rl:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:section">4</a> proves that this is a bounded diagram for each fixed active support and finite switched set. Its bound is independent of the degrees of the ring-class fields. When a further derivative prime is selected, extend the same original finite-stage diagram and its contraction maps, using the same inner ultrafilter. Do not construct an unrelated cohomologically equivalent old complex. The earlier evaluations, including all degree-two cocycle identities, are consequently retained by the same contraction maps after each new switch. Lemma  <a href="#rl:finite-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-limits">14</a> applies to these extended diagrams. Their limiting classes give <a href="#rd:switch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:switch">[rd:switch]</a>. The displayed local formulas agree with the calculation underlying (Howard 2004, Proposition 1.7.4) <a id="pm-cite-52"></a>[[Howard2012](#ref-Howard2012)]; Howard’s odd-prime global hypotheses are not being asserted at $2$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
###### The paired arithmetic determinant.

All the target classes and comparison maps are constructed in the same finite diagram. At each Artin precision, use the character of Proposition <a href="#rl:characters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:characters">16</a>, the global Selmer cone, and the Kummer complexes at $2N$. At a derivative prime impose enough Frobenius precision for Lemma <a href="#rd:derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:derivative">26</a> and also $\ell^2\equiv1\pmod{2^{a+1}}$ at every retained quotient killed by $2^a$. Lemma <a href="#rd:local-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:local-model">25</a> then gives the finite, singular, strict and relaxed inclusions with their common zero isotropy homotopies. Applying the actual global duality morphism <a href="#rl:global-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rl:global-duality">[rl:global-duality]</a> constructs their integral localization and duality triangles. Perform the descent and weighted transfers of Lemma <a href="#rd:derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:derivative">26</a> at each finite stage. Include their target cycles, local Kummer lifts and target boundary witnesses in this diagram before applying Lemmas <a href="#rl:finite-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-model">13</a> and <a href="#rl:finite-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-limits">14</a>, using only the target-side data. All remaining maps have bounded target models as both source and target; the ring-class source complexes are not among these objects.

A further switch extends these original finite stages, their contractions and their fixed inner ultrafilter. Theorem  <a href="#rl:limit-evaluation-package" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:limit-evaluation-package">24</a> therefore retains the same degree-zero, degree-one and degree-two evaluations and the exact-kernel restrictions. In particular, the old evaluations are not replaced by maps on an independently chosen equivalent complex.

Here is how a class produces its paired functional. Work over a height-one localization where the full local complexes at the active primes and $r$ are acyclic. By Lemma <a href="#rl:local-acyclic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:local-acyclic">17</a> this includes $\mathcal O$ and every characteristic-zero test used below. These complexes have integral contractions. They identify the relaxed Selmer complex with its orthogonal complex; at switched primes the plane condition is already self-dual, and at $2N$ the integral Kummer condition is self-dual. Lift the class through this comparison and compose it with the retained global duality map. The result is a closed degree-two functional $\lambda_I$, integral up to the single initial multiplier and any fixed old-support correction. Thus it is not an arbitrarily chosen rational cohomology dual.

When the generic cohomology has dimension one in degrees one and two and is zero elsewhere, define $u_I$ to be the coordinate of $$[P_I]\otimes[\lambda_I]\in
 H^1(C_I)\otimes H^2(C_I)^*
       \simeq\mathcal D(C_I)$$ over the fraction field, in the fixed integral determinant basis. For an acyclic constituent set this class-dual coordinate to zero. The generic functional is determined by the same derived duality at all localizations; different local contractions represent that functional, not one cochain asserted to be integral everywhere. For $I=\varnothing$ write $u_h$. The rank-one assertions needed for the arithmetic applications are verified below by rational central specialization.

The arithmetic switch must now be compared in these determinant lattices. A localized class may be divisible by $2$; the next lemma shows that such a division does not cost a factor at each switch.

<a id="stmt-rd-det-switch"></a>
<div id="rd:det-switch" class="lemma">

<a id="rd:valuation-switch"></a>
**Lemma 27** (Exact determinant switch over a DVR). *Let $O$ be a DVR with fraction field $F_O$. Suppose that integral perfect complexes $C_{\rm str},C_F,C_S,C_{\rm rel}$, their localization triangles, and their dualities are the base changes of the diagram in Lemma <a href="#rd:local-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:local-model">25</a>. Suppose the generic cohomology of these four global complexes is concentrated in degrees one and two, that $H^1(C_F\otimes F_O)=F_OA$, and $$f(A)=v_0\ne0,\qquad s(B)=Jv_0,\qquad f(B)=0$$ for a class $B$ in the switched complex. The determinant tensors are formed from each class and its functional under these same dualities. Then the switched generic cohomology has rank one, is spanned by $B$, and $$v_O(u_F)=v_O(u_S).
 \label{rd:valuation-switch}$$ Neither localization is required to be primitive.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-det-switch"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-det-switch</summary>

*Proof.* For this proof only, cohomology without a coefficient ring means cohomology over $F_O$. Nonzero localization of $A$ gives $H^1(C_{\rm str})=0$. The relaxed localization image is self-annihilating in $F\oplus S$, so it has dimension two. It contains the two independent vectors $(v_0,0)$ and $(0,Jv_0)$ in the opposite planes. Its intersections with either plane are therefore exactly one-dimensional. This proves the generic rank assertions.

<a id="rd:triangles"></a>
Put $V=H^1(C_{\rm rel})$, with ordered basis $A,B$. Duality identifies $H^2(C_{\rm str})$ with $V^*$; denote the dual basis by $A^*,B^*$. The two integral triangles are $$C_{\rm str}\longrightarrow C_F\longrightarrow F[-1]\longrightarrow,
 \qquad
 C_{\rm str}\longrightarrow C_S\longrightarrow S[-1]\longrightarrow.
 \label{rd:triangles}$$ Their fraction-field exact sequences are $$0\longrightarrow F_OA\longrightarrow F
       \xrightarrow{\delta_F}V^*
       \longrightarrow F_OA^*\longrightarrow0$$ and the same sequence with $A,F,A^*$ replaced by $B,S,B^*$. The boundary is the dual of the relaxed localization. In view of <a href="#rd:cross-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:cross-pairing">[rd:cross-pairing]</a> and $s(B)=Jv_0$, it satisfies <a id="rd:boundary"></a>$$\delta_F(x)=\epsilon_F\langle x,v_0\rangle B^*,
 \qquad \epsilon_F\in O^\times.
 \label{rd:boundary}$$ Choose $x\in F\otimes F_O$ with $\delta_F(x)=B^*$. Then $v_0\wedge x$ has unit volume in the integral determinant lattice $\det_O F$, because the alternating form on $F$ is unimodular and $\langle x,v_0\rangle$ is a unit. Although $x$ can be nonintegral, this assertion about its exterior product is an equality of lattices. Similarly choose $y\in S\otimes F_O$ mapping to $A^*$. The volume of $Jv_0\wedge y$ is a unit in $\det_O S$.

<a id="rd:common-tensor"></a>
The determinant isomorphisms of the two exact sequences send $$A\otimes(A^*)^*
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad B\otimes(B^*)^*$$ respectively, up to units and the irrelevant determinant sign, to $$(A^*\wedge B^*)^*\otimes(v_0\wedge x),
 \qquad
 (A^*\wedge B^*)^*\otimes(Jv_0\wedge y).
 \label{rd:common-tensor}$$ These comparisons take place in $\mathcal D(C_{\rm str})\otimes\det F$ and $\mathcal D(C_{\rm str})\otimes\det S$. The two plane determinants are identified by a unimodular map. Hence the two coordinates have the same valuation.

This also explains exactly where the finite indices go. For a two-term elementary summand $[O\xrightarrow{\pi^d}O]$ in degrees one and two, its contribution to the coordinate in $\mathcal D$ is $\pi^{-d}$. Such summands, including those arising from nonprimitive localization, occur inside the integral isomorphisms <a href="#rd:triangles" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:triangles">[rd:triangles]</a>. They are not discarded when writing <a href="#rd:common-tensor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:common-tensor">[rd:common-tensor]</a>. All strict torsion contributions are common to the two sides. The argument does not ask for an integral complement to $v_0$; for example $v_0=2^de_1$ is paired with the fractional vector $2^{-d}e_2$, whose exterior product with $v_0$ has unit volume. ◻

</details>

<a id="rd:outer-subsection"></a>
#### Rank reduction and the outer DVR

The local switch preserves a determinant valuation. We now use it to reduce the dimension of any larger specialized Selmer space until a nonzero minor controls that valuation. The first argument handles each characteristic-zero height-one prime. A second, outer limit turns a hypothetical sequence of unbounded losses at $(2)$ into the same rank reduction problem.

<a id="stmt-rd-rank-two"></a>
<div id="rd:rank-two" class="lemma">

**Lemma 28** (Selecting a rank-two evaluation). *Let $\mathcal G$ be a group, $H$ a normal subgroup acting trivially on an absolutely irreducible representation $W$ over a field $F_O$, and suppose that restriction of the relevant cohomology classes to $H$ is injective. For two independent classes, their joint evaluations on $H$ span $W\oplus W$. If the finite localization is the rank-two projection supplied by $1+\tau$ on the induced representation, some actual evaluation has rank two. One of the two classes may be any prescribed nonzero class.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-rank-two"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-rank-two</summary>

*Proof.* On $H$ a crossed homomorphism is an additive homomorphism, and its image is equivariant for $\mathcal G$. A proper submodule of $W\oplus W$ dominating both factors would impose a nonzero scalar linear relation on the two evaluations: the direct sum of two simple modules is semisimple, and $\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal G}(W)=F_O$. That relation, followed by injectivity of restriction, would contradict independence of the classes. Thus the joint span is the whole direct sum. Projecting by $1+\tau$ gives all pairs in the two-dimensional finite plane.

It remains to pass from a span to a single element of the additive evaluation image $U$. If the determinant quadratic polynomial $Q$ vanished on $U$, then its polar form $Q(u+v)-Q(u)-Q(v)$ would vanish for $u,v\in U$. Consequently $$Q\Bigl(\sum_i a_i u_i\Bigr)
 =\sum_i a_i^2 Q(u_i)
  +\sum_{i<j}a_i a_j
       \bigl(Q(u_i+u_j)-Q(u_i)-Q(u_j)\bigr)
 =0.$$ It would vanish on the entire field span, where it is not the zero polynomial. This contradiction proves the assertion. This polarization argument is valid in characteristic two as well. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rd-away-two"></a>
<div id="rd:away-two" class="proposition">

**Proposition 29** (Nonnegative valuations away from $(2)$). *Assume the simultaneous diagrams, derivative classes, and exact-kernel evaluation maps of Lemmas <a href="#rl:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:evaluation">19</a>, <a href="#rl:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:kernel">20</a>, <a href="#rd:local-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:local-model">25</a>, and <a href="#rd:derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:derivative">26</a>. For a primitive constituent whose generic cohomology has rank at most one in degrees one and two and zero elsewhere, its class-dual determinant coordinate belongs to $\Lambda[1/2]$. This includes a simple-zero constituent and a base with $2^\infty$-Selmer corank one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-away-two"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-away-two</summary>

*Proof.* The zero coordinate needs no argument. Otherwise the generic rank is one and the class spans it. Localize at a height-one prime $\mathfrak p\ne(2)$, and denote the residue field by $F_O$. Its characteristic is zero. Lemma <a href="#rl:local-acyclic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:local-acyclic">17</a>, the degree-zero local conditions, and duality give zero fiber cohomology in degrees zero and three. Canceling unit blocks therefore gives a free DVR model in degrees one and two. A primitive vector in its generic kernel has nonzero image in its $\mathfrak p$-fiber: the kernel of a map of free modules over a DVR is saturated. Include this vector in each localization test. The two-sign reduction below has the same pattern as (Howard 2004, Lemmas 1.5.3 and 1.6.4) <a id="pm-cite-53"></a>[[Howard2012](#ref-Howard2012)]; the integral switch and the evaluation hypotheses needed here at $2$ have been constructed above.

Suppose first that $\Psi^2\ne1$ on $F_O$. Inertia at $r$ distinguishes the two summands of the induced representation. The Tate representation over $K$ is absolutely irreducible in characteristic zero: this follows from the open Tate image, or from its CM normalizer when $K$ is not the CM field. Thus the induced representation is absolutely irreducible. If the test Selmer dimension is $s>1$, Lemma <a href="#rd:rank-two" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:rank-two">28</a> and the finite Chebotarev realization in Lemma <a href="#rl:chebotarev-evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:chebotarev-evaluation">23</a> give a derivative prime with rank-two finite localization, nonzero on the chosen primitive vector.

Poitou–Tate duality now determines the effect on the fiber. The relaxed localization image is a self-annihilating plane. It contains the whole finite plane, and hence equals that plane. The strict kernel has dimension $s-2$, and its replacement by the singular condition adds no new class. The fiber dimension therefore drops by two. On the generic field, the localization is nonzero, so Lemmas <a href="#rd:derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:derivative">26</a> and <a href="#rd:det-switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:det-switch">27</a> preserve generic rank one, the nonzero derivative class, and the determinant valuation.

If $\Psi^2=1$, use conjugation, including its action $(1+t)\mapsto(1+t)^{-1}$, to split the fiber into two signs. All prior plane conditions are stable under this involution in the split local model. The two Galois summands are nonisomorphic absolutely irreducible representations; the only CM quadratic self-twist has been excluded. Each sign has one finite and one singular coordinate. The local pairing in a sign is a nondegenerate symmetric hyperbolic pairing, whose only isotropic lines in characteristic zero are the finite and singular axes. If both signs occur globally, choose one class from each, including a nonzero component of the primitive vector, to obtain the same rank-two reduction.

If only one sign occurs and $s>1$, choose a rank-one finite localization nonzero on that vector. In this sign the relaxed image is the finite line. In the other sign its intersection with the finite line is zero, and self-duality forces it to be the singular line. A switch retains the old strict kernel of dimension $s-1\ge1$, introduces the other sign, and preserves the total dimension. The next rank-two switch reduces it. After finitely many such operations the fiber dimension is one. It cannot fall below one, since a primitive vector from the generic rank-one kernel survives after each switch.

At fiber dimension one, a minimal self-dual model over the DVR has one free term in each of degrees one and two and zero differential. The class and paired functional are integral at this height-one prime. Their determinant coordinate is therefore integral. Reversing <a href="#rd:valuation-switch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:valuation-switch">[rd:valuation-switch]</a> proves nonnegative valuation at $\mathfrak p$. Applying this at every height-one prime other than $(2)$, and using that the normal ring $\Lambda$ is the intersection of its height-one valuation rings inside its fraction field, gives membership in $\Lambda[1/2]$.

For the stated arithmetic instances, the rational central specialization has Selmer dimension one and no invariants. Upper semicontinuity bounds the generic dimension by one. If the generic class is nonzero, it spans this space. This uses Selmer corank at an unknown base and does not replace that corank by Mordell–Weil rank. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rd-minor"></a>
<div id="rd:minor" class="lemma">

<a id="rd:minor-bound"></a>
**Lemma 30** (The terminal minor estimate). *Let $$C_i=[\,O_i^n\xrightarrow{D_i}O_i^n\,]$$ be based complexes over DVRs with uniformizer $2$, with generic rank-one kernel. Let $z_i\in\ker D_i$ be integral, and let $\lambda_i\in 2^{-a}(O_i^n)^*$ annihilate $\operatorname{im}D_i$, where $a$ is fixed. If an $(n-1)$-minor $\Delta_i$ has $v(\Delta_i)\le b$, the determinant coordinate $u_i$ of $z_i\otimes\lambda_i$ satisfies $$v(u_i)\ge-a-b.
 \label{rd:minor-bound}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-minor"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-minor</summary>

*Proof.* Permute rows and columns so that the minor is the upper-left block $A_i$. Write $D_i=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_i&b_i\\c_i&d_i
\end{smallmatrix}\right)$. Since its rank is $n-1$, its Schur complement is zero. Over the fraction field, use the triangular basis changes with entries $A_i^{-1}b_i,c_iA_i^{-1}$ to leave $A_i$ and a zero one-by-one block. These triangular changes have determinant one. If $\alpha_i$ is the last coordinate of $z_i$ and $\beta_i$ the value of $\lambda_i$ on the last target basis vector, cancellation gives exactly <a id="rd:minor-formula"></a>$$u_i=\pm\frac{\alpha_i\beta_i}{\det A_i}.
 \label{rd:minor-formula}$$ Here $\alpha_i\in O_i$ and $\beta_i\in2^{-a}O_i$. This proves <a href="#rd:minor-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:minor-bound">[rd:minor-bound]</a>. If $n=1$, the empty minor is $1$ and the same formula applies. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rd-outer-bound"></a>
<div id="rd:outer-bound" class="proposition">

**Proposition 31** (Uniform residual valuation from outer rank reduction). *Assume all of the following properties of a collection of primitive ring instances:*

1.  *after any fixed number of switches, minimal $\mathcal O$-models have size bounded independently of $h$ and of $K$ with $\omega(k)\le A$;*

2.  *the all-sequence evaluation and exact-kernel restriction of Proposition <a href="#rl:outer-evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:outer-evaluation">21</a>, and the finite Chebotarev realization of Lemma <a href="#rl:chebotarev-evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:chebotarev-evaluation">23</a>, hold for these models, retaining their degree-two cocycle identities;*

3.  *the integral localization and duality diagrams and the derivative classes of Lemma <a href="#rd:derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:derivative">26</a> survive this same outer limit; the class and its closed dual functional have a common bounded clearing exponent.*

*Then there is $C(E,A)$ such that <a id="rd:residual-bound"></a>$$v_{(2)}(u)\ge-C(E,A)
 \label{rd:residual-bound}$$ for every simple-zero ring instance. The hypotheses refer to a single simultaneous construction. In particular, separate abstract models realizing its individual cohomology groups do not establish this proposition’s arithmetic applicability.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-outer-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-outer-bound</summary>

*Proof.* The preceding paired-diagram construction supplies the common clearing exponent in the arithmetic application. The bound on minimal sizes is Lemma <a href="#rl:residual-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:residual-bound">18</a>; in particular, active conductor primes do not contribute to that size after residual localization.

Suppose that $v_{(2)}(u_i)\to-\infty$. All these coordinates are nonzero. First put the complexes into square two-term form. At the residual $\Omega$-test, moving-prime inertia excludes global invariants, and the local degree-zero conditions identify Selmer invariants with those global invariants. Thus $H^0=0$. The retained self-duality gives $H^3=0$ and equal dimensions in degrees one and two. A minimal $\mathcal O$-model therefore has only two free terms, of equal rank, in those degrees; all other terms cancel in integral disks. Fix a nonprincipal ultrafilter $\mathcal U$ and fix the bounded rank on an ultrafilter-large set. Write these models as $$C_i=[\,O_i^n\xrightarrow{D_i}O_i^n\,].$$

Transport the class, the uniformly cleared functional, all evaluation matrices, and the finitely many retained comparison maps and boundary witnesses through these integral disk contractions. Their entries remain integral and their identities remain exact. The individual active-place complexes, whose number may grow with the outer instance, are not retained as an outer list of objects. Their effect has already been incorporated in the target cone and its determinant; only the bounded minimal models and the fixed number of switch diagrams enter this limit.

Apply Proposition <a href="#rl:outer-evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:outer-evaluation">21</a> to these models and their retained integral diagrams. It gives a DVR $O_\infty$ with uniformizer $2$, characteristic-zero fraction field $F_\infty$, and injective all-sequence evaluation, including restriction to the product of the exact inner kernels. The induced representation uses one simultaneous complex conjugation, so the sequence group over the quadratic fields has index two in the rational sequence group. Lemma  <a href="#rl:irreducibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:irreducibility">22</a> gives absolute irreducibility over $F_\infty$: the moving inertia character has infinite order, and the finite Tate-matrix witness survives the outer quotient.

The outer complex may have more cohomology than any inner generic complex. Its new cycles can be represented by vectors $w_i$ for which $v(D_iw_i)\to+\infty$, without exact inner lifts. The retained degree-two evaluation matrices are integral, so the evaluations of $D_iw_i$ also tend to zero. This is the part of Proposition <a href="#rl:outer-evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:outer-evaluation">21</a> that makes these new classes available for the following rank reduction.

Choose in each inner generic kernel a primitive vector $v_i$. Some coordinate is a unit on a set in $\mathcal U$, so its outer limit is nonzero. Thus the outer kernel has dimension at least one. If that dimension is $s>1$, apply Lemma <a href="#rd:rank-two" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:rank-two">28</a> to two outer classes, one the surviving primitive vector. Realize their rank-two evaluation by inner derivative primes. This realization only asks for finite quotients at each stage. For an approximate inner cocycle one chooses the retained precision below the valuation of its coboundary; no exact inner lift of the extra outer class is being assumed.

More explicitly, retain the original inner index $i$ for outer instance $j$, its ultrafilter $\mathcal U_j$, and the outer ultrafilter $\mathcal U$. Represent the selected kernel element by $u_j=(u_{j,i})_i$. At stage $(j,i)$, apply finite Chebotarev to this actual element and the old finite data to choose $\ell_{j,i}$. Adjoin its local complexes, the derivative cocycle and the pairing homotopies to the same original stage diagram with its old contractions. Take the same $\mathcal U_j$ and then the same $\mathcal U$. The retained coefficient precision may increase more slowly, but cofinally; the earlier maps and their arbitrary evaluations are not reconstructed by an independent choice of subsequence or model.

The localization of $v_i$ is generically nonzero on a set in $\mathcal U$: its outer image is nonzero. Therefore the original inner spanning class also localizes nontrivially, even if its multiple of $v_i$ has large valuation. Lemma <a href="#rd:det-switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:det-switch">27</a> preserves each inner determinant valuation exactly. On the outer field, the relaxed image is the finite plane and the singular switch lowers $s$ by two, by the same localization exact sequence as in Proposition <a href="#rd:away-two" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:away-two">29</a>. The new inner generic kernels again supply primitive vectors, so the outer rank remains at least one.

Only finitely many switches are necessary: the initial outer rank is bounded and drops by two. After each fixed number of switches the required model-size bound remains available. At the terminal outer rank one, the outer differential has a nonzero maximal boundary minor. Its inner determinants $\Delta_i$ have one finite valuation on a set in $\mathcal U$, by the definition of $O_\infty$. Lemma <a href="#rd:minor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:minor">30</a> then gives a fixed lower bound on $v(u_i)$, a contradiction.

This proof uses the minor only after rank reduction. Bounded matrix size alone would not suffice: the matrices $[2^i]$ have bounded size and unbounded torsion length. In that example the entire matrix becomes zero in the outer quotient; the rank-reduction and evaluation hypotheses would have to supply additional arithmetic operations before a nonzero terminal minor could be used. ◻

</details>

<a id="rd:specialization-subsection"></a>
#### Central specialization and the local indices

The preceding bounds concern the deformed determinant. We next identify its value at $t=0$ with the index of the original Heegner point and the finite Shafarevich–Tate lengths. The comparison retains the whole integral complex; in particular, every active split conductor prime has an explicit local contribution.

<a id="stmt-rd-derived-specialization"></a>
<div id="rd:derived-specialization" class="lemma">

**Lemma 32** (Derived specialization at constant rational rank). *Let $C$ be a perfect $\Lambda$-complex and put $C_0=C\otimes_\Lambda^{\mathbf L}\mathbb Z_2$, where $t=0$. Suppose its generic rational cohomology and its central rational cohomology both have dimension one in degrees one and two and are zero in other degrees. Put $A=\Lambda_{(t)}$, with residue field $\mathbb Q_2$. Let $z$ be a degree-one cycle and $\lambda$ a closed degree-two functional in a finite free model of $C\otimes_\Lambda A$, and let $z_0,\lambda_0$ be their images under $A\to\mathbb Q_2$. Then the generic determinant tensor $[z]\otimes[\lambda]$ is regular at $(t)$ and specializes, under the determinant base-change isomorphism, to $[z_0]\otimes[\lambda_0]$. Its central valuation is computed from the entire integral complex $C_0$, including its torsion cohomology. It cannot be computed by substituting the naive quotient of $H^1(C)$ by $t$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-derived-specialization"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-derived-specialization</summary>

<a id="rd:tor-basechange"></a>
*Proof.* Represent $C$ by a bounded free complex. Derived reduction is its termwise reduction, so the determinant line itself commutes with specialization. For clarity, the cohomology base-change sequence is $$0\longrightarrow H^i(C)/tH^i(C)
 \longrightarrow H^i(C_0)
 \longrightarrow H^{i+1}(C)[t]\longrightarrow0.
 \label{rd:tor-basechange}$$ These last terms are the relevant $\operatorname{Tor}_1$ terms. Diagonalize the complex over the DVR $A$, or over its completion $\mathbb Q_2[[t]]$. A nonunit pivot would create an additional central cohomology class in its two adjacent degrees. The assumed constant dimensions exclude such pivots. Thus over $\mathbb Q_2[[t]]$ there are only invertible disks and the two free cohomology lines. The rational class-dual tensor consequently specializes to the stated central tensor. Over $\mathbb Z_2$, all elementary divisors of $C_0$, including the torsion supplied by <a href="#rd:tor-basechange" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:tor-basechange">[rd:tor-basechange]</a>, remain in its determinant isomorphism. This proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rd-active-local"></a>
<div id="rd:active-local" class="lemma">

**Lemma 33** (A ramified good odd place). *Let $F_v/\mathbb Q_p$ be unramified, $p\ne2$, and let $T'=T_2(E)\otimes\chi$, where $E$ has good reduction and $\chi$ is ramified quadratic. Then <a id="rd:local-length"></a>$$H^0(F_v,T')=0,\qquad
 H^1(F_v,T')=E^\chi(F_v)^{\wedge}_2,\qquad
 \operatorname{length}_{\mathbb Z_2}H^2(F_v,T')
 =\dim_{\mathbb F_2}E[2]^{\operatorname{Fr}_v}.
 \label{rd:local-length}$$ The quotient of the full local complex by the integral Kummer condition therefore contributes precisely this last length in degree two.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-active-local"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-active-local</summary>

*Proof.* The pro-$2$ tame inertia generator acts by $-1$. Its complex has differential $-2$, so its cohomology is zero in degree zero and $T'/2T'$ in degree one. Taking residue-field cohomology gives $$H^1(F_v,T')\simeq E[2]^{\operatorname{Fr}_v},\qquad
 H^2(F_v,T')\simeq E[2]/(\operatorname{Fr}_v-1)E[2].$$ The possible residue-cardinality factor on inertia cohomology is one modulo $2$. Kernel and cokernel of an endomorphism of a finite vector space have the same dimension, proving the length assertion.

The integral local Kummer exact sequence identifies the $2$-adic completion of the points with all of $H^1(F_v,T')$. Indeed the inverse Tate module of the local Weil–Chatelet group is zero here: local duality identifies its $2$-primary part with the dual of the finite completion of the point group. The point completion is finite because the residue characteristic is odd. Equivalently, inertia $-1$ implies that the local $2$-primary torsion is exactly the Frobenius-fixed $2$-torsion. The Kummer complex includes the derived invariants of this finite module on reduction, as in Lemma <a href="#rl:kummer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:kummer">15</a>. The quotient therefore has exactly the degree-two cohomology displayed above. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rd-central-formula"></a>
<div id="rd:central-formula" class="proposition">

<a id="rd:central-value"></a>
**Proposition 34** (The central determinant formula). *Suppose the product $$L(E^{(h)},s)L(E^{(hk)},s)$$ has a simple zero at $s=1$. Use the primitive Heegner sum $P_h$ and its free-line index $j_h$ defined at the start of Section <a href="#rl:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:section">4</a>, and put $$s(h)=\operatorname{length}_{\mathbb Z_2}
             \operatorname{Sha}(E^{(h)}/\mathbb Q)[2^\infty].$$ Use the primitive Selmer determinant coordinate $u_h$ with the fixed class multiplier of Lemma <a href="#rd:derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:derivative">26</a>. For the primitive Selmer diagrams constructed above, we have $$\begin{split}
 v(u_h(0))
  &=2j_h-
    \operatorname{length}_{\mathbb Z_2}
                  \operatorname{Sha}(E^{(h)}/K)[2^\infty]
    -2\sum_{p\mid h}t_p+O_E(1)\\
  &=2j_h-s(h)-s(hk)-4w(h)+O_{E,A}(1),
       \qquad \omega(k)\le A .
 \end{split}
 \label{rd:central-value}$$ The first error includes the auxiliary prime and the fixed parametrization multiplier. The second has no dependence on the number of primes dividing $h$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-central-formula"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-central-formula</summary>

*Proof.* The two rational twists have analytic ranks zero and one. The forward low-analytic-rank theorems give finiteness of their whole Shafarevich–Tate groups; equivalently the Heegner simple-zero theorem over $K$ gives rank one and finite Shafarevich–Tate group there. Thus all finite lengths in this proof refer to known finite groups.

The specialization comparison is made at each finite stage, before the target data are retained. Put $c=|h|$. At the split auxiliary prime $r_i$, the point distribution relation is $$\operatorname{Tr}_{H_{cr_i}/H_c}(y_{cr_i})
       =(a_{r_i}-\sigma_{r_i}-\sigma_{\bar r_i})y_c,$$ where the two Artin symbols have inverse ring-class actions. At $t=0$, the scalar character is trivial, while the rational quadratic character has value $\chi_h(r_i)$ on both symbols. The completed weighted sum therefore gives exactly $L m_i P_h$, where $m_i=a_{r_i}-2\chi_h(r_i)$.

This point identity is retained on bounded target complexes as follows. Let $C_h^{\rm Kum}$ be a fixed free model of the ordinary Kummer Selmer complex over $K$, and let $b_h$ represent the Kummer class of $P_h$. At the retained coefficient precision, enlargement of the ramification set and relaxation at the active and auxiliary primes give a target comparison $j_i:C_h^{\rm Kum}/2^i\to C_i/(t)$. Each retained local condition is an isomorphism on degree zero and an injection on degree one, so the Selmer-cone exact sequence injects its degree-one cohomology into global cohomology. Kummer naturality and the point relation therefore give, after contraction, a target degree-zero vector $v_i$ with $$z_i(0)-L m_i j_i(b_h)=d v_i.$$ Include this vector, the comparison matrix, and the associated local localization triangles in the same target diagram. Their ranks are bounded for this fixed $h,K$, independently of $r_i$ and of $[H_{cr_i}:K]$. All statements are reduced to the same slower cofinal precision if needed. Lemma <a href="#rl:finite-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:finite-limits">14</a> now retains the displayed boundary identity. The trace bound gives $v_2(m_i)\le c_E$; hence their limit $m\in\mathbf Z_2$ on the same ultrafilter is nonzero and has $v_2(m)\le c_E$. The active local quotients specialize to Lemma <a href="#rd:active-local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:active-local">33</a>, and the Kummer conditions at $2N$, including their differential lattices, are unchanged. The central point is therefore nonzero, so generic and central rational cohomology both have rank one.

To specialize the paired functional, choose its representative over $\Lambda_{(t)}$. At an active ramified prime, inertia minus one is $-2$, a unit in this ring. At $r$, the central Frobenius determinants are nonzero by the trace bound. Their local complexes thus contract over $\Lambda_{(t)}$. Composing these contractions with retained global duality gives the same generic functional as the contractions over $\mathcal O$, and specializes to rational central Kummer duality. Lemma <a href="#rd:derived-specialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:derived-specialization">32</a> applies to this representative; no regularity is claimed for an arbitrary $\mathcal O$-cochain representing the functional.

The ordinary integral Kummer complex has compact Selmer cohomology in degree one. Its degree-two torsion has length equal to that of the finite quotient of the discrete Selmer group by its maximal divisible subgroup. This follows by derived base change to $\mathbb Q_2/\mathbb Z_2$ and the exact local Kummer conditions; the base-change terms must be retained. Here that quotient is $\operatorname{Sha}(E^{(h)}/K)[2^\infty]$. The free parts of degrees one and two are paired unimodularly by integral duality. Torsion in degree one and its dual contribution in degree three have bounded length, by the fixed-scalar bound on global torsion.

For completeness, an elementary divisor of size $2^d$ whose cokernel is in degree $i$ contributes $(-1)^{i+1}d$ to the coordinate in the inverse determinant. Consequently the degree-two Shafarevich–Tate torsion subtracts its length. On the free line, a point of index $2^{j_h}$ and its functional each contribute $j_h$, giving $2j_h$.

At an active prime $p\mid h$, both primes of $K$ above $p$ are governed by Lemma <a href="#rd:active-local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:active-local">33</a>, each with length $t_p$. Relaxation therefore subtracts exactly $2t_p$. There is no bound hiding an error at every such prime. At $r$, the central scalar character is trivial; the local degree-two determinant has the valuation of $r+1-a_r\chi_h(r)$ at each split place. The trace condition $$v_2(2-a_r),\ v_2(2+a_r)\le c_E,\qquad
 v_2(r-1)>c_E$$ makes both valuations at most $c_E$. It also bounds the trace multiplier $a_r-2\chi_h(r)$ on the central point. At primes over $2N$ the integral Kummer condition is retained. The local twisting types form a fixed finite set and $K$ is split there; all comparison indices, including those of Neron differential lattices at $2$, are therefore bounded in terms of $E$. This proves the first equality, using Lemma <a href="#rd:derived-specialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:derived-specialization">32</a> to compare the generic tensor with the central tensor.

<a id="rd:isogeny-identity"></a>
We spell out the quadratic comparison needed for the second equality. Set $$A_h=\operatorname{Res}_{K/\mathbb Q}(E^{(h)}_K),
 \qquad B_h=E^{(h)}\times E^{(hk)}.$$ There are isogenies in both directions whose composites are multiplication by $2$. The isogeny-invariance identity for the BSD quotient, with finite Shafarevich–Tate groups, is $$\frac{\#\operatorname{Sha}(A_h)\operatorname{Reg}(A_h)
             \operatorname{Per}(A_h)\prod_p c_p(A_h)}
      {\#A_h(\mathbb Q)_{\rm tors}
       \#A_h^\vee(\mathbb Q)_{\rm tors}}
 =
 \frac{\#\operatorname{Sha}(B_h)\operatorname{Reg}(B_h)
             \operatorname{Per}(B_h)\prod_p c_p(B_h)}
      {\#B_h(\mathbb Q)_{\rm tors}
       \#B_h^\vee(\mathbb Q)_{\rm tors}} .
 \label{rd:isogeny-identity}$$ Regulators and periods are taken with compatible dual and Neron normalizations. This is the Cassels–Tate isogeny identity. The elliptic-curve local-measure formula is due to Cassels (Cassels 1965, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-54"></a>[[Cassels1965](#ref-Cassels1965)]. For the general abelian-variety identity used here, see the proof of (Milne 2006, I, Theorem 7.3, especially (7.3.1), pp. 97–100) <a id="pm-cite-55"></a>[[MilneADT2006](#ref-MilneADT2006)] and (Dokchitser and Dokchitser 2010, sec. 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-56"></a>[[DokchitserDokchitser2010](#ref-DokchitserDokchitser2010)]. This identity requires finite Shafarevich–Tate groups, as already established at these noncentral twists, but does not assume the BSD conjecture. The fixed-degree composites bound the rank-one regulator indices, the torsion ratios, and the differential and period indices by fixed powers of $2$. The identity is essential: a statement that the maps on Shafarevich–Tate groups have kernel and cokernel killed by $2$ would not bound their lengths uniformly.

At every split prime of $K/\mathbb Q$ the component factors of $A_h$ and $B_h$ agree. In particular this cancels all primes dividing $h$. At an odd prime $p\mid k$ outside $2N$, the curve over $K$ has good reduction. Its Weil restriction has connected special fiber: the reduction of Weil restriction over the ramified quadratic extension is an extension of the good special fiber by a connected additive group. Thus its component factor is $1$. Of the two rational twists, one is good and the other is ramified quadratic with good original curve. The latter has $2$-part of its component number equal to $2^{t_p}$. This follows as well from the ramified tame description in Lemma <a href="#rd:active-local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:active-local">33</a>: its connected reduction is unipotent, and its local $2$-primary torsion is the fixed $2$-torsion. At other good nonsplit places the component factors are $1$; at $2N$ there are only the fixed local comparisons. Taking valuations in <a href="#rd:isogeny-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:isogeny-identity">[rd:isogeny-identity]</a> therefore gives <a id="rd:sha-comparison"></a>$$\operatorname{length}\operatorname{Sha}(E^{(h)}/K)[2^\infty]
 =s(h)+s(hk)+\sum_{p\mid k}t_p+O_E(1).
 \label{rd:sha-comparison}$$ Since $0\le t_p\le2$, the sum over $k$ is at most $2A$. Substituting this equality and $2\sum_{p\mid h}t_p=4w(h)$ proves the result. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-central-formula-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-central-formula</summary>
