*Proof of Corollary <a href="#rd:ring-lower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:ring-lower">11</a>.* The arithmetic diagrams constructed with Lemma <a href="#rd:derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:derivative">26</a>, their paired functionals, and the model bound of Lemma <a href="#rl:residual-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:residual-bound">18</a> give the hypotheses of Propositions <a href="#rd:away-two" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:away-two">29</a> and <a href="#rd:outer-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:outer-bound">31</a>. Those propositions give nonnegative height-one valuations away from $(2)$ and a lower bound $-C(E,A)$ at $(2)$. Normality of $\Lambda$ gives $2^{C(E,A)}u_h\in\Lambda$. Its constant coefficient has valuation at least $-C(E,A)$. Apply <a href="#rd:central-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:central-value">[rd:central-value]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="rd:clearing-subsection"></a>
#### The universal correction and bounded clearing

For transfer to a missing vertex, the individual determinant coordinates must belong to one quadratic group-ring family. We first remove the Euler factors at primes unused by a character. We then clear the remaining denominators with a factor whose central valuation and negative Laurent support are bounded independently of the cube size. The base curve and the companion field are fixed in this argument.

Fix $K$ and a binary family <a href="#rd:binary-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:binary-data">[rd:binary-data]</a>, based at $h_0=1$. Put $$G=(\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z)^b,\qquad R=\Lambda[G].$$ Let $g_q\in G$ be the inertia bit: $g_q(x)=(-1)^{\lambda_q(x)}$. Augmentation is denoted by subscript $0$.

The universal class is constructed before making any character evaluation. Fix this finite family and $G$. At precision $i$ take the Heegner point of conductor $r_i\prod_{q\in Q}q$. Let $\varepsilon:G_K\to G$ be the universal quadratic character. In the finite Shapiro transfer of Lemma <a href="#rd:derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:derivative">26</a>, use the coefficient map $$A_i[\operatorname{Gal}(H_{r_i\prod q}/K)]
       \longrightarrow A_i[G],\qquad
 [\sigma]\longmapsto\Psi_i(\sigma)[\varepsilon(\sigma)],$$ with the same inverse-action convention on both sides. Complete this weighted coset sum to obtain a target Selmer-cone cycle over $A_i[G]$, with local lifts and boundary witnesses, and then contract these target data. The target-side rank bound applies for this fixed family and gives one trace class over $\Lambda[G]$. Evaluation at $x$ commutes with the finite coefficient map and every target identity, so gives the full trace for that character. If scalar models have already been chosen, compare the evaluated model with them through the original target cochains and retain the resulting comparison matrices and contraction homotopies. There are only finitely many characters for this fixed $G$. At $t=0$, the universal split-prime multiplier is $$a_{r_i}-2[\varepsilon(\operatorname{Fr}_{\mathfrak r_i})].$$ The conjugate Frobenius values agree because the character values have exponent two. Its evaluation at $x$ is $a_{r_i}-2\chi_{h_x}(r_i)$, so the central boundary comparison used in Proposition <a href="#rd:central-formula" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:central-formula">34</a> is retained in this same group-ring diagram before character evaluation. It includes Euler factors at primes unused by $x$, which the next lemma removes. No inverse limit over growing $G$ or growing active support is used in this construction.

The accompanying dual functional is constructed over $\mathcal O[G]$, not separately at its characters. At a new prime and at $r$, the full local complex is acyclic modulo the maximal ideal by Lemma <a href="#rl:local-acyclic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:local-acyclic">17</a>, hence has an integral contraction over this local ring. Use these contractions to lift the class to the orthogonal Selmer complex, and then apply the retained integral global duality. This gives a closed integral functional, up to one fixed old-support power of $2$ when such a support is included. The present base is $h_0=1$.

<a id="stmt-rd-unused"></a>
<div id="rd:unused" class="lemma">

**Lemma 35** (Removing unused primes in the group ring). *Suppose the universal relaxed trace class and its duality diagram have been constructed over $R$. There is $\eta\in\mathcal O[G]$ such that $\eta u_{\rm rel}$ has each character coordinate equal to the primitive determinant coordinate, up to a $\Lambda$-unit. The correction has no denominator accumulating with $\#Q$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-unused"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-unused</summary>

<a id="rd:unused-factors"></a>
*Proof.* At an unused split prime $q$, write $z$ for the full scalar Frobenius at one orientation. Conjugation gives $z^{-1}$ at the other orientation. The point trace contributes $$e_q=a_q-z-z^{-1},$$ whereas relaxation of the singular quotient contributes $$l_q=
 \det(1-q^{-1}z\operatorname{Fr}_q\mid T)
 \det(1-q^{-1}z^{-1}\operatorname{Fr}_q\mid T).
 \label{rd:unused-factors}$$ Using reciprocal Frobenius conventions changes only the corresponding unit conventions in the paired determinant. The localization triangle gives $u_{\rm rel}=e_q^2u_{\rm prim}/l_q$ at this unused prime. The square is required because the point and its dual both occur.

<a id="rd:eta"></a>
Both factors are units in the local ring $\mathcal O[G]$. To check this it suffices to reduce modulo its maximal ideal, generated by $2$ and the augmentation ideal. There the scalar is $(1+t)^m$ with $m$ odd, and the residual Frobenius polynomial is nonzero at this nonconstant scalar. More precisely, before character evaluation the following identity holds modulo $2$: <a id="rd:residual-euler"></a>$$(1+a_qz+z^2)(1+a_qz^{-1}+z^{-2})
       =a_q^2+z^2+z^{-2}
       =(a_q+z+z^{-1})^2 .
 \label{rd:residual-euler}$$ Thus $l_q/e_q^2-1\in2\mathcal O[G]$, and $$\eta=
 \prod_{q\in Q}
 \left(1+
       \frac{l_q/e_q^2-1}{2}(1+g_q)\right)
 \in\mathcal O[G].
 \label{rd:eta}$$ Its factor evaluates to $l_q/e_q^2$ when $q$ is unused and to $1$ when $q$ is active. This proves the claim. The apparent inertia projector denominator is canceled inside each factor before taking the product. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rd-schur"></a>
<div id="rd:schur" class="lemma">

<a id="rd:laurent"></a>
**Lemma 36** (Laurent expansion and simultaneous elimination). *There is a natural identification $$\mathcal O/2^M\mathcal O
       =(\mathbb Z/2^M\mathbb Z)((t)).
 \label{rd:laurent}$$ Suppose $A=\operatorname{diag}(A_1,\ldots,A_m)$ has entries in $\Lambda$, each residual determinant is nonzero, and $\operatorname{ord}_t(\det\overline A_j)\le h$. If $B=A+2E$ with $E$ over $\Lambda$, then every entry of $B^{-1}\bmod2^M$ has no term below $t^{-Mh}$. The same bound holds for a Schur complement $P-QB^{-1}S$, where $P,Q,S$ have power-series entries. The bound is independent of $m$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-schur"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-schur</summary>

*Proof.* For a denominator $s\in\Lambda\setminus(2)$, write $s=t^d v+2w$, with $v$ a power-series unit. Its inverse modulo $2^M$ is its finite geometric expansion about $t^dv$, which has finite negative support. Conversely all Laurent series of finite negative support occur after inverting $t$. This proves <a href="#rd:laurent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:laurent">[rd:laurent]</a>. It also proves that an element of $\mathcal O$ has a unique series $\sum a_nt^n$, $a_n\in\mathbb Z_2$, with $v(a_n)\to+\infty$ as $n\to-\infty$, and that its valuation is $\min_n v(a_n)$.

Write $\det A_j=t^{d_j}v_j+2w_j$, with $d_j\le h$, and put $$C_j=\frac{\operatorname{adj}(A_j)}{t^{d_j}v_j},
 \qquad C=\operatorname{diag}(C_j).$$ Each entry of $C$ has pole at most $h$, and $BC=1+2F$, where entries of $F$ have the same pole bound. Therefore <a id="rd:inverse-expansion"></a>$$B^{-1}\equiv C\sum_{j=0}^{M-1}(-2F)^j
                                  \pmod{2^M}.
 \label{rd:inverse-expansion}$$ The $j$-th summand has pole at most $(j+1)h$. Adding more entries in a matrix product cannot worsen this pole bound. Multiplication by power-series matrices proves the Schur assertion.

For a block over $R$ whose augmentation is $A$, the block is invertible over $\mathcal O[G]$: its residual determinant in the local ring is a unit. Each sign evaluation is $A+2E_x$ with power-series entries, so the same estimate holds at all characters. If $S_0=0$, its Schur complement has augmentation exactly $P_0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rd-clearing"></a>
<div id="rd:clearing" class="lemma">

<a id="rd:clearing-equations"></a>
**Lemma 37** (Clearing a bounded complex). *Let $D$ be a based complex of finite free $\mathcal O[G]$-modules of total graded rank at most $r_0$, with $D_0$ defined over $\Lambda$. Suppose $D_0\otimes_\Lambda^{\mathbf L}\mathbb Z_2$ has rational cohomology of dimension one in degrees one and two, zero elsewhere, and total integral torsion length at most $h_1$. Let $z$ be an integral cycle and let $\lambda\in2^{-a_0}(D^2)^*$ be a closed functional. Assume every character constituent is either generically acyclic or has the same generic rank-one pattern; in the generically acyclic case its determinant coordinate is defined to be zero. Multiplication of these coordinates by any $\eta\in\mathcal O[G]$ is permitted. Then there are $f,d\in\mathcal O[G]$ such that $$f_xu_x=d_x,\qquad
 f_0\in\Lambda,\qquad
 v(f_0(0))\le C(r_0,h_1,a_0)<\infty.
 \label{rd:clearing-equations}$$ If all entries of $D_x$ have bounded negative Laurent support at each precision, uniformly in $b$, the same is true of $f_x$. No such bound on $d_x,z,\lambda,\eta$ is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-clearing"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-clearing</summary>

*Proof.* Put the central integral complex into elementary-divisor bases over $\mathbb Z_2$. Its positive pivot valuations sum to at most $h_1$. Lift these constant integral basis changes. Formally cancel the same pivot positions upstairs, recording the pivot numerators and all denominators in the resulting rational basis changes and determinant comparison. There are at most $r_0$ such operations. A product $P$ of sufficiently large, but bounded, powers of their numerators clears every denominator. Its degree in the differential entries is bounded in terms of $r_0$; its augmentation belongs to $\Lambda$ and has central valuation bounded in terms of $r_0,h_1$.

On constituents where $P_x\ne0$, the remaining complex is one-by-one in degrees one and two. If its differential is nonzero, the cycle identity forces the projected cycle to be zero, so the same rational determinant expression gives the stipulated zero coordinate. Otherwise the expression is the product of the projected cycle and functional, with the canceled-pivot factor. Clearing denominators gives $$u_x=2^{-a_0}\eta_x Q_x/P_x,\qquad
 Q\in\mathcal O[G].$$ Take $f=2^{a_0}P^2$ and $d=\eta P Q$. On a constituent where a pivot first vanishes, $P_x=0$, so both sides of $f_xu_x=d_x$ are zero. Thus the identity holds on every character. Since $f$ is a polynomial of bounded degree in the bounded differential entries, its central valuation and pole properties follow. All unused-prime corrections and eliminated-block units occur in $d$; none is inserted into $f$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rd-family-clearing"></a>
<div id="rd:family-clearing" class="proposition">

**Proposition 38** (The bounded clearing factor for the family). *Assume the compatible universal Selmer and Heegner diagrams and their primitive specializations. Suppose every character constituent of the universal Selmer complex is generically acyclic or has generic cohomology of dimension one in degrees one and two and zero elsewhere. If the fixed base has $2^\infty$-Selmer corank one over $K$, the corrected coordinates of Lemma <a href="#rd:unused" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:unused">35</a> admit the factorization <a href="#rd:clearing-equations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:clearing-equations">[rd:clearing-equations]</a>, with central valuation and fixed-precision pole bounds independent of $b$ and $\#Q$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-family-clearing"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-family-clearing</summary>

*Proof.* Use the integral universal class and paired functional just constructed. To apply Lemma <a href="#rd:clearing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:clearing">37</a>, it remains to reduce the complex to bounded size without losing uniform control of its negative Laurent powers.

At augmentation, the localization triangles present the complex as the old complex extended by a direct sum of singular local blocks in degrees one and two, one block for each new prime. Its differential is upper triangular, with the old complex a subcomplex. This graded model lifts over $R$: split disks over the augmentation local ring, identify the minimal parts, and lift the invertible basis changes. This is an application of the actual finite diagram construction, rather than just an equality of Euler characteristics.

Each new block has determinant $l_{q,0}$. Its residual $t$-order is uniformly bounded. In the reducible residual range the residual Frobenius polynomial is $(X+1)^2$, and an odd scalar exponent gives order two at each split place; thus order four suffices for the two-place block. Eliminate the entire new block over $\mathcal O[G]$. By Lemma <a href="#rd:schur" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:schur">36</a>, the resulting complex $D$ has the bounded old size, has augmentation equal to the old complex, and has a uniform pole bound at every fixed precision.

The central cohomology of the old complex is the fixed base Kummer cohomology, modified only by the bounded auxiliary-prime terms. Its rational dimension is one in degrees one and two. The quotient of the discrete Selmer group by its maximal divisible subgroup is finite because its Pontryagin dual is a finitely generated $\mathbb Z_2$-module. For this fixed $K,E$, its length is a finite constant, even when the Shafarevich–Tate group itself is not known to be finite. Together with global torsion and the trace bound at $r$, this bounds all central torsion lengths of $D_0$.

Apply Lemma <a href="#rd:clearing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:clearing">37</a>. The determinant of the eliminated block is a unit over $\mathcal O[G]$, so its inverse is an allowed integral multiplier. Include it and $\eta$ from <a href="#rd:eta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:eta">[rd:eta]</a> in the numerator multiplier of that lemma. This proves the assertion.

The dependence here is on fixed $K,E$; it is different from the uniform $C(E,A)$ of Proposition <a href="#rd:outer-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:outer-bound">31</a>. No uniform bound on the finite Selmer quotient as $K$ varies is asserted or needed for the missing-vertex argument. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rd-assembly"></a>
<div id="rd:assembly" class="proposition">

**Proposition 39** (Assembly of the arithmetic diagrams). *For the ring data stated at the start of this section, the actual finite Heegner and Selmer constructions satisfy the simultaneous diagram hypotheses of Propositions <a href="#rd:away-two" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:away-two">29</a>, <a href="#rd:outer-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:outer-bound">31</a>, and <a href="#rd:central-formula" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:central-formula">34</a>. The bounds needed for the lower-bound argument depend only on $E$, the fixed local data, and a bound $A$ on $\omega(k)$. For the binary data <a href="#rd:binary-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:binary-data">[rd:binary-data]</a> with fixed $E,K$ and base Selmer corank one, assume in addition that every character constituent of the universal Selmer complex is generically acyclic or has generic cohomology of dimension one in degrees one and two and zero elsewhere. Then the constructions satisfy all the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#rd:family-clearing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:family-clearing">38</a>, with bounds independent of the cube dimension and of its new-prime support.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-assembly"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-assembly</summary>

*Proof.* The paired arithmetic diagram constructed after Lemma <a href="#rd:derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:derivative">26</a> contains the actual weighted target classes, local Kummer lifts, target comparison and boundary witnesses, and simultaneous duality triangles. The target-side construction gives its inner rank bound for each fixed finite conductor support; no ring-class source complex is passed to the limit. Theorem <a href="#rl:limit-evaluation-package" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:limit-evaluation-package">24</a> retains their evaluations and exact-kernel restrictions. The same-stage extension in Proposition <a href="#rd:outer-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:outer-bound">31</a> shows explicitly that these data remain compatible after each further switch.

Lemma <a href="#rl:residual-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:residual-bound">18</a> bounds the minimal models in terms of $E,A$ and the number of retained derivative primes, not the active conductor support. Outer rank reduction uses only a bounded number of switches, hence only finitely many of these bounds. The paired functional has the common clearing exponent established above. These are the three hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#rd:outer-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:outer-bound">31</a>; the characteristic-zero evaluations and local acyclicity give those of Proposition <a href="#rd:away-two" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:away-two">29</a>. The actual central trace and $\Lambda_{(t)}$-representative, together with the local quotients of Lemma <a href="#rd:active-local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:active-local">33</a>, were verified in the proof of Proposition <a href="#rd:central-formula" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:central-formula">34</a>.

For a binary family, Section <a href="#rd:clearing-subsection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:clearing-subsection">5.4</a> constructs the universal trace and its dual functional over the group ring before evaluating characters. Lemma <a href="#rd:unused" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:unused">35</a> then removes the unused-prime factors integrally. The bounded complex in Proposition <a href="#rd:family-clearing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:family-clearing">38</a> comes from the actual localization triangles and simultaneous Schur elimination, with the order-four residual local bound. Its central torsion is controlled by the finite quotient of the fixed base Selmer group and the bounded auxiliary-prime terms. Together with the stated constituent-rank assumption, these give all the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#rd:clearing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:clearing">37</a>.

For varying $K$, only its bounded ramification count enters the residual-model and local-comparison constants. For the final binary argument $K$ is fixed, so its finite base Selmer quotient may enter the clearing constants. At $2N$ the original Kummer conditions have been retained throughout; the construction imposes no ordinary or semistable hypothesis. ◻

</details>

<a id="rd:transfer-subsection"></a>
#### Coefficient comparison and the missing vertex

The bounded clearing factor reduces the last step to finitely many coefficient congruences. A parity argument on a sufficiently large binary cube makes a nonzero address agree with the base to the required precision. If the base derivative were zero, that congruence would contradict the upper bound already supplied at every nonzero address.

The common-zero count in the following proof is the characteristic-two Chevalley–Warning principle (Heath-Brown 2011, sec. 1, equation (1)) <a id="pm-cite-57"></a>[[HeathBrownChevalleyWarning](#ref-HeathBrownChevalleyWarning)]; its short parity proof is included here.

<a id="stmt-rd-bits"></a>
<div id="rd:bits" class="lemma">

<a id="rd:bit-threshold"></a>
**Lemma 40** (A nonzero address with prescribed coefficient bits). *Let $A_1(x),\ldots,A_q(x)$ be specified Laurent coefficients of sign evaluations of elements of $\mathcal O[G]$. For $M\ge1$, if $$b>q(2^M-1),
 \label{rd:bit-threshold}$$ there is $x\ne0$ with $A_i(x)\equiv A_i(0)\pmod{2^M}$ for every $i$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-bits"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-bits</summary>

*Proof.* Write the element supplying the $i$-th test as $a_i=\sum_{g\in G}a_{i,g}(t)g$, and let $n_i$ be the Laurent degree being tested. Lemma <a href="#rd:schur" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:schur">36</a> gives integral Laurent coefficients, so $$z_i=\sum_{g\in G}[t^{n_i}]a_{i,g}(t)\,g\in\mathbb Z_2[G].$$ The group $G$ is finite, hence coefficient extraction commutes with character evaluation: $(z_i)_x=A_i(x)$. Apply Lemma <a href="#cy:binary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:binary">7</a> to these $q$ integral group-ring elements. Its bound is exactly <a href="#rd:bit-threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:bit-threshold">[rd:bit-threshold]</a>; no bound on the Laurent degrees or on the coefficients enters this argument. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rd-binary-transfer"></a>
<div id="rd:binary-transfer" class="lemma">

<a id="rd:binary-congruence"></a>
**Lemma 41** (Transfer through the bounded factor). *Suppose $f,d\in\mathcal O[G]$, $u_x\in\Lambda[1/2]$, and $f_xu_x=d_x$ for every character. Suppose $f_0\in\Lambda$ and $c=v(f_0(0))<\infty$. If every $f_x\bmod2^M$ has no powers below $-L(M)$, then for $$M>c,\qquad
 b>(L(M)+2)(2^M-1)$$ there is $x\ne0$ satisfying $$v\bigl(u_x(0)-u_0(0)\bigr)\ge M-2c.
 \label{rd:binary-congruence}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-binary-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-binary-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Lemma <a href="#rd:bits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:bits">40</a> to the coefficients of $f_x$ in degrees $-L(M),\ldots,0$ and the constant coefficient of $d_x$. At its nonzero solution, all negative coefficients of $f_x$ are divisible by $2^M$, and its constant coefficient is congruent to $f_0(0)$. Hence the latter has valuation $c$, and $v_{\mathcal O}(f_x)\le c$. Since $d_x$ is integral over $\mathcal O$, $v_{\mathcal O}(u_x)\ge-c$, and the same holds for $u_0$. The Laurent expansion in Lemma <a href="#rd:schur" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:schur">36</a> then shows that all their coefficients lie in $2^{-c}\mathbb Z_2$. They have no negative coefficients because they belong to $\Lambda[1/2]$.

The constant-term convolution converges $2$-adically, and gives $$[t^0]d_x\equiv [t^0]f_x\,u_x(0)
                          \pmod{2^{M-c}},
 \qquad
 [t^0]d_0=f_0(0)u_0(0).$$ Use the imposed congruences and divide by $f_0(0)$. This proves <a href="#rd:binary-congruence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:binary-congruence">[rd:binary-congruence]</a>. The possibly large negative support of $d_x$ was never tested; only its constant coefficient was used. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-rd-binary-transfer-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-rd-binary-transfer</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#rd:missing-vertex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:missing-vertex">12</a>.* For every character the primitive generic cohomology has rank at most one. At nonzero vertices this follows from the simple-zero theorem; at zero it follows from <a href="#rd:base-corank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:base-corank">[rd:base-corank]</a> and rational specialization. There are no generic invariants, and duality pairs degrees one and two; thus each primitive constituent is acyclic or has exactly the rank-one pattern. The unused-prime local blocks are acyclic over the fraction field, by the nonzero factors <a href="#rd:unused-factors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:unused-factors">[rd:unused-factors]</a>. The universal Selmer complex has the same generic pattern, as required by Proposition <a href="#rd:family-clearing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:family-clearing">38</a>. With this hypothesis verified, Proposition <a href="#rd:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:assembly">39</a> supplies the compatible universal arithmetic construction. By Proposition <a href="#rd:away-two" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:away-two">29</a> and Lemma <a href="#rd:unused" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:unused">35</a>, the corrected coordinates belong to $\Lambda[1/2]$. Proposition <a href="#rd:central-formula" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:central-formula">34</a> gives $$v(u_x(0))\le B_1\quad(x\ne0)$$ for a constant $B_1$ depending only on $B,E,K$ and the fixed local data.

If the base derivative vanished, the Gross–Zagier formula would make the central Heegner sum torsion: its canonical height is a fixed nonzero multiple of that derivative. Here only the conductor-one formula at the fixed base $h=1$ is used, with the corrected primitive sum and the split Heegner data of (Cai, Shu, and Tian 2014, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-58"></a>[[CaiShuTian2014](#ref-CaiShuTian2014)]. At the base this implies $u_0(0)=0$. Indeed, if the generic rank is zero the coordinate was defined to be zero. If the generic rank is one, its central rank is also one by <a href="#rd:base-corank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rd:base-corank">[rd:base-corank]</a>, so Lemma <a href="#rd:derived-specialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:derived-specialization">32</a> identifies its specialized class with the zero rational Kummer class of that torsion point.

Proposition <a href="#rd:family-clearing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:family-clearing">38</a> supplies $f,d$ with a common bound $c_*$ on $v(f_0(0))$ and common pole bounds $L(M)$. Choose $$M>\max\{c_*,B_1+2c_*\}$$ and then take $b$ above the threshold of Lemma <a href="#rd:binary-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:binary-transfer">41</a>. That lemma gives a nonzero vertex with $v(u_x(0))\ge M-2c_*>B_1$, a contradiction. Thus the base derivative is nonzero. The classical split Heegner sign is odd, so the base product has a simple zero. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rd-interface-status"></a>
<div id="rd:interface-status" class="remark">

*Remark 42* (The simultaneous arithmetic interface). The determinant argument can be reused only with the simultaneous arithmetic construction verified in Proposition <a href="#rd:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:assembly">39</a> and Theorem <a href="#rl:limit-evaluation-package" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:limit-evaluation-package">24</a>. Neither the published duality theorem nor the classical Heegner formulas alone supplies that interface.

</div>

<a id="sec:nonvanishing"></a>
### Auxiliary twists with prescribed local behavior

We need auxiliary twists with specified local behavior and nonzero central value or derivative. For a fixed witness, no bound on the prime count is needed. When the curve itself varies, we also need central-value witnesses with a uniformly bounded number of prime factors. In both cases their primes can be chosen beyond any previously specified finite set.

<a id="stmt-nv-local-witness"></a>
<div id="nv:local-witness" class="proposition">

**Proposition 43** (Local witnesses). *Fix an elliptic curve $A/\mathbb Q$ and a nonempty filter of fundamental discriminants prime to $2N_A$, with specified real sign and unit squareclasses at finitely many primes. If the root number in the filter is $+1$, it contains $d$ of arbitrarily large absolute value with $L(A^{(d)},1)\ne0$. If the root number is $-1$, it contains such $d$ with $L'(A^{(d)},1)\ne0$. Any additional finite set of primes may be excluded.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-nv-rough"></a>
<div id="nv:rough" class="proposition">

**Proposition 44** (Bounded-factor completion). *There is an absolute integer $A_0$ with the following property. In the positive-root-number case of Proposition <a href="#nv:local-witness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nv:local-witness">43</a>, one can require $\omega(d)\le A_0$ and require every prime divisor of $d$ to exceed any fixed bound. The size threshold for the resulting $d$ may depend on the curve, the filter, and the excluded primes; $A_0$ does not.*

</div>

The use of a nonnegative first moment and a lower linear sieve to obtain bounded-factor twists goes back to Hoffstein–Luo (Hoffstein and Luo 1997, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-59"></a>[[HoffsteinLuo1997](#ref-HoffsteinLuo1997)]. We retain an arbitrary fixed progression and track an absolute factor-count bound without optimizing it. The central moment follows the model of (Radziwiłł and Soundararajan 2015, Proposition 2) <a id="pm-cite-60"></a>[[RadziwillSoundararajan2015](#ref-RadziwillSoundararajan2015)], which has a sharper divisor error; the derivative calculation adapts the first-moment argument of (Iwaniec 1990) <a id="pm-cite-61"></a>[[Iwaniec1990](#ref-Iwaniec1990)]. The enlarged modulus and the absolute exponents needed here are retained in the proof below.

A nonzero first moment alone supplies the derivative witness. For the central-value completion, positivity allows us to sieve the first moment, so we also need an error term for each prescribed squarefree divisor of the twist parameter.

Write $f$ for the weight-two newform of $A$, normalized so that its central point is $1/2$, and write $\lambda(n)$ for its normalized Hecke eigenvalues. Thus $$L(s,f\otimes\chi)=\sum_{n\ge1}\lambda(n)\chi(n)n^{-s},
\qquad |\lambda(n)|\le d(n).$$ Choose $H$ divisible by $8N_A$, a reduced residue class $a\bmod H$, and $\sigma\in\{1,-1\}$ such that $\sigma a\equiv1\bmod4$. The required twists have discriminant $\sigma n$ with $n>0$ squarefree and $n\equiv a\bmod H$. A filter is a finite union of such progressions, so it suffices to use one of them. Fix a nonnegative smooth nonzero $\Phi$ supported in $(1,2)$.

<a id="stmt-nv-moment"></a>
<div id="nv:moment" class="lemma">

**Lemma 45** (A fixed-progression moment). *There are absolute constants $\eta>0$ and $B>0$ such that the following assertions hold. For squarefree $b$ prime to $H$, put $$S_{j,b}(X)=
\sum_{\substack{n>0\ {\rm squarefree}\\ n\equiv a\ (H)\\b\mid n}}
 L^{(j)}(1/2,f\otimes\chi_{\sigma n})\Phi(n/X).$$ If the common root number is $+1$, then $$S_{0,b}(X)=cX\prod_{p\mid b}\beta_p+
 O_{f,H,\Phi}(b^B X^{1-\eta}),
\qquad
c>0,\quad 0<\beta_p<1,\quad
\beta_p=\frac1p+O(p^{-2}).$$ The last implied constant is absolute. If the common root number is $-1$, then $$S_{1,1}(X)=cX\log X+c_1X+
 O_{f,H,\Phi}(X^{1-\eta}),\qquad c>0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nv-moment"><summary>Proof of stmt-nv-moment</summary>

<a id="nv:afe"></a>
*Proof.* We first control the square-divisor tail, then use Poisson summation to separate a positive main term from a power-saving error. Tracking the dependence on the imposed divisor $b$ will give the absolute exponents. Let $G(z)=e^{z^2}$ and $$V_j(y)=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{(2)}
       G(z)\Gamma(1+z)y^{-z}\frac{dz}{z^{j+1}}.$$ Writing $Q_A=\sqrt{N_A}/(2\pi)$, the symmetric approximate functional equation in the required sign is $$\label{nv:afe}
L^{(j)}(1/2,f\otimes\chi_{\sigma n})
=2\sum_{u\ge1}\frac{\lambda(u)\chi_{\sigma n}(u)}{\sqrt u}
 V_j\!\left(\frac{u}{Q_A n}\right).$$ For $j=1$, differentiating the completion contributes no central-value term, because that value is zero.

<a id="nv:second"></a>
*Square-divisor truncation.* The quadratic large sieve (Heath-Brown 1995, Theorem 1 and Corollary 1) <a id="pm-cite-62"></a>[[HeathBrown1995](#ref-HeathBrown1995)], with the approximate functional equation, gives for fixed $\delta>0$ $$\label{nv:second}
 \sum_{\substack{0<t\le Z\ {\rm squarefree}\\
                 \sigma t\ {\rm fundamental}\\(t,2N_A)=1}}
 |L(1/2+\delta+i\tau,f\otimes\chi_{\sigma t})|^2
 \ll_{f,\rho,\delta} Z^{1+\rho}(1+|\tau|)^{C_\rho}.$$ Here the polynomial exponent can be chosen independently of the level. To see that square indices cause no extra power, write each coefficient index as $ur^2$ with $u$ squarefree. Minkowski’s inequality and the quadratic large sieve bound the square root of the moment of a polynomial of length $U$ by $$(ZU)^{O(\rho')}
 \sum_{r\le\sqrt U}\frac{\sqrt{Z+U/r^2}}r
 \ll (ZU)^{O(\rho')}(\sqrt Z+\sqrt U).$$ Coprimality restrictions at $r$ are discarded only after taking absolute squares. To include both parts of the approximate functional equation, work first with $t\asymp T$. Their lengths are $U\ll_f T(1+|\tau|)^C$, with rapidly decreasing tails. The main coefficients have the additional factor $n^{-\delta}$, which improves the partial-summation bound. The dual coefficients have $n^{\delta}$ and lose at most $U^{\delta}$, whereas their functional-equation multiplier contributes $T^{-2\delta}$, up to a fixed power of $1+|\tau|$. The resulting factor is at most $T^{-\delta}(1+|\tau|)^{C_\delta}$ and causes no positive power of $T$. Mellin inversion separates the smooth $t$-weights; the gamma factors control the resulting integrals and tails. Taking the auxiliary large-sieve exponents sufficiently small and summing dyadic intervals proves Equation <a href="#nv:second" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nv:second">[nv:second]</a>.

<a id="nv:tail"></a>
Let $\mathcal A_j(n)$ denote the right side of Equation <a href="#nv:afe" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nv:afe">[nv:afe]</a>, also for nonsquarefree $n\equiv a\bmod H$. Expanding $\mu^2(n)$ and retaining divisors $d\le Y$ leaves an error $$\label{nv:tail}
 O_{f,H,\Phi,\rho}(X^{1+\rho}/Y).$$ Indeed write $n=tv^2$, with $t$ squarefree. The omitted terms have $v>Y$, and their multiplicity is at most the divisor count of $v$. Let $L^{(v)}(s,f\otimes\chi_{\sigma t})$ be the primitive-twist $L$-function with its Euler factors at primes dividing $v$ removed. For $0<\delta_{\mathrm M}<\rho/8$, Mellin inversion gives $$\mathcal A_j(tv^2)=\frac{2}{2\pi i}
 \int_{(\delta_{\mathrm M})}
 G(z)\Gamma(1+z)(Q_Atv^2)^z
 L^{(v)}(1/2+z,f\otimes\chi_{\sigma t})
 \frac{dz}{z^{j+1}}.$$ Choose the large-sieve and divisor exponents smaller still. On this line the removed Euler factors cost an arbitrarily small fixed power of $v$. Cauchy’s inequality and Equation <a href="#nv:second" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nv:second">[nv:second]</a> give, for suitably small $\rho'>0$, $$\sum_{t\le2X/v^2}^{\!*}
 |L(1/2+\delta_{\mathrm M}+i\tau,f\otimes\chi_{\sigma t})|
 \ll_{f,\rho',\delta_{\mathrm M}}
 (X/v^2)^{1+\rho'}(1+|\tau|)^C,$$ where the star retains the primitive discriminant conditions. The gamma factors control the integral. The divisor count, the removed Euler factors, and $X^{\delta_{\mathrm M}}$ are absorbed in $X^\rho$, leaving $X^{1+\rho}\sum_{v>Y}v^{-2}\ll X^{1+\rho}/Y$. The factor $z^{-2}$ changes only the constant.

*Poisson summation and the main term.* Only $(d,H)=1$ occurs, because $n\equiv a\bmod H$ is a reduced class. For $d\le Y$ put $J=[d^2,b]$ and $c_J\equiv aJ^{-1}\bmod H$, which is therefore defined. Write $u=u_1q$ with $u_1\mid H^\infty$ and $(q,H)=1$, and put $\kappa(u_1)=\chi_{\sigma a}(u_1)$. Define $$G_\nu(q)=\sum_{y\bmod q}\left(\frac yq\right)e(\nu y/q),
\quad
 W_j(z,R)=\int_0^\infty
 \Phi(x)V_j\!\left(\frac z{Q_Ax}\right)e(-Rx)\,dx,$$ where $e(x)=e^{2\pi ix}$. Put $$T_j(J)=\sum_{\substack{n>0,\ n\equiv a\ (H)\\J\mid n}}
             \mathcal A_j(n)\Phi(n/X).$$ Poisson summation in the fixed progression gives the exact identity <a id="nv:poisson"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
\label{nv:poisson}
 T_j(J)
 &=\frac{2X}{HJ}
 \sum_{u_1\mid H^\infty}\frac{\lambda(u_1)\kappa(u_1)}{\sqrt{u_1}}
 \sum_{(q,H)=1}\frac{\lambda(q)}{q^{3/2}}
       \left(\frac{\sigma JH}{q}\right) \\
 &\hspace{14mm}\cdot
 \sum_{\nu\in\mathbb Z}G_\nu(q)e(\nu c_J\bar q/H)
 W_j\!\left(\frac{u_1q}{X},\frac{\nu X}{HJq}\right).
 \nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ Here $q\bar q\equiv1\bmod H$. The arithmetic factor follows directly by applying the Chinese remainder theorem to $$\sum_{\substack{x\bmod Hq\\x\equiv c_J\ (H)}}
 \left(\frac xq\right)e(\nu x/(Hq))
=e(\nu c_J\bar q/H)\left(\frac Hq\right)G_\nu(q).$$ When $(q,J)>1$, the additional Jacobi symbol is zero.

At zero frequency only square $q$ occur. Put $$H_p(z)=1+(1-p^{-1})
     \sum_{r\ge1}\lambda(p^{2r})p^{-r(1+2z)},\qquad H_p=H_p(0),$$ $$L_H(z)=\sum_{u_1\mid H^\infty}
             \lambda(u_1)\kappa(u_1)u_1^{-1/2-z},
 \qquad F_J(z)=\prod_{p\nmid HJ}H_p(z).$$ The zero-frequency term is $$\frac{2X}{HJ}\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{(2)}
G(z)\Gamma(1+z)(Q_AX)^z\widehat\Phi_0(z)L_H(z)F_J(z)
\frac{dz}{z^{j+1}},
\quad
\widehat\Phi_0(z)=\int_0^\infty\Phi(x)x^z\,dx .$$ For the Satake parameters $\alpha_p,\alpha_p^{-1}$, with $|\alpha_p|=1$, we have $$\sum_{r\ge0}\lambda(p^{2r})x^r
   =\frac{1+x}{(1-\alpha_p^2x)(1-\alpha_p^{-2}x)}.$$ Thus $F_J$ factors through $L(1+2z,\operatorname{sym}^2f)$ and a correction product convergent near $\Re z\ge-1/16$. Omitting a prime dividing $J$ removes its correction factor and inserts an inverse symmetric-square Euler polynomial; it creates no pole even when $H_p(z)$ has zeros. The omitted factors cost at most $J^{B_1}$ for an absolute $B_1$. Symmetric-square continuation, including the CM case (Gelbart and Jacquet 1978, Theorem 9.3 and Remark 9.9) <a id="pm-cite-63"></a>[[GelbartJacquet1978](#ref-GelbartJacquet1978)], and the polynomial strip bounds from its functional equation therefore give an error $O_{f,H,\Phi}(J^{B_1}X^{1-1/16})$ when shifting to $\Re z=-1/16$.

The residues have a positive leading coefficient. Indeed $$F_p^{\rm ev}=\sum_{r\ge0}\lambda(p^{2r})p^{-r}
 =\frac{1+p^{-1}}{(1-\alpha_p^2/p)(1-\alpha_p^{-2}/p)}>0,\quad
 H_p=p^{-1}+(1-p^{-1})F_p^{\rm ev}>p^{-1}.$$ The fixed Euler factors are positive at the center, and $L(1,\operatorname{sym}^2f)>0$ by the Rankin–Selberg residue formula. This also holds for CM forms. Summing the residue over the square divisor gives, at $p\nmid Hb$, the factor $H_p-p^{-2}$, and at $p\mid b$ the factor $p^{-1}-p^{-2}$. Consequently $$\beta_p=\frac{p-1}{p^2H_p-1}
        =\frac1{1+pF_p^{\rm ev}}
        =p^{-1}+O(p^{-2}).$$ For $j=1$, the double pole gives $cX\log X+c_1X$ with the same positive leading coefficient. Extending the residue sum from $d\le Y$ to all $d$ costs $O(b^{B_2}X^{1+\rho}/Y)$; the $d^{-2+\rho}$ tail also permits differentiation of that sum.

*The nonzero frequencies.* It remains to obtain a power saving with polynomial dependence on $J$. Put $\epsilon_q=1$ or $i$ according as $q\equiv1$ or $3\bmod4$ and $\widetilde G_\nu(q)=\epsilon_q^{-1}G_\nu(q)$. This is multiplicative in $q$. Since $4\mid H$, expand $\epsilon_q e(\nu c_J\bar q/H)$ in characters $\psi\bmod H$. For $\nu\ne0$ the resulting Dirichlet series is $$D_{\psi,J,\nu}(s)=
 \sum_{(q,H)=1}\frac{\lambda(q)}{q^s}
 \frac{\widetilde G_\nu(q)}{\sqrt q}
 \left(\frac{\sigma JH}{q}\right)\psi(q)
 =L(s,f\otimes\xi_{\rm prim})C_{\psi,J,\nu}(s),$$ where $\xi(q)=\psi(q)(\sigma JH\nu/q)$. At $p\nmid2HJ\nu$ its local factor is $1+\lambda(p)\xi(p)p^{-s}$; dividing by the $\mathrm{GL}_2$ Euler factor leaves a polynomial $1+O(p^{-2\Re s})$. The bound at primes dividing $\nu$ has an absolute exponent. Indeed, for an odd prime $p$, put $a=v_p(\nu)$. Directly summing over the lifts of a residue modulo $p$ gives $$G_\nu(p^r)=
\begin{cases}
 \varphi(p^r),&r\text{ even},\ r\le a,\\
 -p^{r-1},&r\text{ even},\ r=a+1,\\
 \epsilon_p p^{r-1/2}
       \left(\dfrac{\nu/p^{r-1}}p\right),
       &r\text{ odd},\ r=a+1,\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}$$ For even $r$ this is the Ramanujan sum; for odd $r$ the inner sum of lifts vanishes unless $p^{r-1}\mid\nu$, after which the primitive quadratic Gauss sum gives the displayed value. In particular $|\widetilde G_\nu(p^r)/p^{r/2}|\le p^{r/2}$, and the local polynomial on $\Re s\ge3/4$ is bounded by $\sum_{r\ge0}(r+1)p^{-r/4}\le C_0$ with $C_0$ absolute. The inverse degree-two Euler polynomial is bounded by another absolute constant $C_1$. Primes dividing $J$ simply remove the local sum, and have the same bound after multiplication by that inverse Euler polynomial. Thus all primes dividing $J\nu$ cost at most $(C_0C_1)^{\omega(J\nu)}\le (2J|\nu|)^C$, for an absolute $C$. The remaining correction product converges absolutely on $\Re s\ge3/4$, since its local error is $O(p^{-3/2})$. Hence the correction is holomorphic on $\Re s>1/2$ and has the asserted polynomial bound with absolute exponent. The conductor of $\xi_{\rm prim}$ is at most $4HJ|\nu|$, and the twisted cuspidal $L$-function is entire. Thus, with absolute $C$, $$|D_{\psi,J,\nu}(3/4+i\tau)|
 \ll_{f,H}(J|\nu|)^C(1+|\tau|)^C.$$

Integration by parts in both weight variables gives, for fixed $r,s$ and any $K>0$, $$|(z\partial_z)^r(R\partial_R)^sW_j(z,R)|
 \ll_{f,\Phi,r,s,K}(1+z)^{-K}(1+|R|)^{-K}(1+|\log z|).$$ The logarithm is needed only for $j=1$ and is $O(\log X)$ on the actual sums, where $z\ge X^{-1}$. For an absolute small $\rho>0$ the sums can therefore be restricted to $$u_1q\ll X^{1+\rho},\qquad
 0<|\nu|\ll_H JX^{2\rho},\qquad
 q\gg_H X^{1-\rho}/J,$$ at a negligible cost with polynomial dependence on $J$. A trivial bound $|G_\nu(q)|\le q$ gives a polynomial majorant for the discarded tails. To identify the sum to be shifted, write $D_{\psi,J,\nu}(s)=\sum_q\gamma(q)q^{-s}$. On a dyadic range $q\asymp T$, take a fixed smooth cutoff $\vartheta$ and put $$w_T(y)=\vartheta(y)
 W_j\!\left(\frac{u_1Ty}{X},\frac{\nu X}{HJTy}\right).$$ Each dyadic $q$-sum after the character expansion is therefore $$\sum_q\frac{\gamma(q)}q w_T(q/T)
 =\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}
       \widehat w_T(s)T^sD_{\psi,J,\nu}(1+s)\,ds,$$ where $\widehat w_T(s)=\int_0^\infty w_T(y)y^{s-1}\,dy$. The weight bounds permit a contour shift to $\Re s=-1/4$, giving $$O_{f,H,\Phi}\bigl((J|\nu|)^C X^{C'\rho}T^{-1/4}\bigr).$$ There is no residue. Summing over $\nu$, over the dyadic ranges, and over $u_1$ (for which $\sum_{u_1\mid H^\infty}|\lambda(u_1)|/\sqrt{u_1}<\infty$) is bounded, up to a further $X^{O(\rho)}$, by $$\frac XJ\,J^C(JX^{2\rho})^{C+1}
       (X^{1-\rho}/J)^{-1/4}
 =J^{2C+1/4}X^{3/4+O(\rho)}.$$ This gives $O(J^{B_3}X^{3/4+C''\rho})$. Choose $\rho$ so that this is $O(J^{B_3}X^{7/8})$.

*Choosing the truncation.* Since $J\le bd^2$, summing the two contour errors for $d\le Y$, and adjoining Equation <a href="#nv:tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nv:tail">[nv:tail]</a>, gives $$O_{f,H,\Phi}\bigl(
 b^{B_4}Y^{2B_4+1}X^{1-\kappa}
 +b^{B_4}X^{1+\rho}/Y\bigr)$$ for absolute $\kappa>0$ and $B_4$. Take $Y=X^\delta$ with $(2B_4+1)\delta<\kappa/2$, and then $\rho<\delta/4$. Both errors have the form in the statement, with absolute positive saving. This proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-nv-moment-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-nv-moment</summary>

*Proof of Propositions <a href="#nv:local-witness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nv:local-witness">43</a> and <a href="#nv:rough" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nv:rough">44</a>.* For negative root number, Lemma <a href="#nv:moment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nv:moment">45</a> gives a nonzero sum of derivatives in every sufficiently large dyadic interval. One derivative is nonzero, and its central zero is already forced by the sign.

For positive root number, use the nonnegative sequence $$a_n=\mu^2(n)\mathbf1_{n\equiv a\ (H)}
      L(1/2,f\otimes\chi_{\sigma n})\Phi(n/X).$$ Its nonnegativity follows from the Waldspurger formula (Waldspurger 1981) <a id="pm-cite-64"></a>[[Waldspurger1981](#ref-Waldspurger1981)]. The lemma gives a dimension-one sieve density $\beta_p$ and an absolute exponent of distribution: choose $\theta>0$ with $(B+1)\theta<\eta$ and put $D=X^\theta$. The total remainder for squarefree $b\le D$ is $$O_{f,H,\Phi}(D^{B+1}X^{1-\eta})=o(X/\log X).$$ The density estimate also gives the interval product condition $$\prod_{\substack{w\le p<z\\p\nmid H}}(1-\beta_p)^{-1}
 \le \frac{\log z}{\log w}
       \left(1+\frac{C_{f,H}}{\log w}\right)
 \qquad(2\le w<z).$$ Indeed, comparison with $(1-p^{-1})^{-1}$ contributes a logarithmic error $O(\sum_{p\ge w}p^{-2})$, and Mertens’ product estimate gives the displayed bound; finitely many small primes are absorbed in $C_{f,H}$. The same comparison gives $$\prod_{\substack{p<z\\p\nmid H}}(1-\beta_p)\asymp_{f,H}(\log z)^{-1}.$$ The lower linear sieve (Friedlander and Iwaniec 2010, Theorem 11.13) <a id="pm-cite-65"></a>[[FriedlanderIwaniec2010](#ref-FriedlanderIwaniec2010)], with $z=D^{1/s}$ and any fixed $s>2$, therefore gives positive remaining weight. Since $z=X^{\theta/s}$ tends to infinity, its surviving parameters eventually avoid all primes up to any prescribed bound. Every surviving parameter is prime to $H$ and has all other prime factors at least $z$. Since $n<2X$, its number of prime factors is bounded by any fixed integer greater than $2s/\theta$ for sufficiently large $X$. This proves Proposition <a href="#nv:rough" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nv:rough">44</a> and the positive-sign part of Proposition <a href="#nv:local-witness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nv:local-witness">43</a>. Enlarging $H$ and choosing a reduced CRT lift excludes any additional finite prime set. ◻

</details>

The constants in the size threshold may change when the curve or the support is changed. The exponent $\theta$ and the factor-count bound do not. In particular, a previously chosen twist can be absorbed into the curve before this proposition is applied.

<a id="co:section"></a>
### Coefficient systems and their contraction identities

We construct Fourier coefficients detecting central values and Heegner points, then use arithmetic lower bounds to normalize their least nonzero valuation. The resulting binary tests satisfy the contraction and isolation identities used in Section <a href="#gr:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:section">8</a>. The even lower bound is already available from Section <a href="#cy:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:section">3</a>; the odd detector is constructed without one, and its normalization remains conditional until Proposition <a href="#pw:odd-lower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pw:odd-lower">70</a>.

Throughout, $S$ contains $2$ and the primes dividing the conductor $N$. A filter prescribes local unit squareclasses at $S$, together with the infinite sign, and excludes finitely many further primes. Its indices are $h=\sigma D$, where $D$ is positive and squarefree. All primes adjoined subsequently are odd, good, and outside the current support. Put $F=\mathbb Q(E[2])$. In the present residual range, $F$ is trivial or quadratic. A prime split in $F$ has weight $1$, and a prime inert in $F$, called simple, has weight $1/2$. We write $w(D)$ for the sum of these weights. All valuations satisfy $v(2)=1$.

We use the theta multiplier cubed for weight $3/2$. Local unit characters and their associated Dirichlet characters are related by the usual inverse idele convention. Thus, if a finite Schwartz function satisfies $f(ux)=\alpha(u)f(x)$, its scalar classical character on the lower-right entry is the Dirichlet character associated to $\alpha^{-1}$. This convention removes a possible inverse ambiguity in the formulas below.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Even coefficients and exact comparison of ratios

<a id="stmt-co-even"></a>
<div id="co:even" class="proposition">

<a id="co:recurrence"></a>
<a id="co:exact-ratio"></a>
<a id="co:even-bound"></a>
**Proposition 46** (Even coefficient system). *Fix an even-sign filter and a nonvanishing index $D_0$ in it. There is a holomorphic cuspidal form $g=\sum_{n\geq1}a(n)q^n$ of weight $3/2$, orthogonal to unary theta series, of level supported on a fixed enlargement of $S$, and characters $\eta,\psi$ of $2$-power order such that $\psi/\eta=\chi_{-\sigma}$ and $$\label{co:recurrence}
 a(p^2n)=\bigl(\eta(p)a_p(E)-\psi(p)(-n/p)\bigr)a(n)
              -p\eta(p)^2a(n/p^2)\qquad(p\notin S).$$ Its coefficients are zero off the unit-squareclass filter. After normalization at $D_0$, every squarefree coefficient is rational up to a $2$-power root of unity. For two nonzero squarefree coefficients, $$\label{co:exact-ratio}
 2v\!\left(\frac{a(D)}{a(D_0)}\right)
   =v\!\left(\frac{L_*(\sigma D)}{L_*(\sigma D_0)}\right).$$ Moreover $a(D)\neq0$ exactly when $L(E^{(\sigma D)},1)\neq0$. Consequently any previously established bound $v(L_*(\sigma D))\geq2w(D)+s(\sigma D)-C$ gives $$\label{co:even-bound}
 v(a(D))\geq w(D)+\tfrac12s(\sigma D)-C_g$$ with $C_g$ fixed independently of $D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-even"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-even</summary>

*Proof.* Use the Shimura packet of $E^{(-\sigma)}$. Its positive index $D$ tests the twist by $\chi_{-D}$, which is $E^{(\sigma D)}$. The nonvanishing at $D_0$ selects a globally occurring member admitting that Fourier functional. Take its spherical vector outside $S$ and its holomorphic vector of weight $3/2$ at infinity.

Here is the support-local vector selection. Start with a vector on which the $D_0$ Fourier functional is nonzero. Projection under a sufficiently large compact group of upper unipotents to additive frequency $D_0$ restricts the potentially nonzero unit translates to arbitrarily small neighborhoods of the two solutions of $u^2=1$. Choose a unit character agreeing with the forced action at $-1$. It can have $2$-power order: the image of $-1$ is detected in the $2$-primary quotient of the unit group. The neighborhoods of $1$ and $-1$ then contribute with the same sign, so this projection preserves the nonzero functional. Upper integral translations act trivially, and smoothness gives sufficiently deep lower-unipotent invariance. The vector therefore defines a classical form at a level supported on $S$, allowing arbitrary depth at $2$ and at the bad primes. The unit-squareclass and coprimality sieves commute with good $T(p^2)$, since their conditions are unchanged by multiplication of the index by $p^2$. The classical Hecke formula (Shimura 1973) <a id="pm-cite-66"></a>[[Shimura1973HalfIntegral](#ref-Shimura1973HalfIntegral)] gives <a href="#co:recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:recurrence">[co:recurrence]</a>, with $\eta=\psi\chi_{-\sigma}$.

At the level just chosen, let $V$ be the full space of weight-$3/2$ cusp forms orthogonal to unary theta series, with scalar character $\psi$ and good $T(p^2)$ eigenvalues $\eta(p)a_p(E)$. It contains the constructed form, whose coefficient at $D_0$ is nonzero. For $D/D_0$ a unit square at every support prime, Waldspurger’s comparison (Waldspurger 1981, Corollary 2, pp. 483–484) <a id="pm-cite-67"></a>[[Waldspurger1981](#ref-Waldspurger1981)] gives, for every $f\in V$, $$a_f(D)^2=
 \psi(D/D_0)\frac{L_*(\sigma D)}{L_*(\sigma D_0)}a_f(D_0)^2.$$ Its hypothesis (H1) follows from the nonzero eigenspace (Waldspurger 1981, sec. I.2, Proposition 2) <a id="pm-cite-68"></a>[[Waldspurger1981](#ref-Waldspurger1981)]; the final paragraph of the comparison proof removes (H2) for these local squareclasses. Factoring the displayed quadratic identity in the two coefficient functionals shows that they are proportional on all of $V$, with one scalar independent of $f$. This includes oldvectors and their linear combinations; if the central value at $D$ vanishes, the first functional is simply zero.

Choose an algebraic form $g$ in the filtered subspace of $V$ with $a(D_0)\ne0$. To justify this choice, multiplication by ordinary theta embeds the space into an integral-weight space, and divisibility by theta is specified by algebraic cusp conditions (Ramsey 2006, secs. 3–4) <a id="pm-cite-69"></a>[[Ramsey2006HalfIntegral](#ref-Ramsey2006HalfIntegral)]. The character, good-Hecke and filter conditions are algebraic linear conditions as well. Since evaluation at $D_0$ is nonzero after extension to $\mathbb C$, it is nonzero on some algebraic form.

Let $u$ be an automorphism of $\overline{\mathbb Q}$ acting on the character values by an odd power $e$, and put $\nu=\eta^{(e-1)/2}$. The forms $g^u$ and $g\otimes\nu$ have the same scalar character $\psi^e=\psi\nu^2$ and the same good eigenvalues $\eta(p)^e a_p(E)=\nu(p)^2\eta(p)a_p(E)$; here $\psi/\eta$ is quadratic. They belong to one good-Hecke eigenspace at a common level still supported on $S$. Applying the preceding proportionality in that nonzero space and normalizing at $D_0$ gives $$u(\xi)=\nu(D/D_0)\xi,\qquad \xi=a(D)/a(D_0).$$ Local square roots of $D/D_0$ at the conductor of $\eta$ give a value $z$ of $\eta$ with $\eta(D/D_0)=z^2$. Then $u(z)=z^e$ and $\nu(D/D_0)=z^{e-1}$, so $\xi/z$ is fixed by every algebraic automorphism and is rational. Moreover the same local-square condition gives $\psi(D/D_0)=\eta(D/D_0)=z^2$. The comparison therefore reads $$(\xi/z)^2=\frac{L_*(\sigma D)}{L_*(\sigma D_0)},$$ which proves <a href="#co:exact-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:exact-ratio">[co:exact-ratio]</a> since $v(z)=0$. There is no product over the new prime factors: the unramified squarefree formula includes both local valuations zero and one. No exceptional local representation at $2$ is excluded by the same-local-class comparison. The vanishing assertion and <a href="#co:even-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:even-bound">[co:even-bound]</a> follow. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### A detecting Kummer functional

Fix now the positive odd-sign filter and its original support, including the auxiliary class-group tests needed for arithmetic realization. Choose a fixed negative odd fundamental discriminant $h_*$ with $L(E^{(h_*)},1)\neq0$, whose local squareclasses agree with those of the filter at $2N$. Adjoin its prime factors to $S$, exclude them from the variable indices, and refine the fixed unit tests there as required, all before varying $D$. Choose it so that $|h_*|>4$ and one of its ramified primes excludes the CM field of $E$, if there is one. Thus $$K_D=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{Dh_*})$$ is imaginary, split at $2N$, and has only the units $\{1,-1\}$. Fix a modular parametrization $\varphi$ with $\varphi(\infty)=0$ throughout. With this normalization the cuspidal constants in its Hecke and Atkin–Lehner relations are torsion; see (Manin 1972; Drinfeld 1973) <a id="pm-cite-70"></a>[[Manin1972](#ref-Manin1972), [Drinfeld1973](#ref-Drinfeld1973)]. An arbitrary translation by a rational point is not permitted.

Let $H_D$ be the Hilbert class field of $K_D$. Choose an $\mathcal O_{K_D}$-ideal $\mathfrak n_D$ with $\mathcal O_{K_D}/\mathfrak n_D\simeq\mathbb Z/N\mathbb Z$, fixing one orientation at each level prime, and let $x_D\in X_0(N)(H_D)$ be its conductor-one CM point. The unramified genus character $\chi_D$ attached to $K_D(\sqrt{h_*})/K_D$ satisfies $\chi_D([\mathfrak a])=\chi_{h_*}(\operatorname{Norm}\mathfrak a)$ on ideals prime to $Dh_*$. Define the unaveraged character sum $$P_D=\sum_{[\mathfrak a]\in\operatorname{Pic}(\mathcal O_{K_D})}
       \chi_D([\mathfrak a])\varphi(x_D^{[\mathfrak a]}).$$ These conductor-one genus sums over the varying fields $K_D$ are distinct from the conductor-$|h|$ ring-class sums over a fixed field $K$ defined in Section <a href="#rl:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rl:section">4</a>. The point $P_D$ lies in $E(\mathbb Q(\sqrt D,\sqrt{h_*}))$, with the quadratic interpretation when $D=1$. Its genus-character component over $K_D$ is the sum of the $D$- and $h_*$-twist components over $\mathbb Q$. The latter has rank zero because $L(E^{(h_*)},1)\ne0$. Thus the free part of $P_D$ lies in the $D$-twist component, a line when $E^{(D)}$ has a simple zero. We retain the unaveraged sum in the theta comparison; a fixed integer $L$ kills bounded-degree torsion and makes its comparison with a rational twist point integral. At a simple zero write $j(P_D)$ for its index valuation in the free rational twist line, allowing the fixed restriction and projection indices. Put $j(P_D)=+\infty$ if the product derivative vanishes.

<a id="stmt-co-kummer"></a>
<div id="co:kummer" class="lemma">

<a id="co:ell-conditions"></a>
**Lemma 47** (Kummer detection, including CM). *Let $C$ act on $T=T_2E$ as complex conjugation, and choose a nonzero integral row $b:T\longrightarrow\mathbb Z_2$ with $bC=b$. For a fixed positive simple-zero witness $D_w$, there are arbitrarily large precisions $M$ and good primes $\ell$ such that $$\label{co:ell-conditions}
 \rho_T(\operatorname{Fr}_\ell)\equiv C\pmod {2^M},\qquad
 (h_*/\ell)=-1,\qquad(D_w/\ell)=1.$$ Evaluation at Frobenius squared defines an integral homomorphism $$\lambda_M:E(\mathbb F_{\ell^2})\longrightarrow\mathbb Z/2^M\mathbb Z,
 \qquad \lambda_M=b\kappa_M,$$ of Frobenius sign $+1$. The valuation of $\lambda_M(\operatorname{red}_\ell(LP_{D_w}))$ is bounded independently of $M$, for a fixed nonzero integer $L$ making the twist comparison integral. Extra fixed finite-field restrictions can be retained when they agree with the chosen complex-conjugation coset, or when a compatible lift has been specified.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-kummer"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-kummer</summary>

*Proof.* For every elliptic curve $A/\mathbb Q$, the rational Tate module $V_2A$ is irreducible. Indeed Faltings’ semisimplicity and endomorphism theorem (Faltings 1983, sec. 1, assertions (a),(b)) <a id="pm-cite-71"></a>[[Faltings1983](#ref-Faltings1983)] would turn a proper summand into a nontrivial idempotent in $\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb Q}(A)\otimes\mathbb Q_2=\mathbb Q_2$. The equality of this endomorphism ring with $\mathbb Z$ includes CM curves: a nonreal CM endomorphism is not defined over $\mathbb Q$. The Tate image contains a nonidentity central scalar, by the homothety theorem (Bogomolov 1981, sec. 2, Theorem 3 and paragraph 7) <a id="pm-cite-72"></a>[[Bogomolov1981Points](#ref-Bogomolov1981Points)].

Let $Q\in A(\mathbb Q)$ be nontorsion and let $c_Q$ be its division cocycle. On the kernel of the Tate action its image is a closed $\mathbb Z_2$-submodule of $T_2A$, stable under the full Galois image. If its rational span were zero, the rational Kummer class would factor through the Tate image. A central scalar $z\neq1$ annihilates its first cohomology: for a cocycle $c$ the equality of $c(zg)$ and $c(gz)$ gives $(z-1)c(g)=(g-1)c(z)$, so $c$ is a coboundary over $\mathbb Q_2$. This contradicts Kummer injectivity and the finite generation of $A(\mathbb Q)$. Irreducibility consequently makes the translation image contain $2^aT_2A$ for some $a$. Intersecting with the kernel of additional finite data retains a full lattice.

Apply this to the rational twist point supplied by $LP_{D_w}$ on $A=E^{(D_w)}$. Since $D_w>0$, conjugation acts on its Tate module as $C$. Modify a conjugation lift by an element of the simultaneous kernel whose translation is $v$. The square of the modified element has translation $(1+C)v$, and $b(1+C)v=2b(v)$. The full-lattice property allows a choice with a fixed finite nonzero valuation. Conjugation acts trivially on $\mathbb Q(\sqrt{D_w})$ and nontrivially on $\mathbb Q(\sqrt{h_*})$. Chebotarev in each finite torsion, division, and character extension gives <a href="#co:ell-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:ell-conditions">[co:ell-conditions]</a> and the chosen finite nonzero detection.

For good $\ell$, Lang’s theorem and finite-field Kummer theory give $$E(\mathbb F_{\ell^2})/2^M E(\mathbb F_{\ell^2})
 \simeq T/\bigl(2^MT+(\operatorname{Fr}_\ell^2-1)T\bigr)=T/2^MT.$$ This is evaluation of a cocycle at arithmetic Frobenius squared. Its equivariance and $bC=b$ prove the sign assertion. Reduction of the division cocycle gives precisely the detection already arranged. Finally $\det C=-1$ implies $\ell\equiv-1\pmod {2^M}$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### The finite Schwartz function and its transformation

<a id="stmt-co-theta-bridge"></a>
<div id="co:theta-bridge" class="lemma">

<a id="co:weighted-comparison"></a>
**Lemma 48** (Explicit weighted theta construction). *For $\ell$ as in Lemma <a href="#co:kummer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:kummer">47</a>, the reduced modular parametrization followed by $\lambda_M$ admits an integral weighted ternary theta lift. After extraction of the coefficients at multiples of $|h_*|$ it gives a classical holomorphic weight-$3/2$ form $g_M=\sum a_M(n)q^n$, of level supported on $S\cup\{\ell\}$, with <a href="#co:recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:recurrence">[co:recurrence]</a> modulo $2^M$. The associated characters have $2$-power order and quadratic ratio. For squarefree admissible $D$, $$\label{co:weighted-comparison}
 a_M(D)=c_0\,2^{\omega(N)}\zeta_M(D)
       \lambda_M\bigl(\operatorname{red}_\ell(LP_D)\bigr)
                     \pmod {2^M},$$ where $\zeta_M(D)$ is a $2$-power root of unity and $c_0,L$ are fixed integers independent of $D,M,\ell$. The extra index restriction at $\ell$ is $(D/\ell)=1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-theta-bridge"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-theta-bridge</summary>

*Proof.* Let $B$ be ramified at $\ell$ and infinity, $R$ an Eichler order of level $N$, and $V=B^0$ with $Q(x)=-x^2=\operatorname{Nrd}(x)$. The root characters at the fixed level primes below are chosen once. Let $\mathcal O$ be the valuation ring at a place above $2$ of the field generated by their values. The other local weights are quadratic, so this field is independent of $M$ and $\ell$. In this construction integrality means membership in $\mathcal O$, and congruences mean congruences modulo $2^M\mathcal O$.

*The supersingular coefficient function.* The supersingular pairs on $X_0(N)$ are the finite set $$X=B^\times\backslash\widehat B^\times/\widehat R^\times.$$ This description permits every $N$ prime to $\ell$, including arbitrary depth at $2$; see (Jetchev and Kane 2011, secs. 1.1–1.2,2.4–2.6) <a id="pm-cite-73"></a>[[JetchevKane2010](#ref-JetchevKane2010)]. For $z\in X$ put $R_z=B\cap z\widehat Rz^{-1}$. Choose $L$ killing the finite subgroup generated by the images of the cuspidal constants in the Atkin–Lehner relations, and also all the bounded-degree torsion used below. Then $$P_M(z)=\lambda_M(L\varphi_\ell(z)),$$ where $\varphi_\ell$ is the specialization of $\varphi$ to the good-reduction special fiber. This function has Brandt eigenvalues $a_p(E)$ away from $N\ell$, Atkin–Lehner eigenvalue $w_{p^e}$ at $p^e\Vert N$, and eigenvalue $+1$ under the right $\ell$-uniformizer. These identities are modulo $2^M$. Choose any integer lifts of this finite list of values.

*The local Schwartz weights.* We now define the local functions, rather than appealing to an unspecified weighted theta theorem. Off $2Nh_*\ell$ take the characteristic function of $R_p^0$. At $p^e\Vert N$, use a model $$R_p=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}
       :a,b,d\in\mathbb Z_p, c\in p^e\mathbb Z_p\right\}.$$ Require the split fundamental unit squareclass and an integral embedding. Its action on the cyclic level quotient selects one root $r\in\mathbb Z_p^\times$ of $x^2$. Give $x$ weight $\alpha_p(r)$, where $\alpha_p$ is a finite unit character of $2$-power order with <a id="co:root-parity"></a>$$\label{co:root-parity}
 \alpha_p(-1)=w_{p^e}\chi_{h_*}(p)^e.$$ Such a character exists for either prescribed sign. The root is a locally constant function to any fixed character precision: the two idempotents of the split integral quadratic algebra distinguish the two summands of the cyclic quotient. Unit scaling $x\mapsto ux$ multiplies this weight by $\alpha_p(u)$, and order-unit conjugation preserves it.

At $2$ require $(1+x)/2\in R_2$ and $x^2\in\mathbb Z_2^{\times2}$. If $2\mid N$, the preceding selected-root rule is imposed using $e_x=(1+x)/2$. Its two eigenvalues $(1+r)/2,(1-r)/2$ differ by the odd unit $r$; hence the two idempotents remain distinct modulo $2$. This verifies the construction at every exponent of $2$ in $N$. Scaling by an odd $u$ preserves integrality, since $(1+ux)/2=u(1+x)/2+(1-u)/2$. This is an invertible affine change of the operator $e_x$, so it preserves its two summands individually. The selected summand is selected by the cyclic level quotient, not by declaring its eigenvalue to be $0$ or $1$ modulo $2$. On that same summand $x$ acts as $r$ and $ux$ acts as $ur$. Even when $u\equiv-1\pmod4$ interchanges the two eigenvalue parities, the root weight therefore changes by exactly $\alpha_2(u)$. If $2\nmid N$ there is no root weight at $2$.

For $p\mid h_*$ require $x\in M_2(\mathbb Z_p)^0$ and $v_p(Q(x))=1$. Its reduction is a nonzero nilpotent. Fix $X_0=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ and define $$\epsilon_p(gX_0g^{-1})=\left(\frac{\det g}{p}\right).$$ The centralizer consists of $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\0&a\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, with determinant $a^2$, so this is well-defined on the entire nonzero nilpotent orbit. It obeys $$\epsilon_p(uyu^{-1})=(\det u/p)\epsilon_p(y),\qquad
 \epsilon_p(ay)=(a/p)\epsilon_p(y).$$ The second equality follows by conjugating $X_0$ by $\operatorname{diag}(a,1)$. Set $f_p(x)=\epsilon_p(\bar x)$ on the specified support. Thus the right conjugation character is precisely the local component of $\chi_{h_*}\circ\operatorname{Nrd}$.

At $\ell$, let $\mathfrak P$ be the maximal ideal of the maximal order. Its residue field is $\mathbb F_{\ell^2}$, and the reduction of an integral trace-zero element lies on its one-dimensional trace-zero line. Fix a nonzero basis vector $\beta$ on that line and put $$f_\ell(x)=\left(\frac{t}{\ell}\right)
   \quad\text{if }\bar x=t\beta\neq0,
 \qquad f_\ell(x)=0\quad\text{otherwise}.$$ Order units act trivially by conjugation on the residue field. A uniformizer acts by its nontrivial automorphism and sends $\beta$ to $-\beta$. Therefore its multiplier is $-1$, since $\ell\equiv-1\pmod4$; scaling has character $(u/\ell)$. Moreover $\beta^2$ is a nonsquare and $-1$ is a nonsquare modulo $\ell$, so $Q(x)=-t^2\beta^2$ is a nonzero square. Because $(|h_*|/\ell)=1$, the extracted indices automatically satisfy $(D/\ell)=1$. No average over $\mathbb F_\ell^\times$ is used.

<a id="co:order-equivariance"></a>
All these functions have compact support and are locally constant. Let $f=\prod f_p$, incorporating any additional fixed unit-squareclass conditions by their characteristic functions. Those conditions are stable under unit squares and change none of the scaling characters. For $u\in\widehat R^\times$, $$\label{co:order-equivariance}
 f(u^{-1}xu)=\chi_{h_*}(\operatorname{Nrd}u)^{-1}f(x).$$ The product scaling character is even. Indeed its value at $-1$ is $$\left(\prod_{p^e\Vert N}w_{p^e}\chi_{h_*}(p)^e\right)
       \chi_{h_*}(-1)(-1)=1;$$ the parenthesized factor is the even functional sign of $E^{(h_*)}$. Denote its associated classical character by $\eta_B$. In the inverse-idele convention fixed above, if $\alpha$ is this finite scaling character, then $\eta_B(d)=\alpha(d)$ for positive integers $d$ prime to the support: the inverse idele character becomes the product of the finite unit values at the lower-right entry.

*The scalar transformation law.* Here are all the Weil-representation checks needed for scalar modularity. For an additive character of conductor $\mathbb Z_p$, write $n(b)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&b\\0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, $m(u)=\operatorname{diag}(u,u^{-1})$, and $n^-(c)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\c&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)$. Use a Weyl lift $w$, its compatible self-dual Fourier transform, and the Weil factors $\gamma_p(u),\gamma_p$ for this additive character. The defining formulas are $$\omega(n(b))f_p(x)=\mathbf e_p(bQ(x))f_p(x),\quad
 \omega(m(u))f_p(x)=\gamma_p(u)|u|_p^{3/2}f_p(ux),\quad
 \omega(w)f_p=\gamma_p\widehat f_p.$$ They follow from the Weil construction and Poisson summation (Weil 1964, I, no. 13) <a id="pm-cite-74"></a>[[Weil1964](#ref-Weil1964)]. Integral $b$ fixes $f_p$, since $Q$ is integral on its support. If $f_p$ is invariant under translations by a lattice $L_p'$, its Fourier transform is supported on the dual lattice $(L_p')^\vee$. Choose $A_p$ so that $p^{A_p}Q((L_p')^\vee)\subset\mathbb Z_p$. Then $n(p^{A_p}b)$ fixes $\widehat f_p$, and conjugating by $w$ proves that every $n^-(p^{A_p}b)$ fixes $f_p$. This argument works without change at $2$ and for all finite character depths.

Relative to the ordinary theta multiplier cubed, the diagonal Weil factor is $(\det Q,u)_p$. Indeed diagonalizing $Q$ and multiplying the one-dimensional Weil-index identities (Weil 1964, II, nos. 25, 28) <a id="pm-cite-75"></a>[[Weil1964](#ref-Weil1964)] gives the Hilbert symbol of the product of its three coefficients. In a presentation $B=(-a,-b)$ that product is the determinant of $aX^2+bY^2+abZ^2$, namely $(ab)^2$. Thus this additional factor is $1$. The remaining diagonal action is exactly the scaling character already computed. Increasing $A_2$ to the depth needed for the standard metaplectic splitting, the factorization $$\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}
    =n^-(c/a)m(a)n(b/a),\qquad c\in p^{A_p}\mathbb Z_p, a\in\mathbb Z_p^\times,$$ proves scalar transformation on the required local $\Gamma_0$. Conjugation by $z$ commutes with these Weil actions and preserves $Q$. Consequently the same level and scalar character work for every summand. The Gaussian at infinity gives holomorphic weight $3/2$; positive definiteness gives nonnegative exponents at every cusp.

<a id="co:theta-sum"></a>
Choose an integer $c_0$ divisible by every possible $|R_z^\times|$. Such a uniform choice exists because these unit groups embed in $\operatorname{GL}_4(\mathbb Z)$ and their orders are bounded. The theta series is $$\label{co:theta-sum}
 H_M(n)=c_0\sum_{z\in X}
  \frac{P_M(z)\chi_{h_*}(\operatorname{Nrd}z)}{|R_z^\times|}
  \sum_{\substack{x\in B^0\\Q(x)=n}} f(z^{-1}xz).$$ Equation <a href="#co:order-equivariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:order-equivariance">[co:order-equivariance]</a> proves independence of right representatives; rational reduced norms are positive, so the norm character is trivial on the left rational action. The preceding Weil calculation proves modularity (Weil 1964, III, Theorem 6) <a id="pm-cite-76"></a>[[Weil1964](#ref-Weil1964)], and the choice of $c_0$ proves integral Fourier coefficients. More precisely, each $c_0/|R_z^\times|$ is an integer, so changing a lift of $P_M(z)$ by $2^M$ changes every coefficient by an element of $2^M\mathcal O$. No precision is lost to a stabilizer denominator. The chosen integer lifts need satisfy no characteristic-zero eigenrelation.

*The good-prime Hecke relation.* For completeness, the good-prime relation can be checked in a matrix order. Neighbors correspond to the lines in $\mathbb F_p^2$. A nonzero primitive nilpotent reduction has exactly one invariant line. For an integral element of nonzero reduction and norm $n$, the number of invariant lines is $1+(-n/p)$; zero reduction contributes the additional $p$ lines. If a primitive $y$ has $p^2\mid Q(y)$, choose a basis in which its reduction is $X_0$. Its lower-left entry is divisible by $p^2$, and exactly the neighbor for that invariant line makes $y/p$ integral. These counts partition the three terms of the theta Hecke operator. Scaling by $p$ at the support contributes $\eta_B(p)$, and the reduced norm of a Brandt neighbor contributes $\chi_{h_*}(p)$. Thus, with $A(n)=H_M(n)$, $$A(p^2n)+\eta_B(p)(-n/p)A(n)+p\eta_B(p)^2A(n/p^2)
       =\eta_B(p)\chi_{h_*}(p)a_p(E)A(n)\pmod {2^M}.$$ The orbit-stabilizer weights in <a href="#co:theta-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:theta-sum">[co:theta-sum]</a> are exactly the weights in this neighbor count. Extracting $a_M(n)=H_M(|h_*|n)$ gives $\eta=\eta_B\chi_{h_*}$ and $\psi=\eta_B(|h_*|/\cdot)$, proving <a href="#co:recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:recurrence">[co:recurrence]</a>. Fixed-support coefficient sieves are exact zero-or-retain operations; they preserve coefficient integrality and commute with this relation.

*The weighted coefficient comparison.* It remains to prove the weighted comparison; modularity alone does not supply it. At $n=|h_*|D$ the counted elements are precisely the optimal embeddings $\mathcal O_{K_D}\hookrightarrow R_z$, sending $\sqrt{Dh_*}$ to $x$. The condition at $2$ is exactly the condition on the integral generator $(1+\sqrt{Dh_*})/2$. At a level prime there are two orientations. Indeed a stable lattice splits into its two integral idempotent summands, and a cyclic quotient of order $p^e$ lies wholly in one summand. At $\ell$ there are two orientations, the two residue-field embeddings. At all remaining primes there is a single local optimal-embedding orbit.

Fix a rational embedding $x_0$ and an initial orientation tuple $z_0$. The orbits with these orientations are indexed by $\operatorname{Pic}(\mathcal O_{K_D})$, with representatives $az_0$ for $a\in\widehat K_D^\times$. The equality $$(az_0)^{-1}x_0(az_0)=z_0^{-1}x_0z_0$$ shows that the Schwartz weight is constant along that orbit. Its remaining relative weight is $\chi_{h_*}(\operatorname{Norm}_{K_D/\mathbb Q}a)$, exactly the unramified genus character. The reduction correspondence commutes with this ideal-class action and remembers the cyclic level subgroup (Jetchev and Kane 2011, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-77"></a>[[JetchevKane2010](#ref-JetchevKane2010)]; a Galois-equivariant description of the lift, class action and reduction is given in (Cornut and Jetchev 2013, sec. 3.2, §§4.6, 4.9–4.10 and Corollary 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-78"></a>[[CornutJetchev2013](#ref-CornutJetchev2013)]. We use these correspondences only for their geometry; the weight identity has just been computed, and the multiplicity is computed next.

An embedding stabilizer is $\mathcal O_{K_D}^\times=\{\pm1\}$. An orbit in $R_z$ therefore contains $|R_z^\times|/2$ elements, giving the factor $1/2$ in <a href="#co:theta-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:theta-sum">[co:theta-sum]</a>. At $p^e\Vert N$, reversing orientation multiplies its Schwartz factor by $\alpha_p(-1)=w_{p^e}\chi_{h_*}(p)^e$, and its norm-weighted parametrization value by the same sign. At $\ell$ both multipliers are $-1$: one is the residue-line calculation and the other is $\chi_{h_*}(\ell)$ times the Frobenius eigenvalue $+1$. Every one of the $2^{\omega(N)+1}$ orientation tuples consequently has the same contribution. The initial tuple has a $2$-power unit phase. Combining this count with its factor $1/2$ gives exactly <a href="#co:weighted-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:weighted-comparison">[co:weighted-comparison]</a>. There is no division by a class number, and none of its rational multipliers grows with $\ell$ or the number of factors of $D$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-co-odd"></a>
<div id="co:odd" class="proposition">

**Proposition 49** (Odd coefficient detection). *The forms in Lemma <a href="#co:theta-bridge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:theta-bridge">48</a>, after a fixed adjustment of precision, satisfy $$v(a_M(D))\geq\min\{M,j(P_D)-C\},\qquad
 v(a_M(D))<M\ \Longrightarrow\ j(P_D)\leq v(a_M(D))+C.$$ A visible coefficient detects $a(E^{(D)})=1$, and a fixed positive witness has bounded coefficient valuation as $M\to\infty$. All constants are independent of $M,\ell,D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-odd"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-odd</summary>

*Proof.* Gross–Zagier identifies the height of the genus sum with the product derivative (Gross and Zagier 1986) <a id="pm-cite-79"></a>[[GrossZagier1986](#ref-GrossZagier1986)]. Since $L(E^{(h_*)},1)\neq0$, vanishing of the derivative makes $P_D$ torsion, and a nontorsion $P_D$ is equivalent to a simple zero of $E^{(D)}$. The genus sum lies in a biquadratic field, so a fixed integer kills all possible torsion: reduce prime-to-residue-characteristic torsion at two fixed good primes of different residue characteristic; the residue degrees are at most four. Include that integer in $L$. On the free twist line, divisibility by $2^{j(P_D)}$ remains divisibility after the integral maps of reduction and $\lambda_M$. Restriction and the two quadratic projections have composite multiplication by $2$, so their integral indices have a fixed bound. Equation <a href="#co:weighted-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:weighted-comparison">[co:weighted-comparison]</a> therefore proves both valuation assertions, with fixed losses only. Lemma <a href="#co:kummer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:kummer">47</a> gives the witness. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Minimum depth and integral coefficient operators

We now pass from detection to normalization. For the even source, the cyclotomic lower bound gives <a href="#co:even-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:even-bound">[co:even-bound]</a> at every nonzero squarefree coefficient. For the odd source, assume for now that every simple-zero parameter satisfies $$j(P_D)\geq w(D)+s(D)/2-C_*.$$ Proposition <a href="#pw:odd-lower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pw:odd-lower">70</a> will prove this using only the even construction. Proposition <a href="#co:odd" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:odd">49</a> then gives $$v(a_M(D))\geq\min\{M,w(D)+s(D)/2-C'\}$$ at simple zeros, where $s(D)$ is defined. At every other odd-sign parameter the product derivative vanishes and $a_M(D)=0$ modulo $2^M$. In particular the coarser bound $v(a_M(D))\geq\min\{M,w(D)-C'\}$ holds at every admissible $D$. The remaining coefficient calculus is conditional on these lower bounds; it does not establish them.

For the operator and graph arguments, at each actual odd stage we write $S$ for the original fixed support together with the primes of $h_*$ and the current reduction prime $\ell$. The latter is stationary during that stage and is never an element of a newly adjoined set $U$. Thus every statement below about ramification outside $S\cup U$ includes $\ell$. This notation does not change the original fixed data controlling the constants, or choose a new minimum when a vertex is omitted.

<a id="stmt-co-minimum"></a>
<div id="co:minimum" class="lemma">

**Lemma 50** (Stabilized minimum depth). *Assume the uniform arithmetic lower bounds just stated. Each coefficient source defines a binary symbol <a id="co:symbol"></a>$$\label{co:symbol}
 A(D)=\begin{cases}
 1,&v(a(D))=\delta+w(D),\\
 0,&v(a(D))>\delta+w(D)\text{ or }D\text{ is off the filter},
 \end{cases}$$ with a fixed minimum normalized depth $\delta$ and at least one bounded-weight witness. For the odd source $a=a_M$ at an actual stage: the numerical minimum and its divisibility statements stabilize at every fixed finite weight bound. The finite symbol tables on arithmetic labels will be stabilized separately in Section <a href="#pw:odd-stages" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pw:odd-stages">9.5</a>. At a unit symbol, $$v(L_*(\sigma D))\leq2w(D)+O(1)
 \quad\text{or}\quad j(P_D)\leq w(D)+O(1),$$ respectively, and the analytic order is respectively zero or one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-minimum"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-minimum</summary>

*Proof.* For the even source normalize $a(D_0)=1$; the phase-removed squarefree coefficients are rational by Proposition <a href="#co:even" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:even">46</a>.
