For the odd source, <a href="#co:weighted-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:weighted-comparison">[co:weighted-comparison]</a> makes every visible squarefree coefficient an integer times a root of unity modulo $2^M$. Their finite valuations are therefore integers in both cases, not arbitrary elements of a ramified coefficient field’s value group. The fractional part of $w(D)$ is fixed on the filter, because the parity of the number of simple primes is its fixed quadratic discriminant character. The lower bounds and a nonzero witness therefore give a minimum for the even source.

For the odd source choose a nonprincipal ultrafilter on the increasing precisions. Minimize $v(a_M(D_M))-w(D_M)$ over sequences with bounded weight and visible coefficient on an ultrafilter-large set. The uniform lower bound, discrete values, and fixed witness give a finite attained minimum. At each fixed weight cutoff $W$, failure of its divisibility on an ultrafilter-large set would allow a choice of a violating $D_M$ with $w(D_M)\leq W$, contrary to the minimum. Hence every prescribed finite list of these divisibilities holds at a sufficiently advanced actual stage. If larger weights arise in a coefficient-integrality check, the coarse bound $\min\{M,w(D)-C\}$ already pays any fixed normalization once both $M$ and $w(D)$ exceed its fixed threshold. This also explains why no infinite-precision modular lift is asserted or needed. The last conclusions follow from the two coefficient comparisons. ◻

</details>

<a id="co:BU"></a>
Write $U_p\sum c(n)q^n=\sum c(pn)q^n$ and $V_p\sum c(n)q^n=\sum c(n)q^{pn}$. For a squarefree set $U$ put $$\label{co:BU}
 B_U=2^{-\lceil\delta+w(U)\rceil}
       \prod_{p\in U}(U_p-\eta(p)V_p)g,\qquad
 e_U=\lceil\delta+w(U)\rceil-\delta-w(U).$$ We call $U$ matching if $e_U=0$ and opposite if $e_U=1/2$. These operators introduce each new prime to level exponent one: $V_p$ changes the character by $(p/\cdot)$ and raises the level by $p$, while $U_p=U_{p^2}V_p$ on that raised level. Thus the new character is $\psi_U=\psi\prod_{p\in U}(p/\cdot)$.

<a id="stmt-co-operators"></a>
<div id="co:operators" class="lemma">

<a id="co:BU-bound"></a>
<a id="co:cp"></a>
**Lemma 51** (Coefficient bounds and unary removal). *For the even source let $U$ be arbitrary. For the odd source, fix an integer $R\ge1$ and a weight bound $W$; all assertions below are made at a sufficiently advanced actual stage, simultaneously for every $U$ with $w(U)\le W$ and every Fourier index. In these respective settings the coefficients of $B_U$ are integral. Write uniquely $n=Jrm^2$, with $J\subseteq U$, $r$ squarefree, and $(r,U)=1$. The coefficient vanishes unless $D=(U/J)r$ belongs to the filter, and for the even source otherwise $$\label{co:BU-bound}
 v(B_U[n])\geq w(r)+\tfrac12\#\{p\in J:p\text{ simple}\}-e_U.$$ At that stage the odd source satisfies this inequality for every $U$ with $w(U)\le W$ and every $n$, with its right side replaced by its minimum with $R$. In particular the untruncated bound holds on any prescribed bounded range of squarefree weights, after choosing $R$ and the stage. For a split $p\in J$ the difference factor, after its unit phase and one factor of $2$ are removed, has residue $$\label{co:cp}
 c_p(D)=\frac{a_p(E)-(\psi/\eta)(p)(-D/p)-1}{2}\pmod2$$ at every square shell. For a simple $p$ this formula holds on the first shell $p\nmid m$.*

<a id="co:HU"></a>
*Choose $i\in\{0,1\}$ such that $\eta\chi_{-4}^i$ is odd, and set $$\Theta_J=\sum_{\substack{m>0\\(m,S)=1}}
              \eta(m)\chi_{-4}(m)^i m q^{Jm^2}.$$ For matching $U$ the modular form $$\label{co:HU}
 H_U=\frac12\left(B_U-
       \sum_{\substack{J\subseteq U\\J\text{ split only}}}
                 B_U[J]\Theta_J\right)$$ has integral coefficients, with the same stage and weight restriction in the odd case. For opposite $U$ put $H_U=B_U$. The new primes in the level of $\Theta_J$ have exponent one, and its character is $\eta\chi_{-4}^i(-J/\cdot)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-operators"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-operators</summary>

*Proof.* After removing the powers of $\eta(p)$, the square-index multipliers in <a href="#co:recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:recurrence">[co:recurrence]</a> are $$b_0=1,\quad b_1=a_p-\kappa,\quad
 b_{s+1}=a_pb_s-pb_{s-1},\qquad
 \kappa=(\psi/\eta)(p)(-D/p).$$ If $p\mid D$, then $\kappa=0$ and $b_s$ alternates between $1$ and $0$ modulo $2$, because $a_p$ is even. Thus the difference from $p\in U/J$ is odd. If $p\nmid D$, all $b_s$ are odd and the difference from $p\in J$ is even. Subtracting the normalization in <a href="#co:BU" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:BU">[co:BU]</a> gives exactly $$\delta+w((U/J)r)+\#J-\lceil\delta+w(U)\rceil
 =w(r)+\tfrac12\#\{p\in J:p\text{ simple}\}-e_U.$$ This is an integer on the allowed filter. Its only potential negative value before imposing parity is $-1/2$, so it is nonnegative.

For the odd source this calculation first applies on a fixed bounded range of squarefree weights, with enough precision to pay the fixed normalizing power of $2$. To obtain the global truncated assertion, fix $R$ and a bound $W$ for $w(U)$. The normalizing exponents $\lceil\delta+w(U)\rceil$ are uniformly bounded. Above a sufficiently large weight threshold, the coarse bound $\min\{M,w(D)-C\}$ from Lemma <a href="#co:minimum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:minimum">50</a> pays that normalizing power and at least $R$ further factors of $2$, once $M$ is large enough. Below the threshold use the stabilized minimum and increase the precision again if needed. The recurrence has integral multipliers, so errors modulo $2^M$ remain divisible by $2^M$ at every square shell before normalization. Thus at the chosen stage the truncated bound holds at every coefficient for every $U$ with $w(U)\le W$, giving integrality without asserting an unbounded valuation estimate for a fixed finite-precision lift.

For split $p$, rationality of $E[2]$ over $\mathbb F_p$ gives $a_p\equiv p+1\pmod4$. With $\Delta_s=b_{s+1}-b_s$, every $\Delta_s$ is even and $\Delta_{s+1}=a_p\Delta_s-p\Delta_{s-1}$, so $\Delta_{s+1}\equiv\Delta_{s-1}\pmod4$. Direct calculation gives $$\Delta_1-\Delta_0
  =a_p^2-a_p\kappa-2a_p+2\kappa+1-p\equiv0\pmod4.$$ Consequently every $\Delta_s/2$ has the residue in <a href="#co:cp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:cp">[co:cp]</a>. At a simple prime only $\Delta_0$ is used.

In matching parity the right side of <a href="#co:BU-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:BU-bound">[co:BU-bound]</a> can vanish only when $r=1$ and $J$ is split only. The same recurrence, retaining the character phases, gives $B_U[Jm^2]\equiv B_U[J]\eta(m)\pmod {2}$. The corresponding coefficient of $\Theta_J$ has exactly that residue, and different squarefree $J$ have disjoint supports. The numerator in <a href="#co:HU" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:HU">[co:HU]</a> is therefore divisible by the integer $2$, not just by the maximal ideal of a ramified character field. The ordinary odd-character unary theta transformation and dilation by $J$ give the asserted character and level. For the odd source these calculations use the same sufficiently advanced stage, uniformly for $w(U)\le W$, with precision paying the fixed divisions as in Lemma <a href="#co:minimum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:minimum">50</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Trace operators and omission of a whole rational prime

The next step turns changing a Frobenius label into a difference of coefficient tests. Fix a coefficient valuation ring $\mathcal O$, enlarging its field if needed for the finite eigensystem span below, and write $\mathfrak m$ for its maximal ideal. Divisibility by $2^a$ means divisibility in $2^a\mathcal O$; reduced traces and expansions mean coefficientwise reduction of Fourier expansions modulo $\mathfrak m$. These are different operations; no saturation of the integral module below is assumed. The normalized squarefree tests are binary because their phase-removed values are rational $2$-adic integers (integer congruences at an odd stage), and every $2$-power root of unity reduces to $1$.

Multiply the form under consideration by $\theta=1+2\sum_{m>0}q^{m^2}$. This gives a weight-two form with the same reduced expansion. Work in the finite-dimensional sum of its characteristic-zero good Hecke eigensystems, retaining old multiplicity spaces and its diamond transforms. The associated two-dimensional representations are the usual modular Galois representations for cusp forms (Deligne 1971) <a id="pm-cite-80"></a>[[Deligne1969](#ref-Deligne1969)], and the sums of the two defining characters for Eisenstein series. Their commuting trace and determinant operators are denoted by $t(u),d(u)$. Arithmetic Frobenius at a good prime $q$ has trace $T_q$ and determinant $q\langle q\rangle$. Let $\mathcal M$ be the $\mathcal O$-module generated by this weight-two vector and the undivided terms $\theta B_U[J]\Theta_J$, together with all their good-Hecke and diamond translates.

<a id="stmt-co-traces"></a>
<div id="co:traces" class="lemma">

<a id="co:trace-identities"></a>
**Lemma 52** (Integral traces). *The module $\mathcal M$ is an integral Fourier lattice preserved by the trace and determinant operators. On the coefficientwise reduced trace orbit of the indicated vector, $$\label{co:trace-identities}
 d(u)t(v)=t(v),\qquad
 t(u)t(v)=t(uv)+t(uv^{-1}),\qquad t(1)=0.$$ Every coefficient test of a finite product of traces can be performed using distinct fresh primes avoiding the tested index. In particular testing $t(u_1)\cdots t(u_a)$ at $n$ becomes testing the original reduced coefficient at $nq_1\cdots q_a$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-traces"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-traces</summary>

*Proof.* We first check integrality of the generators. Diamonds act by scalars on $B_U$ and on each unary term. For <a href="#co:HU" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:HU">[co:HU]</a>, the only apparent denominator is removed by $$\langle u\rangle H_U=\psi_U(u)H_U+
 \frac12\sum_J B_U[J]
 \bigl(\psi_U(u)-\eta(u)\chi_{-4}(u)^i(-J/u)\bigr)\Theta_J;$$ the difference of the two characters is zero or twice a unit. The same formula applies after multiplication by theta. Good Hecke operators commute with diamonds, and their coefficient formula preserves integrality. Thus all generators of $\mathcal M$ are integral. Finitely many Fourier coefficients inject the ambient finite-dimensional space into a finite-dimensional coefficient space, so $\mathcal M$ embeds in $\mathcal O^r$ for some finite $r$. It is consequently a finite, closed $\mathcal O$-module. Continuity and simultaneous Chebotarev approximation on the finitely many eigensystems approximate $t(u)$ and $d(u)$ by $T_q$ and $q\langle q\rangle$ at every prescribed precision. These operators preserve $\mathcal M$, so their limits do as well. They act by scalars on each good eigensystem and by the identity on its old multiplicity space, which retains the asserted commutation with level operators.

The formula $(T_qf)[n]=f[qn]+q(\langle q\rangle f)[n/q]$ has no second term when $q\nmid n$. Successive distinct fresh primes therefore give the coefficient assertion. On a single character system, determinants reduce to $1$ because all characters have $2$-power order and the cyclotomic character is odd. On a subtracted vector the displayed diamond formula leaves unary corrections. A fresh trace kills each correction modulo the maximal ideal: the tested index $nq$ cannot have its prescribed squarefree part $J$ when $q\nmid nJ$. This continues to hold after further fresh tests. Thus $d(u)t(v)=t(v)$ on the reduced trace orbit. Apply the matrix identity $$\operatorname{tr}(A)\operatorname{tr}(B)
  =\operatorname{tr}(AB)+\det(B)\operatorname{tr}(AB^{-1})$$ to each characteristic-zero system and use that determinant identity. Finally $t(1)=2$ before reduction. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-co-inertia"></a>
<div id="co:inertia" class="lemma">

<a id="co:inertia-identity"></a>
**Lemma 53** (The level-prime inertia identity). *For $p\in U$, choose inertia $\tau_p$ on which the new ramified quadratic character is $-1$, and an arithmetic Frobenius lift $\phi_p$ in the same decomposition group. In characteristic zero, $$\label{co:inertia-identity}
 U_p^{(2)}\bigl(t(g\tau_p\phi_p)-t(g\phi_p)\bigr)
   =d(\phi_p)\bigl(t(g\tau_p)-t(g)\bigr).$$ Here $U_p^{(2)}$ extracts $p$-multiples in weight two. The identity commutes with further good traces.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-inertia"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-inertia</summary>

*Proof.* A constituent old from a level omitting $p$ has trivial inertia, so both differences are zero, whatever $U_p^{(2)}$ does on its old multiplicity space. Every other constituent has conductor exponent one and primitive ramified quadratic determinant. The conductor formula excludes monodromy and identifies it as principal series with one unramified and one ramified character. Local compatibility (Carayol 1986, Theorem (A)) <a id="pm-cite-81"></a>[[Carayol1986](#ref-Carayol1986)], after converting its geometric Frobenius convention to the arithmetic convention fixed above, identifies its $U_p^{(2)}$ eigenvalue with the Frobenius eigenvalue $\lambda$ on the unramified line. Write $$\rho(\tau_p)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \rho(\phi_p)=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&0\\0&\mu\end{pmatrix}.$$ If $z$ is the lower-right entry of $\rho(g)$, the two trace differences are $-2z\mu$ and $-2z$. Both sides of <a href="#co:inertia-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:inertia-identity">[co:inertia-identity]</a> equal $-2z\lambda\mu$. The Eisenstein character pair has the same calculation. A unary term is old if $p\notin J$, and has precisely this ramified quadratic character if $p\in J$. This exhausts the constituents. ◻

</details>

For the interface below a *label* is the restriction of a Frobenius element to an elementary $2$-extension unramified outside the indicated support. Its coordinates are quadratic characters. When $F$ is quadratic, choosing one of the two places at a split prime specifies an orientation; changing its ramification bit means multiplying by the image of inertia at that place. Product labels are the restrictions of products of Frobenius elements, so their quadratic coordinates add. Simple primes use rational quadratic labels. Section <a href="#gr:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:section">8</a> will realize these labels simultaneously by primes and interpret the independent inertia changes as graph edges.

<a id="stmt-co-symbol-interface"></a>
<div id="co:symbol-interface" class="proposition">

**Proposition 54** (Symbol interface). *Under the arithmetic lower bounds in Lemma <a href="#co:minimum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:minimum">50</a>, the symbols of either coefficient source have the following properties.*

1.  *For matching $U$, the trace on split elements factors through the maximal elementary $2$-extension of $F$ unramified outside $S\cup U$. For opposite $U$, traces factor through the corresponding rational elementary extension. Adding a split vertex $q$ is read by coefficient $1$ of $t(\operatorname{Fr}_q)H_U$; adding a simple vertex is read on the opposite $B_U$.*

<a id="co:contraction"></a>
2.  *Let $p\in U$ and $g=\operatorname{Fr}_q$. Changing one oriented ramification bit at $p$ changes the symbol by $$\label{co:contraction}
     A\bigl((U\setminus\{p\})\cup\{q'\}\bigr),\qquad
     \operatorname{Fr}_{q'}\sim g\phi_p,$$ unless $p,q$ are both simple, in which case the change is zero. The replacement has the product labels at all retained places. Its coefficient system has support $S\cup(U\setminus\{p\})$: the entire rational prime $p$, including both places above it when it splits in $F$, is omitted.*

3.  *A split vertex with trivial label in the elementary extension for the other vertices has zero symbol.*

4.  *In matching parity, insertion of two simple primes with equal rational labels on the fixed support and at the existing split vertices preserves the symbol if $\#E(\mathbb F_q)/2$ is odd for that label. Their mutual simple bits and their incidences with other simple vertices do not affect this statement.*

*These are coefficient identities. Simultaneous realization of their labels by primes is a separate arithmetic construction.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-symbol-interface"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-symbol-interface</summary>

*Proof.* For an odd source choose the stage in Lemma <a href="#co:operators" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:operators">51</a> with cutoff $R\ge2$; only these fixed divisibilities and finitely many bounded-weight coefficient tests are needed below. Two fresh traces in matching parity, at least one split, add weight at least $3/2$. The allowed valuation in <a href="#co:BU-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:BU-bound">[co:BU-bound]</a> is an integer, so it is at least $2$ before the unary division and the result vanishes after it. In opposite parity any two fresh traces add weight at least $1$; subtracting $e_U=1/2$ and using integral valuation again gives vanishing. The estimates persist under further tests. Hence respectively $t(g)t(u)=0$ for split $u$, or $t(g)t(u)=0$ for every $u$. Applying <a href="#co:trace-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:trace-identities">[co:trace-identities]</a> to $t(gu)t(u)$ gives $t(gu^2)=t(g)$. The quotient by the subgroup generated by squares is elementary abelian, and the Galois systems are unramified off their level. This proves the stated factorization. At coefficient $q$ the fresh split weight $1$ pays exactly the division in $H_U$; the fresh simple weight $1/2$ pays exactly $e_U$ in opposite parity. No unary term survives. This proves the symbol-reading assertion.

For contraction, apply <a href="#co:inertia-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:inertia-identity">[co:inertia-identity]</a> at coefficient $1$, so that its left side is a coefficient-$p$ test. Replace its trace arguments by fresh primes. The first-shell calculation in Lemma <a href="#co:operators" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:operators">51</a> gives, in each of the split–split, split–simple, and simple–split cases, $$c_p(D')A(D'),\qquad D'=(U/p)q',\quad
 \operatorname{Fr}_{q'}\sim g\phi_p.$$ Here the omitted source is exactly $B_V$ or $H_V$, with $V=U\setminus\{p\}$, constructed from the same original modular form and the same $\eta,\psi,\delta$; no new minimum or normalization is chosen. To check the integer powers explicitly, put $n_U=\lceil\delta+w(U)\rceil$ and let $m_U$ be $1$ in matching parity and $0$ otherwise. After fresh tests kill the unary terms, the first shell relative to the omitted-source test has multiplier $$2^{1+n_V-n_U-m_U+m_V}c_p(D').$$ For split $p$ the exponent is zero. For simple $p$ and matching $U$ it is also zero. For simple $p$ and opposite $U$ it is one; this is precisely the simple–simple case. The character change $\psi_U/\psi_V=(p/\cdot)$ is already the Legendre factor in this shell calculation. The phase is a unit and disappears on reduction. Let $q_0',q_1'$ realize $g\phi_p,g\tau_p\phi_p$, respectively, and put $D_i'=Vq_i'$. Applying inertia changes the Legendre term in <a href="#co:cp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:cp">[co:cp]</a> by a sign, so $c_p(D_1')=c_p(D_0')+1$. Although these Frobenius elements may first be specified in the full current elementary extension, their restrictions to the omitted-source extension agree: inertia at either place above $p$ is killed in that extension. Its original characters are unramified at $p$, and its only new character factors come from $V$. Thus it is unramified at the whole rational prime $p$, giving $A(D_1')=A(D_0')$. If $\Delta_{\tau_p}A(Uq)$ denotes the difference of the original tests with Frobenius labels $g\tau_p$ and $g$, the reduced local identity is now explicitly $$\Delta_{\tau_p}A(Uq)
   =c_p(D_1')A(D_1')+c_p(D_0')A(D_0')=A(D_0'),$$ which is <a href="#co:contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:contraction">[co:contraction]</a>. When both vertices are simple the product Frobenius is split and the first-shell valuation has a positive excess; its reduction is zero. The entire-prime assertion follows from the actual support of the omitted-$p$ coefficient form, rather than from any convention about a chosen orientation. Isolation is the same symbol-reading test with $t(1)=0$.

For the final assertion first use two copies of a single simple Frobenius trace on matching $H_U$. Put $b=B_U[1]$, $\epsilon=\chi_{-4}(q)$, and $\kappa=(\psi_U/\eta)(q)$. Substitution of <a href="#co:recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:recurrence">[co:recurrence]</a> and of the unary character gives the two terms in the coefficient-$1$ test of $T_q^2$, modulo $\mathfrak m$: $$\begin{aligned}
 H_U[q^2]&=\frac{b\eta(q)}2
        (a_q-\kappa\epsilon-q\epsilon^i),\\
 q(\langle q\rangle H_U)[1]&=\frac{b\eta(q)}2
        q(\kappa-\epsilon^{i+1}).
\end{aligned}$$ Their sum is $$\frac{b\eta(q)}2
 \left(a_q+\kappa(q-\epsilon)
                    -q\epsilon^i(1+\epsilon)\right).$$ For $q\equiv3\pmod4$ the parenthesized half has residue $a_q/2$; for $q\equiv1\pmod4$ it has residue $a_q/2+1$. In both cases it is $\#E(\mathbb F_q)/2$ modulo $2$. Since $b$ reduces to $A(U)$, the squared trace has the required coefficient. Fresh-prime testing replaces its two copies by distinct primes. To pass to any pair with the specified labels, vary one prime at a time. After that simple prime is omitted the opposite-parity symbol depends only on rational elementary labels; simple–simple changes vanish by the proved contraction rule. Thus only the stated support and split-vertex labels matter. ◻

</details>

<a id="gr:section"></a>
### Arithmetic graphs and simultaneous addressing

Fix $b\ge1$. An *address* is a point $x\in\mathbf F_2^b$; a vertex with activation $\lambda\in(\mathbf F_2^b)^*$ is present at $x$ when $\lambda(x)=1$. We will adjoin fresh primes with nonzero activations to a given collection of primes. The goal is to make one or two binary coefficient tests equal to $1$ at every $x\ne0$, while retaining all the original primes and their residue data. No new prime is present at $0$. The hypothesis is that each test, separately at each nonzero address, is a nonzero polynomial in the available residue bits.

The arithmetic identities are those of Proposition <a href="#co:symbol-interface" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:symbol-interface">54</a>, conditional on its coefficient lower bounds. We first describe and realize their graph data, then state the simultaneous-testing result. Its proof uses contraction operators and two finite networks that isolate a prescribed derivative at one address.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Labels and arithmetic realization

Fix a finite set $S$ of rational primes, containing $2$ and all the ramified primes in the fixed data. Let $F$ be either $\mathbf Q$ or a quadratic field. For the rational two-torsion application $F=\mathbf Q(E[2])$; the construction with full rational two-torsion also uses a quadratic auxiliary field in place of $F$. Include in $S$ primes whose prime ideals generate the class group of $F$, and include the infinite places in $S_F$. Put $$V_S(F)=\{\alpha\in F^\times/F^{\times2}:
           v_{\mathfrak p}(\alpha)\equiv0\pmod2
           \text{ for }\mathfrak p\notin S_F\}.$$ This is a finite-dimensional $\mathbf F_2$-space: the valuation sequence expresses it as an extension of the finite squareclass group of $S_F$-units by a subgroup of the $2$-torsion of the $S_F$-class group. Its Kummer extension is denoted $F_S^{(2)}=F(\sqrt{V_S(F)})$.

<a id="gr:norm"></a>
A *split vertex* is a rational prime $p\notin S$ split in $F$, together with a choice $\mathfrak p\mid p$ if $F$ is quadratic. This choice is its *orientation*. Choose a generator $\pi_{\mathfrak p}$ with divisor $\mathfrak p$ outside $S_F$; class-group generation makes this possible. Conjugation supplies the generator for the other orientation, and $$\label{gr:norm}
 N_{F/\mathbf Q}(\pi_{\mathfrak p})=p u_{\mathfrak p},
 \qquad u_{\mathfrak p}\text{ a signed rational }S\text{-unit}.$$ For $F=\mathbf Q$ use the rational generator $p$. A *simple vertex* is an inert rational prime in the quadratic case, with rational generator $p$. The fixed label of a split vertex is its Frobenius on $F_S^{(2)}$; simple vertices have rational fixed-support labels. A *type* may additionally record the generators’ local squareclasses, valuation parities, real signs, and prescribed finite ray conditions at $S_F$. Only compatible, individually realizable types will be prescribed. A *filter* fixes the total rational labels and sign of the signed-prime product $\prod_p p^*$, where $p^*=(-1/p)p$.

For a pair of split vertices in the quadratic case there are two independent forward bits: the residues of $\pi_{\mathfrak p}$ and $\overline{\pi_{\mathfrak p}}$ at the chosen place of the other vertex. Write quadratic residues additively in $\mathbf F_2$. Reverse bits are determined by Hilbert reciprocity and the fixed types. Rational norm bits are determined by summing the two oriented bits and the fixed correction in <a href="#gr:norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gr:norm">[gr:norm]</a>. When at least one endpoint is simple, or $F=\mathbf Q$, there is one rational forward bit. Self-residues are never graph data. An *arithmetic graph* is this collection of vertices, types, and bits. A split vertex is *primary* when its generator is a square unit at every finite place of $S_F$ and positive at all real places. Different symbols may read compatible projections of the same graph, for example its rational norm graph. Here compatibility means that the projection forgets fixed-label coordinates or takes rational norm data, carries primary vertices to primary vertices, and commutes with the omitted-vertex product substitution. For the norm projection a single oriented-bit toggle changes the rational bit once. We use these arithmetic projections throughout.

<a id="stmt-gr-realization"></a>
<div id="gr:realization" class="lemma">

**Lemma 55** (Finite realization and product labels). *Fix finitely many old vertices, with all their mutual data, and individually realizable new types. Every prescription of the independent forward bits involving at least one new vertex has a realization by distinct fresh primes, avoiding an arbitrary finite set. The old vertices and their mutual data remain unchanged. The same holds with further fixed local tests included in $S$. A contracted split cluster may be represented by the product of its oriented generators, with conjugations prescribed along its contraction tree; its support types and external residue bits are the corresponding sums. A cluster containing one simple vertex has the corresponding rational product data.*

*For a symbol satisfying the omitted-vertex dependence in Proposition <a href="#co:symbol-interface" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:symbol-interface">54</a>, its value depends only on the graph data. Products in the preceding assertion may therefore be replaced by fresh prime representatives without changing the prescribed contracted symbol.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-gr-realization"><summary>Proof of stmt-gr-realization</summary>

*Proof.* First note that the listed generators account for all the relevant radicals. If $U$ is a finite set of new primes and a squareclass has even valuations outside $S_F\cup U_F$, divide by the generators at the primes of $U_F$ where its valuation is odd. The result belongs to $V_S(F)$. At an inert rational prime its rational generator has valuation one at the unique place of $F$. Thus fixed-support labels and the displayed residue bits determine every Frobenius test in an extension with this allowed ramification.

We describe the prime selection carefully. For a new split vertex take a template $\pi_0$ of its prescribed type, with prime ideal $\mathfrak p_0$ away from the old vertices. Weak approximation gives $a\in F^\times$ which is sufficiently close to $1$ at $S_F$, positive at the prescribed real places, and has any prescribed nonzero residues at the old prime ideals. Choose those residues so that $a\pi_0$ has the desired quadratic residues there. Use the ray modulus consisting of sufficiently deep powers of the primes of $S_F$, all old prime ideals, and the real places. Choose a prime ideal $\mathfrak q$ in the ray class of $(a)\mathfrak p_0$. Then $$\mathfrak q=(a\beta)\mathfrak p_0,
 \qquad \beta\equiv1\pmod{\text{ray modulus}},$$ and $\pi_{\mathfrak q}=a\beta\pi_0$ is the required generator outside $S_F$. There are infinitely many such degree-one prime ideals. Indeed, the prime-ideal theorem in a ray class gives order $X/\log X$ prime ideals of norm at most $X$, whereas prime ideals of residue degree greater than one contribute only $O(X^{1/2})$ in a quadratic field. Degree one gives a split rational prime, and finite exclusions remove only finitely many choices.

At an old inert prime with residue field $\mathbf F_{p^2}$, prescribe the norm of the nonzero residue. The norm onto $\mathbf F_p^\times$ is surjective, so either rational residue bit is possible. For a new simple vertex the requirements are rational congruences, together with the inert class in $F$. The Chinese remainder theorem and Dirichlet’s theorem supply fresh primes. Successively adjoining vertices proves simultaneous finite realization. At every step reciprocity determines the reverse bits: the local Hilbert symbols at the support are fixed, and the only other contributions are the two endpoint residues. Taking norms gives exactly <a href="#gr:norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gr:norm">[gr:norm]</a>, so it imposes no additional independent bit.

The same argument applies to a product template. Its valuations and local squareclasses at $S_F$ are those of the product, and its residues at the remaining vertices multiply. The ray-class construction replaces that template by a degree-one prime with those data. In a simple cluster we instead use rational congruences for the norm labels. No residue at a prime inside the cluster is imposed on its replacement.

In particular this preserves the rational filter, including its norm correction. Write $\pi=\prod_i\pi_i$ for the oriented product template and $\pi'=a\beta\pi$ for its fresh representative; $a\beta$ is a square unit at $S_F$ and positive at its real places. The support valuations of $\pi'$ and $\pi$ agree exactly. Hence $$N\pi=(\prod_i p_i)u,\qquad N\pi'=q u$$ with the same signed rational $S$-unit $u$: all its valuations and its sign agree. The quotient $q/\prod_i p_i=N(a\beta)$ is a local square at the rational support. Its squareclass at $2$ also identifies $q^*$ with $\prod_i p_i^*$ at those fixed tests. Thus no free choice of an untracked $S$-unit has entered a product replacement.

Finally, compare two disjoint realizations with identical graph data. Introduce fresh bridge vertices with matching bits to both realizations, and replace vertices one at a time. At each replacement the omitted-vertex elementary Frobenius tests agree, by the generator description in the first paragraph. The symbol interface gives equal values. A preliminary bridge removes any overlap between the original realizations. The same reasoning applies to the product replacements. ◻

</details>

Primary types are realized by the preceding construction, starting with the principal ray class. Reciprocity makes their fixed radical labels trivial. Their signed rational contributions are squares at the fixed rational tests. If stationary vertices are also to have neutral incidences with an auxiliary, include their primes among these local tests. In particular, including the support of a fixed quadratic discriminant $k$ ensures that every such primary auxiliary splits in $\mathbf Q(\sqrt{k})$.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### The simultaneous-testing statement

Separate the given vertices into *terminals* and, if present, stationary external vertices. Terminals may be always present or may have nonzero linear activations; external vertices have fixed types and incidences throughout. At each nonzero address the symbol is a function of the independent bits between the active terminals, with those types and external data fixed. We call its unique multilinear polynomial over $\mathbf F_2$ the *terminal polynomial*. Its variables describe formal changes of the terminal bits, while the prescribed prime realization supplies a fixed evaluation point.

For a bit $z$ define the finite difference $\Delta_z f=f|_{z=0}+f|_{z=1}$. On a multilinear polynomial it is the formal partial derivative. In particular, differences commute and $\Delta_z^2=0$.

<a id="stmt-gr-partitionable"></a>
<div id="gr:partitionable" class="definition">

**Definition 56** (Witnesses and permitted partitions). A *witness* is a finite graph on which the symbol is $1$. A graph is *partitionable* for the symbol if its vertices can be partitioned into nonempty clusters, each with at most one simple vertex, with a choice of oriented contraction trees whose contracted graph is a witness. This definition includes a specified contraction certificate; it makes no assertion about a Selmer group.

</div>

<a id="stmt-gr-address"></a>
<div id="gr:address" class="theorem">

**Theorem 57** (Address lemma). *Suppose the binary symbols under consideration depend only on the arithmetic graph data and satisfy the contraction, whole-rational-prime omission, and primary-isolation rules of Proposition <a href="#co:symbol-interface" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:symbol-interface">54</a>, with the realization freedoms of Lemma <a href="#gr:realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:realization">55</a>. Contraction includes the product labels and external incidences, and the zero rule for two simple vertices. These identities are required only on nonempty configurations and for contractions with nonempty output. Fix $b\ge1$ and either of the following terminal arrangements.*

1.  *One symbol, a nonempty set of always active terminals, and a nonzero polynomial in their independent pair bits.*

2.  *One or two symbols on terminals with nonzero linear activations, including at least one terminal for every nonzero linear form. At each $x\ne0$ each symbol separately is a nonzero polynomial in the active terminal bits. The symbols may read different compatible projections of a common graph alphabet and may have fixed stationary external data.*

*Suppose also that at each nonzero address the total active terminal data, together with any stationary external data, obey the required filter. Starting from any fixed realization of the terminal and stationary vertices, one can adjoin finitely many fresh primary auxiliary vertices, activated by nonzero linear forms, and choose only incidences involving these new vertices so that every symbol equals $1$ at every $x\ne0$. All data on the original vertices remain unchanged. No auxiliary is present at $0$. All auxiliaries may satisfy the fixed splitting and neutral external-incidence conditions incorporated into the primary type.*

*It is enough that a terminal polynomial have a nonzero evaluation or a nonzero iterated edge difference. In particular a partitionability certificate suffices for the stationary hypothesis.*

</div>

The proof has two algebraic steps. Forests of auxiliary vertices act as finite-difference operators on the original terminal polynomial. We then build networks whose degree-one terms select a chosen derivative at a single address. Applying them to top-degree coefficients will give the simultaneous value $1$, independently of the fixed terminal bits.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### From arithmetic contraction to forest operators

<a id="stmt-gr-forest"></a>
<div id="gr:forest" class="lemma">

**Lemma 58** (Oriented forests). *Assume the graph-symbol rules stated in Theorem <a href="#gr:address" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:address">57</a>. Differentiation in a tree of graph bits contracts the tree, adding product labels and external incidences; its value is zero if the tree contains two simple vertices. Differentiation in any collection of edge variables containing a cycle is zero, even when different edges use incompatible relative orientations. Two different oriented variables on the same pair count as a cycle of length two.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-gr-forest"><summary>Proof of stmt-gr-forest</summary>

*Proof.* The one-edge assertion is precisely the arithmetic contraction rule, with product replacements justified by Lemma <a href="#gr:realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:realization">55</a>. Induction proves the tree assertion: a split–split merger is split, a split–simple merger is simple, and the first merger of two components containing simple vertices has zero derivative. Along a tree there is a unique choice of relative orientations once one root orientation is fixed. Its product template depends on support types and external bits, not on the internal bits being differentiated.

For clarity, consider first the two variables on a split pair $p,q$. Differentiation in the inertia bit at one place over $p$ gives the symbol formed with the *entire rational prime $p$ omitted*. Its elementary extension is unramified at both places over $p$. Differentiating in the other oriented bit is therefore zero. Repetition of the same variable is zero by $\Delta_z^2=0$. This argument would not follow from omission of only one oriented prime ideal.

For a longer cycle, first contract a spanning path. By the product-label rule the closing variable has become internal to the merged cluster. Its change affects neither the product’s support data nor any incidence to a remaining vertex. Equivalently it is an inertia change at an omitted endpoint, covered by the preceding unramifiedness argument. Thus its derivative vanishes. Possible conjugation of the product generator only changes the induced orientation and does not reintroduce that endpoint. Since there is no self-residue among the inputs to the replacement symbol, there is no extra closing-edge datum to retain. Additional derivatives commute with this zero derivative.

For example, contract the aligned edge $12$ in a triangle. With $\pi_{12}=\pi_1\pi_2$, the two bits toward vertex $3$ are $t_0=z_{13}^0+z_{23}^0$ and $t_1=z_{13}^1+z_{23}^1$, up to fixed type corrections. An aligned edge $23$ then differentiates $t_0$; a conjugately aligned closing edge $31$ differentiates $t_1$. Their mixed derivative is the omitted-endpoint zero just proved. If the closing edge is aligned instead, both become the same difference and its square is zero. Contracting a longer spanning path reduces to exactly these two alternatives. ◻

</details>

Fix a realizable baseline terminal graph and let $P$ be its terminal polynomial. A *network* consists of additional primary vertices and programmed edges, with no direct terminal–terminal edges. All unprogrammed incidences of its primary vertices are neutral. Within one network choose one orientation at each terminal and consistently use the corresponding edge alignment. Networks may choose different alignments. The *degree* of a contraction is the number of terminal vertices it removes by merging them.

<a id="stmt-gr-network"></a>
<div id="gr:network" class="lemma">

**Lemma 59** (Network expansion and its first two degrees). *A network acts on $P$ by a sum of finite-difference operators. A surviving term is a forest in which every component containing a primary vertex meets a terminal. A component meeting $t$ terminals acts by a contraction of degree $t-1$; a component containing two simple terminals is zero. Networks on disjoint sets of internal vertices, with no edges between those sets, multiply as operators on the same terminal polynomial. Consequently an operator of degree greater than $\deg P$ annihilates $P$.*

<a id="gr:forest-coefficients"></a>
*Let $Q$ be the grounded Laplacian on the internal vertices: $Q_{ij}$ for $i\ne j$ records the internal edge $ij$, while $Q_{ii}$ is the total incidence at $i$, including its terminal edges. Let $(c_a)_i$ record the incidence from internal vertex $i$ to terminal $a$. All entries are in $\mathbf F_2$. The constant and pair-contraction coefficients of the operator are respectively $$\label{gr:forest-coefficients}
 \det Q,\qquad c_a^{\mathsf t}\operatorname{adj}(Q)c_b\quad(a\ne b).$$ The second coefficient multiplies the terminal difference in the alignment selected by the network. A prohibited pair multiplies the zero operator.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-gr-network"><summary>Proof of stmt-gr-network</summary>

*Proof.* Expand the evaluation at the programmed incidences from the graph with all primary incidences zero. For a set $E_0$ of programmed bits this is the elementary identity $$f(\mathbf1)=\sum_{T\subseteq E_0}(\Delta_T f)(\mathbf0).$$ Lemma <a href="#gr:forest" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:forest">58</a> kills cyclic $T$. A tree component meeting no terminal contracts to an isolated primary vertex, and the isolation rule kills it. This includes an untouched primary vertex. In any remaining component all primary labels are trivial and all external primary bits at the expansion baseline are zero. Its contracted label and its incidences are exactly those obtained by contracting its terminal vertices in the original graph. Choose any terminal tree inducing its relative orientations; the corresponding terminal differences therefore give the same value. Its degree is the number of lost terminal vertices, namely $t-1$. This establishes the operator interpretation, rather than merely a count of possible contraction degrees.

Expanding two disjoint networks at once gives the composition of these operators: the product-label rule gives the same final labels in either order, and any cycles created between networks vanish by Lemma <a href="#gr:forest" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:forest">58</a>. Ordinary polynomial differentiation now proves the asserted degree bound.

This argument also explains the compatible projections. Taking norms commutes with every oriented product in a forest; primary factors have neutral norm corrections, and each programmed oriented toggle projects to its one rational toggle. Thus the projected forest acts by the corresponding differences on the projected *original* terminal polynomial at every degree. If projected differences repeat an edge or merge two simple clusters, that term is zero. Forgetting fixed-label coordinates plainly commutes with the same products. Each projected network therefore acts on the original projected terminal polynomial.

The determinant counts are instances of the all-minors matrix-tree theorem (Chaiken 1982, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-82"></a>[[Chaiken1982](#ref-Chaiken1982)]. We give the Cauchy–Binet proof in the form needed here, including singular $Q$. Let $I$ be the internal vertex set and $A$ the terminal set. Write the full Laplacian as $L=DD^{\mathsf t}$, where a column of the incidence matrix $D$ has ones at its edge’s endpoints. Cauchy–Binet applied to $L_{I,I}=Q$ counts edge sets whose incidence matrix with the terminal rows deleted is invertible. Such an edge set is a forest with exactly one terminal in each component: a cycle gives dependent columns; a component with no deleted row gives dependent rows; and for a tree with one row deleted, leaf removal proves invertibility. These are exactly the degree-zero terms.

For the pair coefficient apply Cauchy–Binet with row set $I\cup\{a\}$ and column set $I\cup\{b\}$. Each counted forest has one root from $A\setminus\{a\}$ and one from $A\setminus\{b\}$ in each component. This requires that $a,b$ lie together and that every other terminal lie in its own component. The corresponding Laplacian minor is $$\det\begin{pmatrix}Q&c_b\\c_a^{\mathsf t}&0\end{pmatrix}
       =c_a^{\mathsf t}\operatorname{adj}(Q)c_b.$$ The corner is zero because the network has no direct terminal edge; signs disappear in characteristic two. The determinant identity uses the adjugate and so remains valid when $Q$ is singular. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### Two networks which select one address

Fix a nonzero target $x_*$ and a terminal bit $z$ joining two terminals $a_0,a_1$ active at $x_*$. In the moving case assume that some terminal has each nonzero activation form; in the stationary case all terminals are always present.

Choose a basis $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_b$ of the dual space with $\lambda_i(x_*)=1$ for every $i$. To obtain it, choose $\lambda_1(x_*)=1$ and replace a basis $\mu_2,\ldots,\mu_b$ of the annihilator of $x_*$ by $\lambda_1+\mu_2,\ldots,\lambda_1+\mu_b$. Make $b$ groups of three primary vertices, group $i$ activated by $\lambda_i$. Since these forms are a basis, all groups are present exactly at $x_*$. At every other address at least one group is absent. In each physical three-dimensional vertex space choose a basis $(e_i,u_i,v_i)$ with $e_i=(1,1,1)$. Thus the physical coordinate change is block diagonal by groups.

Prescribe a symmetric bilinear form $Q$ by making the $e_i$ orthonormal and orthogonal to all $u_j,v_j$, and putting on the remaining coordinates <a id="gr:H"></a>$$\label{gr:H}
 H(T,B)=\begin{pmatrix}0&T\\T^{\mathsf t}&B\end{pmatrix}.$$ Matrix entries in the physical coordinates are denoted by the same letter $Q$. For a subset $J$ of active groups, $Q_J$ means its physical principal restriction. Define the column $h_i=Qe_i$. It is supported on group $i$: in adapted coordinates it is the covector dual to $e_i$, and the coordinate change does not mix groups. Its value is independent of $T$ and $B$.

Attach $h_i$ to an anchor terminal with activation $\lambda_i$. In the stationary case attach it to any fixed terminal instead. The support property gives, on every address, $$\sum_{i\in J}h_i|_J=Q_J\mathbf1_J.$$ Program internal edges using the off-diagonal physical entries of $Q$. A matrix is their grounded Laplacian precisely when the sum of the terminal incidence columns is its row-sum vector, so these anchor columns give exactly $Q_J$, including its diagonal.

We shall also attach the same additional column $c$ to $a_0$ and $a_1$. In the moving case with distinct activation forms $\alpha,\beta$, attach $c$ also to a terminal of activation $\alpha+\beta$; this terminal is inactive at $x_*$. If $\alpha=\beta$, no third column is needed. The sum of the additional active columns is zero at every address, since $\alpha+\beta+(\alpha+\beta)=0$. In the stationary case the two columns cancel directly. Coincident terminal roles mean addition of their columns. This preserves the prescribed grounded matrix after every restriction.

For a concrete instance, take $b=1$, target $x_*=1$, and two always-active split terminals $a_0,a_1$ with selected bit $z$. Label the three primary vertices $1,2,3$, all activated by $x$. Choose $e=(1,1,1)^{\mathsf t}$, $u=(1,0,0)^{\mathsf t}$, $v=(0,1,0)^{\mathsf t}$, and $T=(0)$, $B=(1)$. The form in this adapted basis is $\operatorname{diag}(1,0,1)$. In physical coordinates $h=Qe=(0,0,1)^{\mathsf t}$ and the covector dual to $u$ is $c=(1,0,1)^{\mathsf t}$. Using $a_0$ as the anchor gives $$Q=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\0&1&1\\0&1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 c_{a_0}=h+c=\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\\0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 c_{a_1}=c=\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\\1\end{pmatrix}.$$ Thus the programmed graph is the path $a_0\! -\! 1\! -\! a_1\! -\! 3\! -\! 2$. Here $\det Q=0$ and $\operatorname{adj}(Q)=uu^{\mathsf t}$, so $c_{a_0}^{\mathsf t}\operatorname{adj}(Q)c_{a_1}=1$. There are only two terminals, so no higher contraction degree occurs: the network acts exactly by $\Delta_z$ at $1$. At $0$ all three primary vertices are absent, and the network acts by the identity.

Write $O_{\ge d}$ for a sum of contraction operators of degrees at least $d$; it is a filtration notation, not an estimate.

<a id="stmt-gr-programs"></a>
<div id="gr:programs" class="lemma">

**Lemma 60** (The two address programs). *With the preceding grounding convention one has the following programs for every $b\ge1$.*

1.  *A network $\mathcal S$ with $$\mathcal S_{x_*}=\Delta_z+O_{\ge2},\qquad
     \mathcal S_x=1+O_{\ge1}\quad(x\ne x_*).$$*

2.  *Two networks on the same activated sites, indexed by $\varepsilon\in\mathbf F_2$, with $$\mathcal N_x^{(\varepsilon)}=1+O_{\ge1},\qquad
     \mathcal N_x^{(1)}+\mathcal N_x^{(0)}
     =\begin{cases}\Delta_z+O_{\ge2},&x=x_*,\\
     O_{\ge2},&x\ne x_*.
     \end{cases}$$*

*All nonzero-activation sites are absent at $0$. Unspecified incidences, including those to stationary external vertices, may remain neutral.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-gr-programs"><summary>Proof of stmt-gr-programs</summary>

*Proof.* For the first program take $T=I+P$, where $P$ is the cyclic permutation matrix on $b$ coordinates, and $B$ diagonal with exactly one nonzero entry. A proper principal group restriction of $P$ is a union of directed paths, hence nilpotent. Thus $T_J$ is invertible for every proper $J$, and so is $H(T_J,B_J)$: its kernel equations first give $v=0$ and then $u=0$. This includes the empty restriction, whose determinant is $1$.

On the full group set, both $\ker T$ and $\ker T^{\mathsf t}$ are spanned by the all-one vector. A radical vector of $H$ has $v=t\mathbf1$; summing the equation $T^{\mathsf t}u+Bv=0$ gives $t=0$ because $\mathbf1^{\mathsf t}B\mathbf1=1$. Thus the full $Q$ has one-dimensional radical spanned by $r=\sum_i u_i$. In characteristic two its adjugate is $rr^{\mathsf t}$. Indeed a rank-$n-1$ matrix has a nonzero rank-one adjugate with image its kernel; symmetry identifies the two factors, and the only nonzero scalar is $1$. Choose $c$ to be the covector dual to $u_1$, so $c^{\mathsf t}r=1$. All anchors pair to zero with $r$. At $x_*$ the only columns pairing nontrivially with $r$ are consequently those of $a_0,a_1$. Formula <a href="#gr:forest-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gr:forest-coefficients">[gr:forest-coefficients]</a> gives zero constant and exactly $\Delta_z$ in degree one. At every other address the group subset is proper and the constant is $1$. When $b=1$, $I+P=0$ and $B=(1)$, so the same radical calculation applies without an exception.

For the second program let $T=I+L$, where $L$ is the consecutive lower shift, and take $B_\varepsilon=\varepsilon E_{bb}$. Every group restriction of $T$ is invertible, and block multiplication gives <a id="gr:inverse"></a>$$\label{gr:inverse}
 H(T,B)^{-1}=
 \begin{pmatrix}T^{-\mathsf t}BT^{-1}&T^{-\mathsf t}\\
 T^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}.$$ Again take $c$ dual to $u_1$. On a group subset $J$, the change of $c^{\mathsf t}Q_J^{-1}c$ is $$\bigl((T_J^{-1})_{b1}\bigr)^2,$$ interpreted as zero if $1$ or $b$ is absent. Since $(I+L_J)^{-1}=I+L_J+L_J^2+\cdots$, that entry is $1$ precisely when the entire path $1,2,\ldots,b$ is present. This is exactly the target address. Pairings involving an anchor do not change: $Q_J^{-1}h_i=e_i$, and the vectors $e_i,h_i,c$ do not depend on $\varepsilon$. At the target the balancing terminal is absent, so the only changed pair coefficient is the one for $a_0,a_1$. Every constant is $1$. Lemma <a href="#gr:network" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:network">59</a> proves the claims, including $b=1$, when the path consists of its single vertex. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### Proof of the address lemma

<details id="proof-stmt-gr-programs-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-gr-programs</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#gr:address" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:address">57</a>.* Use the prescribed realization of the terminal and stationary vertices as a common baseline for all terminal bits. Regard their toggles as common variables, omitting variables invisible to a given symbol. A nonzero iterated difference implies that the polynomial is nonzero. Conversely, the unique multilinear polynomial of a nonzero function has a top monomial with nonzero coefficient. These observations also explain the last assertion using Lemma <a href="#gr:forest" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:forest">58</a>. Every nonzero address has an active terminal, and contractions never remove the last vertex. Thus only nonempty configurations are used.

First consider one symbol. At each $x\ne0$ let $P_x$ have degree $d_x$ and choose a monomial with coefficient $1$ in its top homogeneous part. For each variable of this monomial add a separate singular program $\mathcal S$ targeted at $x$. Give all these networks disjoint internal vertices. At address $x$, its own $d_x$ networks each have zero constant, so every surviving term spends at least $d_x$ contraction degrees. An extra degree from any network kills $P_x$. Thus only the designated degree-one contractions of its own networks and the constant terms of all other networks survive. Their value is the chosen top coefficient, namely $1$. A nonzero constant polynomial needs no network and already has value $1$. This argument applies equally to always active terminals, since Lemma <a href="#gr:programs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:programs">60</a> supplies their stationary anchors.

<a id="gr:chosen-monomial"></a>
For two symbols write their polynomials at address $x\ne0$ as $P_x,R_x$, of degrees $d_x,e_x$, and let $P_x^{\rm top},R_x^{\rm top}$ be their top homogeneous parts. Multiply these parts in the ordinary polynomial ring $\mathbf F_2[z_1,\ldots,z_m]$, *before* imposing any Boolean relations. That ring is an integral domain, so choose a coefficient equal to $1$ at a monomial $$\label{gr:chosen-monomial}
 M_x=\prod_z z^{\nu_{xz}},\qquad
 \nu_{xz}\in\{0,1,2\},\qquad
 \sum_z\nu_{xz}=d_x+e_x.$$ For $\nu_{xz}=2$ add one singular program for $z$ targeted at $x$. For $\nu_{xz}=1$ add one nonsingular program for $z$ targeted at $x$, with its own choice $\varepsilon_{xz}\in\mathbf F_2$. All internal vertices of different programs are disjoint. Let $$\Phi(\varepsilon)=\prod_{x\ne0}
       \bigl(\text{final evaluation of }P_x\bigr)
       \bigl(\text{final evaluation of }R_x\bigr).$$ We prove that the sum of $\Phi$ over all choices of $\varepsilon$ is $1$. This proves that some single choice has all the required evaluations equal to $1$.

Expand every program in contraction degree using Lemma <a href="#gr:network" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:network">59</a>. A singular program targeted at $x$ has zero constant in both factors there, so it costs at least one degree in $P_x$ and one in $R_x$. A nonsingular program has constant $1$ independent of its choice. In the sum over that choice, a term using only its constants in all factors cancels. Every remaining term must therefore spend at least one degree somewhere for this program. The sum of these minimum costs is exactly <a id="gr:budget"></a>$$\label{gr:budget}
 D=\sum_{x\ne0}(d_x+e_x),$$ by <a href="#gr:chosen-monomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gr:chosen-monomial">[gr:chosen-monomial]</a>. A nonzero term cannot exceed this total: if it did, some factor would receive more differences than its degree. Hence every surviving term uses every minimum cost exactly.

It follows that a singular program spends exactly one degree in each factor at its target and zero elsewhere. Its degree-one term there is precisely $\Delta_z$. Each nonsingular program spends exactly one degree in exactly one factor. After summing its choice, that degree-one contribution is zero away from its target, and is $\Delta_z$ at its target. All other occurrences of these programs contribute their common constant $1$. Thus no unknown higher forest coefficient enters the surviving calculation.

At a fixed address, let $Z_2$ be the variables with exponent two and $Z_1$ those with exponent one. The local allocation sum is $$\sum_{J\subseteq Z_1}
 [z_{Z_2}z_J]P_x^{\rm top}\,
 [z_{Z_2}z_{Z_1\setminus J}]R_x^{\rm top}
   =[M_x](P_x^{\rm top}R_x^{\rm top})=1.$$ Coefficients of the wrong degree in this display are zero. Every factor must in fact receive its full degree, since the total is $D$ and none can exceed its own degree. Therefore its final derivative is exactly the displayed top coefficient, independent of the chosen baseline values. Allocations at different addresses are independent, so their product is $1$, as asserted. This also covers a constant factor, whose degree is zero. For example, when both top forms are $z$, their ordinary product is $z^2$ and the singular program differentiates each factor once; Boolean reduction would lose this allocation.

In the two-symbol case, choose the program parameters for which $\Phi=1$. In either case, apply Lemma <a href="#gr:realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:realization">55</a> to adjoin the resulting finite networks to the fixed original vertices. Only new vertices and incidences involving them have been prescribed. The primary types preserve the filters. All activations are nonzero linear forms, so no new vertex appears at $0$, and the stipulated neutral and splitting conditions persist. ◻

</details>

The theorem is finite for each $b$; it does not assert a single infinite array of primes. For a stabilized symbol, first choose $b$, its finite terminal data, and all the networks above. Their total weight and the list of coefficient tests are then finite. A sufficiently advanced stage at which the symbol interface holds for that weight range realizes the construction. Required precision and the number of auxiliaries may grow with $b$. The arithmetic valuation constant attached to a unit symbol must still be supplied by the coefficient construction independently of $b$; no determinant or forest count in this section proves that uniformity.

<a id="pw:section"></a>
### Assembly at a fixed curve

Fix $E/\mathbb Q$ with nonzero rational two-torsion. The even construction has two uses: it proves the corank-zero case and supplies the uniform estimate needed to normalize the odd coefficients. After that normalization, the corank-one argument fixes a nonvanishing companion and constructs the two simultaneous coefficient tests required for ring-class transfer.

<a id="pw:interfaces-section"></a>
#### Interfaces and conventions

We use the fixed-support conventions of Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>. Thus all variable prime factors are odd primes of good reduction, $p^*=(-1/p)p$, and a filter fixes the real sign and unit squareclasses at a finite support containing $2N$, with finitely many further exclusions. Recall $$t_p=\dim_{\mathbb F_2}E[2]^{\operatorname{Fr}_p},\qquad
 w(h)=\frac12\sum_{p\mid h}t_p.$$ Whenever the two-primary group is finite, we write $$s(h)=\log_2|\operatorname{Sha}(E^{(h)}/\mathbb Q)[2^\infty]|.$$ This includes a base of Selmer corank zero and every parameter of analytic order zero or one. It is distinct from $s_2(E^{(h)})$, the Selmer corank. The normalized central value is denoted $L_*(h)$, and $v=v_2$ with $v(2)=1$.

Here are the preceding results in the form used below. Their roles are different: coefficient units give upper bounds, interpolation returns those bounds to an unknown base, and the ring-class lower bound makes the odd normalization possible. The latter step is carried out in Proposition <a href="#pw:odd-lower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pw:odd-lower">70</a>.

<a id="stmt-pw-interfaces"></a>
<div id="pw:interfaces" class="proposition">

**Proposition 61** (Collected interfaces). *The following statements hold for the fixed curve and fixed local data.*

1.  *The cyclotomic missing-vertex statement of Theorem <a href="#cy:missing-vertex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:missing-vertex">5</a> applies to any fixed base $h_0$ of Selmer corank zero. If arbitrarily large binary cubes based at $h_0$, with no old prime removed, have nonzero central values at every other address and satisfy $$v(L_*(h_x))-2w(h_x)-s(h_x)\le B\quad(x\ne0),$$ then the base central value is nonzero and satisfies the same inequality with $B$ replaced by $B+C$. The constant $C$ depends only on the fixed curve and local data, not on $h_0$, its number of prime factors, or the cube dimension. The dimension required for the argument may depend on the base. The cyclotomic lower bound at an analytically nonvanishing parameter is also available.*

<a id="pw:ring-lower-interface"></a>
2.  *Corollary <a href="#rd:ring-lower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:ring-lower">11</a> has the following uniform form. If $k<0$ is an odd fundamental discriminant with at most $A$ prime factors, $K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{k})$ is neither an extra-unit field nor the CM field, $k$ is a square at $2Nh$, and the twists $h,hk$ have respective analytic orders one and zero, then their ring-class Heegner sum has free-line index $j_h(k)$ satisfying $$\label{pw:ring-lower-interface}
     2j_h(k)\ge 4w(h)+s(h)+s(hk)-C_{E,A}.$$ For a fixed such $k$, Theorem <a href="#rd:missing-vertex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:missing-vertex">12</a> applies to cubes based at $h=1$ whose new primes all split in $K$. If $s_2(E/K)=1$ and the nonzero addresses have product analytic order one and uniformly bounded $2j_h(k)-4w(h)-s(h)-s(hk)$, the base product has analytic order one.*

<a id="pw:even-unit-interface"></a>
<a id="pw:odd-unit-interface"></a>
3.  *The even coefficient system, exact same-filter square comparison, divided operators, and trace identities of Proposition <a href="#co:even" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:even">46</a> and Lemmas <a href="#co:operators" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:operators">51</a>, <a href="#co:traces" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:traces">52</a>, and <a href="#co:inertia" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:inertia">53</a> are available. After a uniform lower bound for the relevant point indices has been proved, the moving odd coefficient system and its stabilized minimum are available as in Proposition <a href="#co:odd" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:odd">49</a> and Lemma <a href="#co:minimum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:minimum">50</a>. The resulting unit symbols satisfy the contraction, good-twin, and primary-isolation rules of Proposition <a href="#co:symbol-interface" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:symbol-interface">54</a>. An even unit gives $$\label{pw:even-unit-interface}
     L_*(h)\ne0,\qquad v(L_*(h))\le 2w(h)+C;$$ an odd unit gives analytic order one and $$\label{pw:odd-unit-interface}
     j(P_h)\le w(h)+C.$$ The constants are independent of the number and sizes of the variable primes. The fixed genus partner used to define $P_h$ is denoted $h_*$.*

4.  *Arithmetic realization and the forest and address statements of Lemmas <a href="#gr:realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:realization">55</a> and <a href="#gr:forest" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:forest">58</a> and Theorem <a href="#gr:address" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:address">57</a> hold. In particular, separate nonzero terminal polynomials may be addressed simultaneously. Primary auxiliaries preserve all fixed local classes, have neutral incidence to specified stationary external vertices, and may be required to split in any compatible fixed fields. They contribute no prime at address zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pw-interfaces"><summary>Proof of stmt-pw-interfaces</summary>

*Proof.* These are the cited results, with the arithmetic ring-class hypotheses verified by Proposition <a href="#rd:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rd:assembly">39</a>. The even coefficient minimum uses the cyclotomic lower bound; the odd detector of Proposition <a href="#co:odd" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:odd">49</a> still needs the arithmetic lower bound for Lemma <a href="#co:minimum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:minimum">50</a>. Neither interpolation statement nor the even symbol uses that odd normalization. ◻

</details>

The analytic witnesses and the rough completion used below are supplied by Propositions <a href="#nv:local-witness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nv:local-witness">43</a> and <a href="#nv:rough" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nv:rough">44</a>; the latter has an absolute prime-count bound. The bounds in Proposition <a href="#pw:interfaces" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pw:interfaces">61</a>, unlike the eventual sizes of the primes chosen by nonvanishing or Chebotarev, must be uniform in the parameters indicated there.

<a id="stmt-pw-isogeny-corank"></a>
<div id="pw:isogeny-corank" class="lemma">

<a id="pw:quadratic-corank"></a>
<a id="pw:kummer-corank"></a>
**Lemma 62** (Isogeny orientation and coranks). *Recall that a rational isogeny preserves analytic rank, Mordell–Weil rank, and $2^\infty$-Selmer corank. If $k$ is a quadratic discriminant and $K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{k})$, then $$\label{pw:quadratic-corank}
 s_2(E/K)=s_2(E/\mathbb Q)+s_2(E^{(k)}/\mathbb Q).$$ For every $E/\mathbb Q$ one has, separately, $$\label{pw:kummer-corank}
 s_2(E)=r(E)+
 \operatorname{corank}_{\mathbb Z_2}\operatorname{Sha}(E/\mathbb Q)[2^\infty].$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pw-isogeny-corank"><summary>Proof of stmt-pw-isogeny-corank</summary>

*Proof.* These are Lemmas <a href="#pr:invariance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pr:invariance">3</a> and <a href="#pr:ranks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pr:ranks">2</a>. In particular, the quadratic identity is an identity of coranks, not an integral direct-sum decomposition. ◻

</details>

If the isogeny class contains a curve with full rational two-torsion, we use that curve. Otherwise choose a rational two-isogeny $E\to E'$ and write $F=\mathbb Q(E[2])$, $F'=\mathbb Q(E'[2])$; both fields are quadratic. A prime split in $F$ is called split, and one inert in $F$ is called simple. Their weights are respectively $1$ and $1/2$. A simple prime is good if it is also inert in $F'$. At such a prime <a id="pw:good-simple"></a>$$\label{pw:good-simple}
 \#E(\mathbb F_p)\equiv2\pmod4.$$ Indeed there is exactly one nonzero rational two-torsion point in the reduction. If the group order were divisible by four, its two-primary subgroup would contain a rational point of order four. Its image under the two-isogeny would supply on $E'$ a rational two-torsion point different from the dual kernel, contradicting inertness in $F'$. Thus the good-twin rule applies. When $F=F'$, every simple prime is good. A prime split in $F$ and inert in $F'$ is a *donor*: attaching its label to a bad simple label makes that label good. This terminology describes finite label operations; the required prime supplies will be constructed below.

<a id="pw:seeds-section"></a>
#### Seeds and finite supplies

The word “label” below means an individually realizable fixed-support elementary Frobenius label, with orientations when required. It does not include a self-residue of a prime generator. All contractions and comparisons are those of Proposition <a href="#co:symbol-interface" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:symbol-interface">54</a> and Lemma <a href="#gr:forest" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:forest">58</a>.

<a id="stmt-pw-minimum-seeds"></a>
<div id="pw:minimum-seeds" class="lemma">

**Lemma 63** (Minimum seeds).

1.  *In an all-split alphabet, suppose a unit symbol has a nonempty witness. At the least positive vertex count $r$ of such a witness, all mutual edge bits are immaterial. The symbol is determined by the individual fixed-support labels. This remains true if the empty configuration is a unit, provided no contraction to the empty configuration is used.*

2.  *In the quadratic case, suppose the symbol has a nonzero witness, the alphabet is closed under the allowed contractions, and it contains the good twin operations. There are $r\ge0$ and $s_1\ge1$ such that for every $s\ge s_1$ of the permitted simple parity, the minimum split count of a unit with $s$ simple vertices is $r$. At these counts all edge bits are immaterial. A chosen minimum seed may be padded by arbitrarily many good twin pairs.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pw-minimum-seeds"><summary>Proof of stmt-pw-minimum-seeds</summary>

*Proof.* For the first assertion, a derivative in an edge of an $r$-vertex graph is a symbol on $r-1$ vertices. If $r\ge2$, this graph remains nonempty and has value zero by minimality. If $r=1$, there is no edge. The omitted-vertex description then leaves exactly the fixed-support labels.

For the second assertion let $r(s)$ be the minimum split count at simple count $s$, with value $+\infty$ when no witness exists. Once a finite value occurs, good-twin insertion gives $r(s+2)\le r(s)$. A nonincreasing sequence of nonnegative integers stabilizes. Choose $s_1\ge1$ in that range. A derivative of an edge with a split endpoint reduces the split count and preserves the simple count; it is therefore zero at the minimum. A simple–simple derivative is zero by the symbol rule. Twin insertion gives the last assertion. ◻

</details>

Call a list partitionable if some permitted contraction forest has a unit symbol as its output. A contraction certificate proves that its terminal polynomial is nonzero: the indicated iterated finite difference is nonzero.

<a id="stmt-pw-reserves"></a>
<div id="pw:reserves" class="lemma">

**Lemma 64** (Finite label reserves). *Fix a minimum seed and a finite label alphabet.*

1.  *In the all-split rational alphabet, a list with the same total label as the seed and sufficiently many copies of its labels is partitionable.*

<a id="pw:donor-balance"></a>
2.  *In the quadratic alphabet, retain $r$ split anchors with the seed labels, and suppose the list and seed have the same total rational label on the projection used by the symbol. Suppose there are sufficiently many simple vertices of the required parity and sufficiently many split reserves of each realizable rational shift. If $F\ne F'$, also suppose $$\label{pw:donor-balance}
     \Delta=\#\{p:\ p\text{ split in }F\text{ and inert in }F'\}
           -\#\{p:\ p\text{ inert in }F\text{ and split in }F'\}>C_0.$$ Here $C_0$ depends only on the anchors and the reserves. Then all other split vertices can be attached to simple vertices so that the result is the minimum seed and good twin pairs. The twins may match a prescribed finite enrichment of the rational labels. The base simple labels may be specified on any projection on which the required total agrees.*

*All reserve requirements depend only on the finite alphabets, seed, and projection. They do not depend on the original list length.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pw-reserves"><summary>Proof of stmt-pw-reserves</summary>

*Proof.* In the first case retain the first $r-1$ seed labels and merge all other vertices to the last cluster. Sufficient copies ensure that this cluster is nonempty. Its label is forced by the total, hence is the last seed label. Lemma <a href="#pw:minimum-seeds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pw:minimum-seeds">63</a> makes its unknown edges immaterial.

For the second case, the rational labels of split attachments form the subgroup with trivial $F$-coordinate. They act transitively on the simple coset, so every prescribed change of a simple label has a realizable split shift. Reserve sufficiently many representatives of every such shift. Remove these reserves and the anchors from the donor pool. If $F\ne F'$, <a href="#pw:donor-balance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pw:donor-balance">[pw:donor-balance]</a>, with $C_0$ exceeding the number removed, leaves a distinct donor for each bad simple vertex. Attach these donors; every simple vertex is now good. If $F=F'$, this operation was unnecessary.

Pair equal enriched good labels. By withholding a bounded number of pairs, leave between $s_1$ and $s_1+C_1$ unpaired vertices, with the correct parity; $C_1$ depends only on the number of enriched labels. Use reserved shifts to turn the excess unpaired vertices into good pairs and to set the first $s_1-1$ base positions to the desired seed labels. The seed labels themselves need not all be good. Attach every remaining nonanchor split vertex to the last base position. Its projected label is forced by the total, the anchors, the other base positions, and the twin pairs. It is the required last seed label. Each cluster contains one simple vertex, so every contraction is permitted. Only a bounded number of reserve shifts was used.

When incidences to a retained split anchor are needed only to remove twins, they can be prescribed after the contracted labels are fixed, by the independent terminal-edge realization. Fixed stationary vertices instead enter the finite label enrichment from the outset. These two uses do not increase the number of label types with the original list length. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pw-packets"></a>
<div id="pw:packets" class="lemma">

**Lemma 65** (Neutral packets). *For any of the finite reserve problems above with prescribed rational total zero, one can choose a finite packet of terminal labels with rational total zero such that every nonempty union of copies of the packet has the required supplies. In the quadratic case with $F\ne F'$, its donor surplus may be made arbitrarily large. When $F=F'$, every simple label is good and no donor surplus is required. Compatible fixed splitting conditions may be included whenever the needed individual labels and shifts remain realizable.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pw-packets"><summary>Proof of stmt-pw-packets</summary>

*Proof.* Choose ample copies of every required label, including anchors and shifts. In the quadratic case with $F\ne F'$, add enough donors to exceed all the losses from the other labels and the prescribed reserve margin. Duplicate every full label in the resulting list. Duplication gives total zero in every rational elementary coordinate and preserves any strict donor surplus. Choose the margins so that after reserving the finitely many anchors in any one packet, the union of any positive number of packets still meets the reserve requirements. All labels are realized by distinct fresh primes; duplication never means repeating an actual prime factor. ◻

</details>

<a id="pw:lower-section"></a>
#### The even estimate and the corank-zero case

<a id="stmt-pw-partitionable-even"></a>
<div id="pw:partitionable-even" class="proposition">

<a id="pw:partitionable-bound"></a>
**Proposition 66** (Partitionable even estimate). *Fix an even symbol furnished by Proposition <a href="#pw:interfaces" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pw:interfaces">61</a>. For every partitionable parameter $h$ on its filter with $s_2(E^{(h)})=0$, $$\label{pw:partitionable-bound}
 L_*(h)\ne0,\qquad v(L_*(h))-2w(h)-s(h)\le C.$$ The constant depends only on the symbol and fixed local data.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pw-partitionable-even"><summary>Proof of stmt-pw-partitionable-even</summary>

*Proof.* If the parameter has no prime factors, a contraction certificate means that the empty symbol itself is a unit. Equation <a href="#pw:even-unit-interface" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pw:even-unit-interface">[pw:even-unit-interface]</a> then proves the assertion directly. Otherwise the stationary terminal set is nonempty. Keep the prime factors of $h$ stationary. Their terminal polynomial is nonzero by its contraction certificate. The stationary one-symbol case of Theorem <a href="#gr:address" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:address">57</a> gives arbitrarily large binary cubes based at $h$, with only primary new primes, whose other addresses have unit symbol. Equation <a href="#pw:even-unit-interface" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pw:even-unit-interface">[pw:even-unit-interface]</a> and $s(h_x)\ge0$ give the uniform upper bound required by the cyclotomic transfer. The base corank is zero, so that transfer gives <a href="#pw:partitionable-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pw:partitionable-bound">[pw:partitionable-bound]</a>. A base-dependent dimension threshold does not affect the asserted constant. ◻

</details>

The original corank-zero base can now be treated without any odd coefficient system or companion field.

<a id="stmt-pw-even-base"></a>
<div id="pw:even-base" class="proposition">

**Proposition 67** (Even original base). *If $s_2(E)=0$, then $a(E)=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pw-even-base"><summary>Proof of stmt-pw-even-base</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#pr:invariance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pr:invariance">3</a> gives even functional sign. Fix an even symbol on the filter of $1$, including all auxiliary class-group tests. If its value at $1$ is a unit, analytic nonvanishing is already detected. Otherwise use a nonempty minimum seed in the full-torsion case, or a stabilized quadratic seed in the other case.

For each $b$, assign a neutral packet from Lemma <a href="#pw:packets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pw:packets">65</a> to every nonzero linear form on $\mathbb F_2^b$, and activate its primes by that form. At every nonzero address at least one packet is active; their union satisfies the reserve problem and has a nonzero terminal polynomial. At zero no terminal is active. The single-symbol case of Theorem <a href="#gr:address" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gr:address">57</a> adds primary auxiliaries and makes the symbol a unit at every nonzero address. All local classes remain those of $1$. Equation <a href="#pw:even-unit-interface" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pw:even-unit-interface">[pw:even-unit-interface]</a>, followed by cyclotomic transfer at the original corank-zero base, gives $a(E)=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pw:odd-lower-section"></a>
#### The lower bound needed by the odd coefficients

For the odd construction, first fix the original coefficient and class-group support and its unit tests. Then choose the genus partner $h_*$ as in Section <a href="#co:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:section">7</a>, adjoin its prime factors to the support, and refine the unit tests there. All variable $h$ avoid this support. The positive filter below is this prepared filter, fixed before $h$ varies or the odd coefficients are normalized.

We next use the even estimate at analytically nonvanishing parameters, not the corank-zero conclusion just proved. The companion $k'=k'(h)$ in this step may vary with $h$, but its number of prime factors will be bounded. It is distinct from both the detector’s fixed genus partner $h_*$ and the fixed companion used in the final ring-class transfer.

At a parameter already known to have analytic rank zero, the estimate <a href="#pw:partitionable-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pw:partitionable-bound">[pw:partitionable-bound]</a> transfers between either orientation of a fixed two-isogeny. The isogeny formula for the BSD quotient gives a bounded change in $s(h)+2w(h)$. At an active odd good prime the component-group valuation is $t_p$, and the two active contributions are included in the two weights. At the fixed support only finitely many local twisting classes occur. Differential, period, and torsion indices are bounded by the fixed isogeny and its reverse, and there is no regulator at rank zero.
